Measuring data types
Lukas Mulder 1, Paige Randall North 2, Maximilien Péroux 3

Abstract
In this article, we combine Sweedler’s classic theory of measuring coalgebras – by which k k -algebras are enriched in k k -coalgebras for k k a field – with the theory of W-types – by which the categorical semantics of inductive data types in functional programming languages are understood. In our main theorem, we find that under some hypotheses, algebras of an endofunctor are enriched in coalgebras of the same endofunctor, and we find polynomial endofunctors provide many interesting examples of this phenomenon. We then generalize the notion of initial algebra of an endofunctor using this enrichment, thus generalizing the notion of W-type. This article is an extended version of North and Péroux (2023b) : it adds expository introductions to the original theories of measuring coalgebras and W-types along with some improvements to the main theory and many explicitly worked examples.

tocauthor:
Lukas Mulder, Paige North, Maximilien Péroux

and Department of Mathematics, Michigan State University

1
Introduction

Many features of functional programming languages like Haskell and Agda are justified by their categorical semantics.
For instance, perhaps the central feature – inductive data types – is justified by the categorical theory of categorical W-types – initial algebras of polynomial endofunctors
Dybjer (1997)
;
Moerdijk and Palmgren (2000)
.
When programming in a functional programming language, most (data) types – such as the type of natural numbers, of booleans, of lists, of strings, of trees, etc. – are defined as inductive types.
Passing to the categorical semantics of a functional programming language, this way of defining types corresponds exactly to specifying an object of a category by asking that it is the initial algebra of a specified endofunctor.
Depending on the category, not all endofunctors have initial algebras of course.
Thus of central importance are
polynomial
endofunctors, which have initial algebras (called categorical W-types) in elementary toposes with natural numbers objects, in particular the category of sets.
These correspond to the inductive types that are guaranteed in languages such as Coq and Agda, and though in languages such as Haskell more inductive types are guaranteed, categorical W-types correspond to the inductive types that are usually used.

Such categorical semantics are important in general because they make programming languages susceptible to mathematical study.
And more specifically, the categorical semantics of inductive data types is important because it connects types – e.g., the type of natural numbers – living in an abstract programming language with ‘real’ mathematical objects – e.g., the ‘real’ natural numbers studied by mathematicians and living in the category of sets, thus justifying (or giving meaning to, hence the word
semantics
) programs written in such a language.

We seek in this line of work to further elucidate the mathematical structure in which categorical W-types are embedded – driven not only by motivations from pure mathematics, but also the fact that categorical phenomena that are at first adjacent to the semantics of programming languages often find their way into the programming languages themselves.

The mathematical structure surrounding categorical W-types that we study here is an analogue for our setting of Sweedler’s classic theory of measuring coalgebras
Sweedler (1969)
. In that theory,
k
-algebras are found to be enriched in
k
-coalgebras, where
k
is a field in the usual sense. This enrichment adds extra structure to the category of
k
-algebras. One can see this extra structure as giving a notion of
partial algebra homomorphism
. That is, while the hom-sets in the category of
k
-algebras consist of algebra homomorphisms, the hom-coalgebras in the
enriched
category of
k
-algebras consist not only of algebra homomorphisms but also include other morphisms which are ‘almost’ algebra homomorphisms. The hom-coalgebras also include information about ‘how close’ a morphism is to being a homomorphism, hence the word
measuring
. That is, if one thinks of the hom-objects of an (enriched) category as being the tools with which one studies the objects, this classic Sweedler theory gives us higher-precision tools with which to study the interactions between algebras.

Inspired by this, we give an analogue in our setting.

Theorem 1.1
(
Theorem

4.3
)

Let
\mathscr{C}
be a locally presentable, closed symmetric monoidal category.
Let
F\colon\mathscr{C}\rightarrow\mathscr{C}
be an accessible, lax symmetric monoidal functor.
Then the category of
F
-algebras in
\mathscr{C}
is enriched, tensored and cotensored over the closed symmetric monoidal category of
F
-coalgebras in
\mathscr{C}
.

We use this to give a notion of partial algebra homomorphism.
The theorem says that, given
F
-algebras
A
and
B
, in lieu of a set
\mathrm{Alg}(A,B)
of algebra homomorphism
A\rightarrow B
, there is a coalgebra
{\underline{\mathrm{Alg}}}(A,B)
of partial algebra homomorphisms.
Inspired by the generalization of (co)limits in ordinary categories to weighted (co)limits in enriched categories, we generalize the notion of initial algebra to
C
-initial algebra for any coalgebra
C
.
Thus, by considering polynomial endofunctors, we generalize the notion of categorical W-types (i.e., initial algebra of a polynomial endofunctor), and we give many such examples.
We emphasize that not all polynomial endofunctors can be considered, as the endofunctor must be lax monoidal. For instance, this prevents the theory to be applied to general list type, but only lists over a monoid, see
Sect.

5.5
below. Moreover, the requirement of “symmetry” for
\mathscr{C}
and
F
above is not strictly necessary, and is only there for convenience.

We also make sense of partial homomorphisms up to index
n
and construct a tower of coalgebras

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\underline{\mathrm{Alg}}}_{\infty}(A,B)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdots}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\underline{\mathrm{Alg}}}_{2}(A,B)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\underline{\mathrm{Alg}}}_{1}(A,B)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\underline{\mathrm{Alg}}}_{0}(A,B).}

Its limit is composed of the
\infty
-partial homomorphisms which are morphisms
A\rightarrow B
, built layer by layer in the towers, that approximates inductively a total algebra homomorphism.

This is not an isolated phenomenon: several other analogues in other settings have appeared since Sweedler’s version:
Anel and Joyal (2013)
;
Hyland et al. (2017)
;
Vasilakopoulou (2019)
;
Péroux (2022)
;
McDermott et al. (2022)
.
One of our motivations from pure mathematics is purely to understand this phenomenon better.
Indeed, our setting is the ‘simplest’ in the sense that our notion of (co)algebra does not require any coherence: that is, an algebra of an endofunctor is purely an object
X
with a morphism
FX\to X
; there are no units or multiplication to keep track of.
Thus, here, Sweedler’s phenomenon is visible in its purest form.

In future work, we hope to push both the categorical, pure mathematical story further as well as develop applications in programming languages.
In this article, we fix a category
\mathscr{C}
and an endofunctor
F
to obtain an enriched category
\mathrm{Alg}_{F}(\mathscr{C})
.
In future work, we hope to show that this construction is categorical, i.e., functorial in
\mathscr{C}
and
F
.
On the programming side, we hope to see how such W-types could be usefully implemented in a programming language like Haskell.

1.1
Outline and Contents

In
Sect.

2
, we review Sweedler’s classic story of
k
-algebras enriched in
k
-coalgebras, for
k
a field.
In
Sect.

3
, we review the basic theory of W-types, that is, of data types defined as initial algebras for an endofunctor.
In these first two sections, we aim to make this paper accessible to master’s students or advanced undergraduates with knowledge of category theory; in addition, we hope to bridge the gap between algebraists in the tradition of Sweedler and (functional) programming language theorists. Thus, while a familiarity with abstract algebra is useful for
Sect.

2
and a familiarity with functional programming is useful for
Sect.

3
, we hope that the basic theory is explained here clearly enough to get the ideas across to members of either camp.
Thus, in particular,
Sect.

2
and
Sect.

3
are expository.

In
Sect.

4
, we put the content of Sections
2
and
3
to find that an analogue of Sweedler’s story holds for endofunctors: under some hypotheses, algebras for endofunctors are enriched in coalgebras for the same endofunctor (
Theorem

4.3
).

Section

4
is an extended version of
North and Péroux (2023b)
. There are several differences between that section and
North and Péroux (2023b)
. First, all proofs have been included (these were already available in the preprint
North and Péroux (2023a)
). Second, while
North and Péroux (2023b)
presented an extended example before the categorical theory, here we have first given the categorical theory first with that example given as a running example. Third, we have included some small improvements. In particular,
Theorem

4.4
now generalizes
(
North and Péroux, 2023b
, Thm. 33)
.
We also clarify and generalize an inductive construction of partial homomorphisms in
Example

27
,
Example

30
and
Remark

9
that was originally found in
(
North and Péroux, 2023b
, Construction 1)
.

In
Sect.

5
, we give many worked-out examples of the theory presented in
Sect.

4
. Because we want this paper to be accessible to a wide range of readers, we provide many details for the calculations in these examples.

2
Sweedler Theory

In this section, we present the classical theory of algebras and coalgebras found in
Sweedler (1969)
, with a modern perspective that will allow us to extend the arguments in the next sections. Sweedler arguments was formalized in a more general context in
Fox (1976)
, and his approach was extended further in
Hyland et al. (2017)
.

2.1
Algebras

Let
k
be a field.
Let
Vect

k

be the category of
k
-vector spaces with
k
-linear transformations.
Recall that a
k
-algebra

A
is a vector space
A
together with a multiplication

\displaystyle A\times A

\displaystyle\longrightarrow A

\displaystyle(x,y)

\displaystyle\longmapsto xy

that is
k
-bilinear, meaning that it defines a
k
-linear homomorphism
\mu\colon A\otimes_{k}A\rightarrow A
, associative, meaning that for all
x,y,z\in A
we have

(xy)z=x(yz),

and unital, meaning there exists
1_{A}\in A
such that

x1_{A}=x=1_{A}x

for all
x\in A
. The existence of such element
1_{A}\in A
is recorded by a
k
-linear homomorphism
\eta\colon k\rightarrow A
. Associativity and unitality can now be rephrased to say that the following diagrams commute in
k
-vector spaces

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A\otimes_{k}A\otimes_{k}A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A\otimes_{k}A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A\otimes_{k}k}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces k\otimes_{k}A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A\otimes_{k}A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A\otimes_{k}A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A\otimes_{k}A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A.}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 1\otimes\mu}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mu\otimes 1}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mu}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cong}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 1\otimes\eta}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cong}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\eta\otimes 1}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mu}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mu}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mu}

Given two
k
-algebras
(A,\mu_{A},\eta_{A})
and
(B,\mu_{B},\eta_{B})
, an
algebra homomorphism

f\colon A\rightarrow B
is a
k
-linear homomorphism
f\colon A\rightarrow B
such that
f(xy)=f(x)f(y)
and
f(1_{A})=1_{B}
: i.e., the following diagrams commute in
k
-vector spaces

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A\otimes_{k}A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B\otimes_{k}B}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces k}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B.}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f\otimes f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mu_{A}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mu_{B}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\eta_{A}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\eta_{B}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}

Denote by
\mathrm{Alg}_{k}
the resulting category of
k
-algebras. We denote by
Alg

k

(A,B)
the set of algebra homomorphisms
A\to B
.
The initial
k
-algebra is
k
by unitality.

The
free
k
-algebra
on a vector space
V
is given by the
tensor algebra

\lx@glossaries@gls@link{main}{freeAlg}{{{}}T(V)}=\bigoplus_{n\geq 0}V^{\otimes n}

with multiplication given by concatenation: the identifications
V^{\otimes i}\otimes V^{\otimes j}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\cong}}{{\rightarrow}}V^{\otimes i+j}
assemble to give the multiplication
TV\otimes TV\rightarrow TV
.
The free algebra is the left adjoint with respect to the forgetful functor
U\colon\mathrm{Alg}_{k}\rightarrow\mathrm{Vect}_{k}
: i.e., we obtain the following adjunction

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{Vect}_{k}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{Alg}_{k}.}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\perp}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces U}

In particular, for a vector space
V
and a
k
-algebra
A
, we obtain the following natural bijection

\mathrm{Alg}_{k}(TV,A)\cong\mathrm{Vect}_{k}(V,UA).

Here are some non-examples of algebra homomorphisms that we should keep in mind. We will see that Sweedler’s theory
Sweedler (1969)
allows to consider these as examples of
partial
algebra homomorphisms.

Example 1

Consider the polynomial ring
\mathbb{R}[x]
which is an
\mathbb{R}
-algebra with the usual multiplication of polynomials.
Consider the derivation operator

\displaystyle D\colon\mathbb{R}[x]

\displaystyle\longrightarrow\mathbb{R}[x]

\displaystyle f(x)

\displaystyle\longmapsto f^{\prime}(x).

This is a
\mathbb{R}
-linear homomorphism but not an
\mathbb{R}
-algebra homomorphism as
D(fg)\neq D(f)D(g)
and
D(1)\neq 1
.

Example 2

Consider the multiplication by 2 on the rationals:
m_{2}\colon\mathbb{Q}\rightarrow\mathbb{Q}
sending
a\mapsto 2a
for all
a\in\mathbb{Q}
. It is a
\mathbb{Q}
-linear homomorphism but not an algebra homomorphism when
\mathbb{Q}
is viewed as a
\mathbb{Q}
-algebra with its usual multiplication, as
m_{2}(ab)=2ab\neq 4ab=m_{2}(a)m_{2}(b)
.

2.2
Coalgebras

We can dualize the notion of
k
-algebras as follows. A
k
-coalgebra

C
is a vector space
C
together with
k
-linear homomorphisms
\Delta\colon C\rightarrow C\otimes_{k}C
(called
comultiplication
) and
\varepsilon\colon C\rightarrow k
(called the
co-unit
) that is co-associative and co-unital in the sense that the following diagrams commute

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C\otimes_{k}C\otimes_{k}C}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C\otimes_{k}C}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C\otimes_{k}k}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces k\otimes_{k}C}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C\otimes_{k}C}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C\otimes_{k}C}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C\otimes_{k}C}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C.}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 1\otimes\Delta}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Delta\otimes 1}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Delta}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cong}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 1\otimes\varepsilon}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cong}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varepsilon\otimes 1}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Delta}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Delta}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Delta}

Compare the definition of
k
-coalgebra with that of
k
-algebra, and notice that we have simply reversed the direction of the arrows. We can express the commutativity of these diagrams nicely using
Sweedler notation
from
(
Sweedler, 1969
, §1.2)
.
For
c\in C
, we abbreviate the sum notation in the comultiplication as follows

\Delta(c)=\sum_{i}{c_{(1)}}_{i}\otimes{c_{(2)}}_{i}\eqqcolon\lx@glossaries@gls@link{main}{SweedlerNot}{{{}}\sum_{(c)}c_{(1)}\otimes c_{(2)}}.

The co-associativity then says that

\sum_{(c)}\left(\sum_{(c_{(1)})}{(c_{(1)})}_{(1)}\otimes{(c_{(1)})}_{(2)}\right)\otimes{c_{(2)}}=\sum_{(c)}c_{(1)}\otimes\left(\sum_{(c_{(2)})}{(c_{(2)})}_{(1)}\otimes{(c_{(2)})}_{(2)}\right),

and that element is often denoted
\sum_{(c)}c_{(1)}\otimes c_{(2)}\otimes c_{(3)}
.
Co-unitality says that

\sum_{(c)}\varepsilon(c_{(1)})c_{(2)}=c=\sum_{(c)}\varepsilon(c_{(2)})c_{(1)}.

Therefore, it is often useful to think of the comultiplication on
C
to be a (co-associative) decomposition of each element
c\in C
into parts
{c_{(1)}}_{i}
and
{c_{(2)}}_{i}
and to think of the co-unit as a projection such that when applied to one ‘side’ of the result of decomposition, returns
c
.

Example 3

The
binomial

k
-coalgebra structure on
k[x]
is given by the comultiplication

\displaystyle\Delta\colon k[x]

\displaystyle\longrightarrow k[x]\otimes_{k}k[x]

\displaystyle x^{n}

\displaystyle\longmapsto\sum_{i}\binom{n}{i}x^{n-i}\otimes x^{i}

and co-unit

\displaystyle\varepsilon\colon k[x]

\displaystyle\longrightarrow k

\displaystyle x^{n}

\displaystyle\longmapsto 0.

Given two
k
-coalgebras
(C,\Delta_{C},\varepsilon_{C})
and
(D,\Delta_{D},\varepsilon_{D})
, a
coalgebra homomorphism

f\colon C\rightarrow D
is a
k
-linear homomorphism
f\colon C\rightarrow D
such that

\sum_{(c)}f(c_{(1)})\otimes f(c_{(2)})=\sum_{(f(c))}f(c)_{(1)}\otimes f(c)_{(2)}

and

\varepsilon_{D}(f(c))=\varepsilon_{C}(c)

for all
c\in C
.
In other words, the following diagrams commute:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C\otimes_{k}C}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces D\otimes_{k}D}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces k}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces D}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces D.}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f\otimes f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Delta_{C}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Delta_{D}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varepsilon_{C}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varepsilon_{D}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}

Denote by
\mathrm{coAlg}_{k}
the resulting category of
k
-coalgebras. Denote the set of coalgebra homomorphisms
C\to D
by
coAlg

k

(A,B)
.
The terminal
k
-coalgebra is
k
by co-unitality.

Although more subtle than its algebra analogue, there is a notion of
cofree coalgebra
on a vector space
V
.
A candidate for this cofree coalgebra could be the direct product
\prod_{n\geq 0}V^{\otimes n}
coming from “de-concatenation”
V^{\otimes(i+j)}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\cong}}{{\rightarrow}}V^{\otimes i}\otimes_{k}V^{\otimes j}
.
However, because direct products do not commute with tensor products, the de-concatenation only leads to an injective
k
-linear homomorphism

\Delta\colon\prod_{n\geq 0}V^{\otimes n}\hookrightarrow\prod_{i,j\geq 0}V^{\otimes i}\otimes V^{\otimes j}.

The cofree
k
-coalgebra
T^{\vee}(V)
is defined to be the largest vector subspace of
\prod_{n\geq 0}V^{\otimes n}
such that

\Delta(T^{\vee}(V))\subseteq T^{\vee}(V)\otimes_{k}T^{\vee}(V).

In fact, by
Block and Leroux (1985)
;
Hazewinkel (2003)
, this can be defined as the following pullback (i.e. intersection) in vector spaces

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T^{\vee}(V)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\left(\prod_{i\geq 0}V^{\otimes i}\right)\otimes_{k}\left(\prod_{j\geq 0}V^{\otimes j}\right)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\prod_{n\geq 0}V^{\otimes n}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\prod_{i,j\geq 0}V^{\otimes i}\otimes_{k}V^{\otimes j}.}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lrcorner}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Delta}

The cofree coalgebra is right adjoint to the forgetful functor
U\colon\mathrm{coAlg}_{k}\rightarrow\mathrm{Vect}_{k}
: i.e. we obtain an adjunction

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{coAlg}_{k}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{Vect}_{k}.}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces U}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\perp}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T^{\vee}}

In particular, for a vector space
V
and a
k
-coalgebra
C
, we obtain a natural bijection

\mathrm{coAlg}_{k}(C,T^{\vee}(V))\cong\mathrm{Vect}_{k}(U(C),V).

(2.2.1)

2.3
The Convolution Algebra

Denote by
[V,W]=\hom_{k}(V,W)
the
k
-vector space of linear homomorphisms
V\rightarrow W
.
Suppose that
(C,\Delta,\varepsilon)
is a
k
-coalgebra and that
(B,\mu,\eta)
is a
k
-algebra.
Then
[C,B]
is also a
k
-algebra with the following convolution product

\displaystyle[C,B]\otimes_{k}[C,B]

\displaystyle\longrightarrow[C,B]

\displaystyle f\otimes g

\displaystyle\longmapsto\lx@glossaries@gls@link{main}{ConvProd}{{{}}f\ast g}.

Here,
f\ast g\colon C\rightarrow B
is the composite

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C\otimes_{k}C}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B\otimes_{k}B}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B.}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Delta}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f\otimes g}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mu}

In Sweedler notation:

(f\ast g)(c)=\sum_{(c)}f(c_{(1)})g(c_{(2)}).

The unit
k\rightarrow[C,B]
corresponds to
1_{[C,B]}\colon C\rightarrow B
defined as the composite

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces k}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B,}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varepsilon}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\eta}

i.e.
1_{[C,B]}(c)=\varepsilon(c)1_{B}
.

Definition 1
(convolution algebra)

Given a
k
-coalgebra
C
and a
k
-algebra
B
, we call
([C,B],\ast,1_{[C,B]})
the
convolution algebra
of
C
with
B
.
The construction is natural in
C
and
B
and defines a functor

[-,-]\colon\mathrm{coAlg}_{k}^{\mathrm{op}}\times\mathrm{Alg}_{k}\longrightarrow\mathrm{Alg}_{k}.

Example 4

If we let
C=k
, the terminal
k
-coalgebra, then the convolution algebra
[k,B]
is isomorphic to
B
as a
k
-algebra.

2.4
Partial Algebra Homomorphism

Recall that given
k
-vector spaces
V,W,Z
, we have the
k
-linear exponential correspondence

\mathrm{Vect}_{k}\left(V\otimes_{k}W,Z\right)\cong\mathrm{Vect}_{k}(V,[W,Z])\cong\mathrm{Vect}_{k}(W,[V,Z])

for which a map
f\colon V\otimes_{k}W\rightarrow Z

corresponds to
\widetilde{f}\colon V\rightarrow[W,Z]
(where
\widetilde{f}(v)(w)=f(v\otimes w)
) which corresponds to
\widehat{f}\colon W\rightarrow[V,Z]
(where
\widehat{f}(w)(v)=f(v\otimes w)
).
It is customary to denote
\widetilde{f}(v)\colon W\rightarrow Z
as
f_{v}
.

Let us now consider
k
-algebras
(A,\mu_{A},1_{A})
and
(B,\mu_{B},1_{B})
and a
k
-coalgebra
(C,\Delta,\varepsilon)
. Then by our discussion above, we know that a
k
-linear homomorphism
f\colon C\otimes_{k}A\rightarrow B
corresponds to
k
-linear homomorphisms
\widetilde{f}\colon C\rightarrow[A,B]
and
\widehat{f}\colon A\rightarrow[C,B]
.
Since
[C,B]
can be given the structure of a
k
-algebra via the convolution product, then we can consider maps
\widehat{f}\colon A\rightarrow[C,B]
that are algebra homomorphisms, i.e., for all
x,y\in A
:

\widehat{f}(xy)=\widehat{f}(x)\ast\widehat{f}(y),\quad\quad\quad\widehat{f}(1_{A})=1_{[C,B]}.

Definition 2
(partial homomorphism)

Given
k
-algebras
A
and
B
and a
k
-coalgebra
C
, a
k
-linear homomorphism
\widetilde{f}\colon C\rightarrow[A,B]
is called a
C
-indexed partial homomorphism from
A
to
B
if its adjunct
\widehat{f}\colon A\rightarrow[C,B]
is an algebra homomorphism.
Denoting
\lx@glossaries@gls@link{main}{partialhm}{{{}}f_{c}}\coloneqq\widetilde{f}(c)

for all
c\in C
, this means that for all
x,y\in A
we have

f_{c}(xy)=\sum_{(c)}f_{c_{(1)}}(x)f_{c_{(2)}}(y)\quad\quad\quad f_{c}(1_{A})=\varepsilon(c)1_{B}.

In other words, we obtain the following commutative diagrams in vector spaces

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C\otimes_{k}C}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{[A,B]\otimes_{k}[A,B]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{[A\otimes_{k}A,B\otimes_{k}B]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{[A,B]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{[A\otimes_{k}A,B]}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Delta}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widetilde{f}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widetilde{f}\otimes\widetilde{f}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nabla}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mu_{B}}_{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mu_{A}^{*}}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{[A,B]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces k}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{[k,k]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{[A,k]}.}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widetilde{f}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varepsilon}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\eta_{B}}_{*}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cong}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\eta_{A}^{*}}

Here,
\nabla
is the transformation

\displaystyle\nabla\colon[V_{1},V_{2}]\otimes_{k}[V_{3},V_{4}]

\displaystyle\longrightarrow[V_{1}\otimes_{k}V_{3},V_{2}\otimes_{k}V_{4}]

\displaystyle f\otimes g

\displaystyle\longmapsto f\otimes g

natural in vector spaces
V_{1}
,
V_{2}
,
V_{3}
and
V_{4}
.

Remark 1

A
C
-indexed partial homomorphism is
not
a partial function. It is a function that should be regarded as only partially multiplicative. Indeed, a homomorphism is multiplicative function, therefore, the term partial homomorphism should not be confused with the notion of partial function.

Example 5

Choose
C=\mathbb{R}[x]
and
A,B=\mathbb{R}[x]
as in
Example

3
. We can view the derivation
D\colon\mathbb{R}[x]\rightarrow\mathbb{R}[x]
as an
\mathbb{R}[x]
-indexed partial homomorphism

\displaystyle\mathbb{R}[x]

\displaystyle\longrightarrow[\mathbb{R}[x],\mathbb{R}[x]]

\displaystyle x^{n}

\displaystyle\longmapsto D^{n}.

We have
\Delta(x)=x\otimes 1+1\otimes x
, and so we recover the formula

D(fg)=D(f)g+fD(g),

and more generally

D^{n}(fg)=\sum_{i=0}^{n}\binom{n}{i}D^{n-i}(f)D^{i}(g).

Example 6

We can view
\mathbb{Q}
as a coalgebra over itself via
\mathbb{Q}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\cong}}{{\rightarrow}}\mathbb{Q}\otimes_{\mathbb{Q}}\mathbb{Q}
defined by
q\mapsto q\otimes 1
.
We have a
\mathbb{Q}
-indexed partial homomorphism

\displaystyle\mathbb{Q}

\displaystyle\longrightarrow[\mathbb{Q},\mathbb{Q}]

\displaystyle q

\displaystyle\longmapsto m_{q}

where
m_{q}(a)=qa
.
The partial homomorphism says that

m_{q}(ab)=q(ab)=(qa)b=m_{q}(a)m_{1}(b).

Example 7

Building on the previous example, given any
k
-algebra homomorphism
f\colon A\rightarrow B
, choose the trivial
k
-coalgebra structure on
k
via
\Delta\colon k\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\cong}}{{\rightarrow}}k\otimes_{k}k
, and define a
k
-partial homomorphism

\displaystyle k

\displaystyle\longrightarrow[A,B]

\displaystyle q

\displaystyle\longmapsto f_{q}

where
f_{q}(x)=qf(x)
for all
x\in A
and
q\in k
.
As
\Delta(1)=1\otimes 1
, we get:

f(xy)=f_{1}(xy)=f_{1}(x)f_{1}(y)=f(x)f(y),

recovering the fact that
f
is an algebra homomorphism.
We obtain in fact a correspondence between algebra homomorphisms
A\rightarrow B
and
k
-partial homomorphisms
k\rightarrow[A,B]
.

2.5
Measuring

By the adjunction
\mathrm{Vect}_{k}(C\otimes_{k}A,B)\cong\mathrm{Vect}_{k}(C,[A,B])
, we can rephrase the notion of partial homomorphisms as follows.

Definition 3
(measuring)

Given
k
-algebras
A
and
B
and a
k
-coalgebra
C
, a
k
-linear homomorphism
f\colon C\otimes_{k}A\rightarrow B
is called

a
C
-measuring of
A
and
B
if its adjunct
\widetilde{f}\colon C\rightarrow[A,B]
is a
C
-indexed partial homomorphism, or equivalently, the other adjoint
\widehat{f}\colon A\rightarrow[C,B]
is an algebra homomorphism.
This means that for all
c\in C
and
x,y\in A
, we have

f(c\otimes xy)=\sum_{(c)}f(c_{(1)}\otimes x)f(c_{(2)}\otimes y),\quad\quad f(c\otimes 1_{A})=\varepsilon(c)1_{B}.

In other words, we have the following commutative diagrams in vector spaces:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C\otimes_{k}A^{\otimes 2}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C^{\otimes 2}\otimes_{k}A^{\otimes 2}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(C\otimes_{k}A)^{\otimes 2}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B\otimes_{k}B}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C\otimes_{k}A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 1\otimes\mu_{A}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Delta\otimes 1}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cong}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f\otimes f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mu_{B}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C\otimes_{k}k}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces k}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C\otimes_{k}A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B.}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 1\otimes\eta_{A}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cong}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varepsilon}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\eta_{B}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}

We can summarize the equivalent definitions as in the table below.

C\otimes_{k}A\rightarrow B

C\rightarrow[A,B]

A\rightarrow[C,B]

C
-

C
-indexed partial

algebra

measuring

homomorphism

homomorphism

These notions can be defined in any closed symmetric monoidal category.
If not closed, we cannot make sense of internal hom, and thus the notion of
C
-indexed partial homomorphism
C\rightarrow[A,B]
is not well defined, nor a convolution algebra
[C,B]
. However, we can still make sense of
C
-measurings
C\otimes_{k}A\rightarrow B
in the non-closed case, which may explain why this term is more present in the literature.

Definition 4
(functoriality of measurings)

Let
A
,
B
be
k
-algebras and
C
a
k
-coalgebra.
Let
\mu_{C}(A,B)
be the set of
C
-measurings
C\otimes_{k}A\rightarrow B
of
A
to
B
, or equivalently, the set of
C
-indexed partial homomorphisms
C\rightarrow[A,B]
.
Given a measuring
f\colon C\otimes_{k}A\rightarrow B
, a coalgebra homomorphism
c\colon C^{\prime}\rightarrow C
, and algebra homomorphisms
a\colon A^{\prime}\rightarrow A
and
b\colon B\rightarrow B^{\prime}
, we obtain a new
C
-measuring
f^{\prime}\colon C^{\prime}\otimes_{k}A^{\prime}\rightarrow B^{\prime}
as the composite

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C^{\prime}\otimes_{k}A^{\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C\otimes_{k}A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B^{\prime}.}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces c\otimes a}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces b}

By doing this for any such
f
, we obtain a function
\mu_{C}(A,B)\rightarrow\mu_{C^{\prime}}(A^{\prime},B^{\prime})
.
This makes
\mu
into a functor

\displaystyle\mu\colon\mathrm{coAlg}_{k}^{\mathrm{op}}\times\mathrm{Alg}_{k}^{\mathrm{op}}\times\mathrm{Alg}

\displaystyle\longrightarrow\mathrm{Set}

\displaystyle(C,A,B)

\displaystyle\longmapsto\mu_{C}(A,B).

As a
C
-measuring
f\colon C\otimes_{k}A\rightarrow B
(equivalently,
C
-indexed partial homomorphism
C\rightarrow[A,B]
) is equivalent to an algebra homomorphism
\widehat{f}\colon A\rightarrow[C,B]
, we get an identification

\mu_{C}(A,B)\cong\mathrm{Alg}_{k}(A,[C,B]),

showing that
\mu_{C}(-,B)\colon\mathrm{Alg}_{k}^{\mathrm{op}}\rightarrow\mathrm{Set}
is represented by the convolution algebra
[C,B]
.
We will show how
\mu\colon\mathrm{coAlg}_{k}^{\mathrm{op}}\times\mathrm{Alg}_{k}^{\mathrm{op}}\times\mathrm{Alg}\longrightarrow\mathrm{Set}
is represented in its other variables.

2.6
Composing Partial Homomorphisms

Given
k
-algebras
A_{1}
,
A_{2}
and
A_{3}
, we can compose algebra homomorphisms
f\colon A_{1}\rightarrow A_{2}
and
g\colon A_{2}\rightarrow A_{3}
into an algebra homomorphism
g\circ f\colon A_{1}\rightarrow A_{3}
. We wish to do the same for partial homomorphisms.

Let
(C,\Delta_{C},\varepsilon_{C})
and
(D,\Delta_{D},\varepsilon_{D})
be
k
-coalgebras.
Given partial homomorphisms
f\colon C\rightarrow[A_{1},A_{2}]
and
g\colon D\rightarrow[A_{2},A_{3}]
, we can define a new partial homomorphism
g\circ f\colon D\otimes_{k}C\rightarrow[A_{1},A_{3}]
by

(g\circ f)_{d\otimes c}\coloneqq g_{d}\circ f_{c}.

First, notice that
D\otimes_{k}C
remains a coalgebra with comultiplication given by

\displaystyle D\otimes_{k}C

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\Delta_{D}\otimes\Delta_{C}}}{{\longrightarrow}}(D\otimes_{k}D)\otimes_{k}(C\otimes_{k}C)\cong(D\otimes_{k}C)\otimes_{k}(D\otimes_{k}C)

\displaystyle d\otimes c

\displaystyle\longmapsto\sum_{(d)}\sum_{(c)}(d_{(1)}\otimes c_{(1)})\otimes(d_{(2)}\otimes c_{(2)})

and counit given by

D\otimes_{k}C\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\varepsilon_{D}\otimes\varepsilon_{C}}}{{\longrightarrow}}k.

We can check that
g\circ f
remains a
(D\otimes_{k}C)
-partial homomorphisms. Indeed for all
x,y\in A_{1}
,
c\in C
and
d\in D
, we obtain

\displaystyle(g\circ f)_{d\otimes c}(xy)

\displaystyle=g_{d}(f_{c}(xy))

\displaystyle=g_{d}\left(\sum_{(c)}f_{c_{(1)}}(x)f_{c_{(2)}}(y)\right)

\displaystyle=\sum_{(c)}g_{d}\left(f_{c_{(1)}}(x)f_{c_{(2)}}(y)\right)

\displaystyle=\sum_{(c)}\sum_{(d)}g_{d_{(1)}}(f_{c_{(1)}}(x))g_{d_{(2)}}(f_{c_{(2)}}(y))

\displaystyle=\sum_{(d)}\sum_{(c)}(g\circ f)_{d_{(1)}\otimes c_{(1)}}(x)(g\circ f)_{d_{(2)}\otimes c_{(2)}}(y).

Similarly, we have

\displaystyle(g\circ f)_{d\otimes c}(1_{A_{1}})

\displaystyle=g_{d}(f_{c}(1_{A_{1}}))

\displaystyle=g_{d}(\varepsilon_{C}(c)1_{A_{2}})

\displaystyle=\varepsilon_{D}(d)\varepsilon_{C}(c)1_{A_{3}}.

Therefore we have defined a composition of partial homomorphisms

\displaystyle\begin{split}\mu_{D}(A_{2},A_{3})\times\mu_{C}(A_{1},A_{2})&\longrightarrow\mu_{D\otimes_{k}C}(A_{1},A_{3})\\
(g,f)&\longmapsto g\circ f.\end{split}

(2.6.1)

The usual identity homomorphism
\mathrm{id}_{A}\colon A\rightarrow A
defines a
k
-indexed partial homomorphism
\mathrm{id}_{A}\colon k\rightarrow[A,A]
, i.e. an element of
\mu_{k}(A,A)
. Given any partial homomorphism
f\colon C\rightarrow[A,B]
, we can check that
f\circ\mathrm{id}_{A}=f=\mathrm{id}_{B}\circ f
.

We are getting closer to being able to construct a new category of
k
-algebras, where instead of algebra homomorphisms we have partial homomorphisms.
An issue that arises is that there is no control on the coalgebra being indexed on. That is, for what coalgebra
C
should we set
\hom(A,B)
to
\mu_{C}(A,B)
?

2.7
The Measuring Coalgebra

Fix
k
-algebras
A
and
B
.
Define
\mathscr{M}(A,B)

to be the category of measurings from
A
and to
B
. Each object consists of a pair
(C,\widetilde{f})
where
C
is a
k
-coalgebra and
\widetilde{f}\colon C\rightarrow[A,B]
is a
C
-indexed partial homomorphism (equivalently a measuring
f\colon C\otimes_{k}A\rightarrow B
).
A morphism
(C,\widetilde{f})\rightarrow(D,\widetilde{g})
consists of a coalgebra homomorphism
\gamma\colon C\rightarrow D
such that the following commutes

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces D}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{[A,B].}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\gamma}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widetilde{f}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widetilde{g}}

Composition and identity on
\mathscr{M}(A,B)
are induced by
\mathrm{coAlg}_{k}
.

Definition 5
(universal measuring)

Given
k
-algebras
A
and
B
, denote by
(\lx@glossaries@gls@link{main}{UnivMeasuring}{{{}}\underline{\mathrm{Alg}}\lx@text@underline{\mathrm{Alg}}_{k}(A,B)},\widetilde{\mathrm{ev}})
the terminal object (if it exists) of the category
\mathscr{M}(A,B)
of measurings from
A
to
B
. The coalgebra
\underline{\mathrm{Alg}}_{k}(A,B)
is called the
universal measuring coalgebra

from
A
to
B
and its measuring
\mathrm{ev}\colon\underline{\mathrm{Alg}}_{k}(A,B)\otimes_{k}A\rightarrow B
is called the
evaluation
.
This means given an
C
-indexed partial homomorphisms
\widetilde{f}\colon C\rightarrow[A,B]
, there exists a unique coalgebra homomorphism
u_{f}\colon C\rightarrow\underline{\mathrm{Alg}}_{k}(A,B)
such that the following diagram commutes:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{[A,B]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\underline{\mathrm{Alg}}_{k}(A,B).}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widetilde{f}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\exists!u_{f}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widetilde{\mathrm{ev}}}

The construction is natural in
A
and
B
and defines a functor

\underline{\mathrm{Alg}}_{k}(-,-)\colon\mathrm{Alg}_{k}^{\mathrm{op}}\times\mathrm{Alg}_{k}\longrightarrow\mathrm{coAlg}_{k}.

It is useful to think of
\underline{\mathrm{Alg}}_{k}(A,B)
as the coalgebra containing all possible partial homomorphisms from
A
to
B
.
In order to construct
\underline{\mathrm{Alg}}_{k}(A,B)
, we rephrase the data of a
C
-indexed partial homomorphism
\widetilde{f}\colon C\rightarrow[A,B]
entirely coalgebraically.
By the forgetful-cofree adjunction (
Eq.

2.2.1
), a
k
-linear homomorphism
\widetilde{f}\colon C\rightarrow[A,B]
corresponds to a coalgebra homomorphism
\overline{f}\colon C\rightarrow T^{\vee}([A,B])
.
Following the commutative diagrams of
Definition

2
, the map
\widetilde{f}
is a partial homomorphism if and only if the map
\overline{f}
fits into the following equalizing diagram in
\mathrm{coAlg}_{k}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T^{\vee}([A,B])}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T^{\vee}\left([A^{\otimes 2},B]\right)\oplus T^{\vee}([A,k]).}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overline{f}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Phi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Psi}

The morphism
\Phi
is the cofree map induced by summing the
k
-linear homomorphisms
\mu_{A}^{*}\colon[A,B]\rightarrow[A^{\otimes 2},B]
and
{\eta_{B}}_{*}\colon[A,B]\rightarrow[A,k]
.
The morphism
\Psi
is induced from using the forgetful-cofree adjunction on a
k
-linear homomorphism
T^{\vee}([A,B])\rightarrow[A^{\otimes 2},B]\oplus[A,k]
that we now define.
On the first summand, it is defined as the following composite

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T^{\vee}([A,B])}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T^{\vee}([A,B])^{\otimes 2}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{[A,B]}^{\otimes 2}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{[A^{\otimes 2},B^{\otimes 2}]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{[A^{\otimes 2},B]}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Delta}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nabla}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mu_{B}}_{*}}

where the unlabeled arrow is induced by
T^{\vee}([A,B])\rightarrow[A,B]
, the counit of the forgetful-cofree adjunction (we have omitted
U
for clarity).
On the second summand, it is defined as the composite

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T^{\vee}([A,B])}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces k\cong{[k,k]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{[A,k]},}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\eta_{A}^{*}}

where
T^{\vee}([A,B])\rightarrow k
is the counit of
T^{\vee}([A,B])
.

With this coalgebraic perspective, the category
\mathscr{M}(A,B)
can now be reformulated as the category of cones over the following parallel morphisms in
\mathrm{coAlg}_{k}
that we have just described

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T^{\vee}([A,B])}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T^{\vee}\left([A^{\otimes 2},B]\right)\oplus T^{\vee}([A,k]).}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Phi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Psi}

Therefore, the measuring coalgebra is the terminal object of the category of cones, i.e. the limit. We have just proved the following theorem.

Theorem 2.1
(
(
Sweedler, 1969
, 7.0.4)
)

Given
k
-algebras
A
and
B
, the measuring coalgebra
(\underline{\mathrm{Alg}}_{k}(A,B),\widetilde{\mathrm{ev}})
exists and is determined by the following equalizer in
\mathrm{coAlg}_{k}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\underline{\mathrm{Alg}}_{k}(A,B)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T^{\vee}([A,B])}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T^{\vee}\left([A^{\otimes 2},B]\right)\oplus T^{\vee}([A,k]).}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overline{\mathrm{ev}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Phi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Psi}

The existence of the universal measuring means that we obtain the natural bijection for all coalgebras
C
and algebras
A
and
B

\mu_{C}(A,B)\cong\mathrm{coAlg}_{k}(C,\underline{\mathrm{Alg}}_{k}(A,B))

showing that
\mu_{-}(A,B)\colon\mathrm{coAlg}_{k}^{\mathrm{op}}\rightarrow\mathrm{Set}
is represented by the universal measuring
\underline{\mathrm{Alg}}_{k}(A,B)
.

Moreover, we can now rephrase the composition of partial homomorphisms from
Eq.

2.6.1
.
Given
k
-algebras
A_{1}
,
A_{2}
,
A_{3}
and
k
-coalgebras
C
and
D
, our previously defined composition of partial homomorphisms produces a function

\displaystyle\mathrm{coAlg}_{k}(D,\underline{\mathrm{Alg}}_{k}(A_{2},A_{3}))

\displaystyle\times\mathrm{coAlg}_{k}(C,\underline{\mathrm{Alg}}_{k}(A_{1},A_{2}))

\displaystyle\rightarrow\mathrm{coAlg}_{k}(D\otimes_{k}C,\underline{\mathrm{Alg}}_{k}(A_{1},A_{3})).

Choose
C=\underline{\mathrm{Alg}}_{k}(A_{1},A_{2})
and
D=\underline{\mathrm{Alg}}_{k}(A_{2},A_{3})
, and then plug
\mathrm{id}_{\underline{\mathrm{Alg}}_{k}(A_{2},A_{3})}
and
\mathrm{id}_{\underline{\mathrm{Alg}}_{k}(A_{1},A_{2})}
into this function to obtain a morphism of coalgebras

\circ\colon\underline{\mathrm{Alg}}_{k}(A_{2},A_{3})\otimes_{k}\underline{\mathrm{Alg}}_{k}(A_{1},A_{2})\longrightarrow\underline{\mathrm{Alg}}_{k}(A_{1},A_{3}).

It is universal in the sense that given any partial homomorphisms
f\colon C\rightarrow[A_{1},A_{2}]
and
g\colon D\rightarrow[A_{2},A_{3}]
, the universal map
u_{g\circ f}
(as in
Definition

5
) associated to the composition
g\circ f
fits into the commutative diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces D\otimes_{k}C}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\underline{\mathrm{Alg}}_{k}(A_{2},A_{3})\otimes_{k}\underline{\mathrm{Alg}}_{k}(A_{1},A_{2})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\underline{\mathrm{Alg}}_{k}(A_{1},A_{3}).}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces u_{g\circ f}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces u_{g}\otimes u_{f}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\circ}

Moreover, the identity
\mathrm{id}_{A}
on a algebra
A
is an element of

\mathrm{Alg}_{k}(A,A)\cong\mu_{k}(A,A)\cong\mathrm{coAlg}_{k}(k,\underline{\mathrm{Alg}}_{k}(A,A)),

and thus can be viewed as a coalgebra homomorphism
\mathrm{id}_{A}\colon k\rightarrow\underline{\mathrm{Alg}}_{k}(A,A)
.
The universal composition and identities defined above assemble into a compatible enriched structure on
\mathrm{Alg}_{k}
(see
Theorem

2.3
). Instead of a set of algebra homomorphisms, we obtain a coalgebra of all possible partial algebra homomorphisms.

Example 8

Given a
k
-coalgebra
(C,\Delta,\varepsilon)
, a grouplike element in
C
is an element
g\in C
such that
\Delta(g)=g\otimes g
and
\varepsilon(g)=1
.
The set of grouplike elements in
C
is in bijective correspondence with coalgebra homomorphisms
k\rightarrow C
.
Applying this to a universal measuring
C=\underline{\mathrm{Alg}}_{k}(A,B)
we obtain

\mathrm{coAlg}_{k}(k,\underline{\mathrm{Alg}}_{k}(A,B))\cong\mathrm{Alg}_{k}(A,[k,B])\cong\mathrm{Alg}_{k}(A,B).

Therefore, the set of grouplike elements of
\underline{\mathrm{Alg}}_{k}(A,B)
is precisely the hom-set of algebra homomorphisms
A\rightarrow B
.

Example 9

Given a
k
-coalgebra
(C,\Delta,\varepsilon)
and a grouplike element
g\in C
, a
primitive element over
g
of
C
is an element
d\in C
such that
\Delta(d)=d\otimes g+g\otimes d
.
Given a
C
-partial homomorphism
\widetilde{f}\colon C\rightarrow[A,B]
, we obtain for all
x,y\in A

f_{d}(xy)=f_{d}(x)f_{g}(y)+f_{g}(x)f_{d}(y).

Here,
f_{g}\colon A\to B
is an algebra homomorphism by the previous example, and thus
f_{d}\colon A\rightarrow B
is a derivation from
A
to
B
. Therefore, the set of primitive elements of
\underline{\mathrm{Alg}}_{k}(A,B)
is the set of derivations
A\rightarrow B
.

Example 10

Given a vector space
V
and an algebra
B
, we obtain an isomorphism of coalgebras:

\underline{\mathrm{Alg}}_{k}(T(V),B)\cong T^{\vee}([V,B]).

This is due to the fact that for any coalgebra
C
we have the series of invertible linear maps

\displaystyle\mathrm{coAlg}_{k}(C,\underline{\mathrm{Alg}}_{k}(T(V),B))

\displaystyle\cong\mathrm{Alg}_{k}(T(V),[C,B])

\displaystyle\cong\mathrm{Vect}_{k}(V,[C,B])

\displaystyle\cong\mathrm{Vect}_{k}(C,[V,B])

\displaystyle\cong\mathrm{coAlg}_{k}(C,T^{\vee}([V,B])).

Example 11

Given a
k
-coalgebra
C
and a
k
-algebra
A
, we can view
k
as both a
k
-algebra and
k
-coalgebra, and we obtain a bijection

\mathrm{coAlg}_{k}(C,\underline{\mathrm{Alg}}_{k}(A,k))\cong\mathrm{Alg}_{k}(A,[C,k]).

The
k
-algebra
C^{*}\coloneqq[C,k]
is called the
dual algebra of
C
, and the
k
-coalgebra
A^{\circ}\coloneqq\underline{\mathrm{Alg}}_{k}(A,k)
is called the
finite dual
or
Sweedler dual of
A
.
We can check that in vector spaces, we have
A^{\circ}\cong A^{*}
if and only if
A
is finite dimensional.
We obtain natural identifications for all
C\in\mathrm{coAlg}_{k}
and
A\in\mathrm{Alg}_{k}
:

{\mathrm{Alg}}_{k}(A,C^{*})\cong{\mathrm{coAlg}}_{k}(C,A^{\circ}).

2.8
The Measuring Tensor

While building the measuring coalgebra
\underline{\mathrm{Alg}}_{k}(A,B)
, we re-framed the definition of a
C
-indexed partial homomorphism
\widetilde{f}\colon C\rightarrow[A,B]
as a certain coalgebra homomorphism
\overline{f}\colon C\rightarrow T^{\vee}([A,B])
using the cofree coalgebra.
We can provide a dual framework using the free algebra.
We first make the following consideration and provide a dual formulation of the category
\mathscr{M}(A,B)
of measurings.

Definition 6

Given a
k
-coalgebra
C
and
k
-algebra
A
, define
\mathscr{M}_{C}(A)
to be the category whose objects consist of pairs
(B,f)
where
B
is a
k
-algebra and
f\colon C\otimes_{k}A\rightarrow B
is a
C
-measuring.
A morphism
(B_{1},f_{1})\rightarrow(B_{2},f_{2})
in
\mathscr{M}_{C}(A)
consists of an algebra homomorphism
\beta\colon B_{1}\rightarrow B_{2}
such that the following diagram commutes.

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C\otimes_{k}A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B_{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\beta}

Composition and identity on
\mathscr{M}_{C}(A)
are induced from
\mathrm{Alg}_{k}
.

Definition 7
(measuring tensor)

Given a
k
-coalgebra
C
and
k
-algebra
A
, denote by
(C\triangleright A,u)
the initial object of the category
\mathscr{M}_{C}(A)
.
The
k
-algebra
C⊳A
is called
the measuring tensor
of
C
with
A
, and its measuring
u\colon C\otimes_{k}A\rightarrow C\triangleright A
is called the universal measuring of
C
with
A
.
This means given any
C
-measuring
f\colon C\otimes_{k}A\rightarrow B
, there exists a unique algebra homomorphism
C\triangleright A\rightarrow B
such that:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C\otimes_{k}A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B.}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C\triangleright A}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces u}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\exists!}

We can build the measuring tensor
C\triangleright A
in a dual way as the measuring coalgebra
\underline{\mathrm{Alg}}_{k}(A,B)
.
By the free-forgetful adjunction, a
k
-linear homomorphism
f\colon C\otimes_{k}A\rightarrow B
corresponds to an algebra homomorphism
{f^{\prime}}\colon T(C\otimes A)\rightarrow B
.
Following the commutative diagrams of
Definition

3
, the map
f
is a measuring if and only if its corresponding map
{f^{\prime}}
fits into the following coequalizing diagram in
\mathrm{Alg}_{k}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{T(C\otimes_{k}A^{\otimes 2})}\oplus T(C\otimes_{k}k)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T(C\otimes_{k}A)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B.}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Phi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Psi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{f^{\prime}}}

The morphism
\Phi
is the free map induced by summing the
k
-linear homomorphism
1\otimes\mu_{A}\colon C\otimes_{k}A^{\otimes 2}\rightarrow C\otimes_{k}A
and
1\otimes\eta_{A}\colon C\otimes_{k}k\rightarrow C\otimes_{k}A
.
The morphism
\Psi
is induced from using the forgetful-free adjunction on a
k
-linear homomorphism
(C\otimes_{k}A^{\otimes 2})\oplus(C\otimes_{k}k)\rightarrow T(C\otimes_{k}A)
that we now define.
On the first summand, it is defined as the composite

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C\otimes_{k}A^{\otimes 2}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C^{\otimes 2}\otimes_{k}A^{\otimes 2}\cong(C\otimes_{k}A)^{\otimes 2}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T(C\otimes A)^{\otimes 2}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T(C\otimes A)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Delta\otimes 1}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mu}

where the unlabeled arrow is induced by
C\otimes_{k}A\rightarrow T(C\otimes_{k}A)
, the unit of the free-forgetful adjunction (we have omitted to write
U
for clarity).
On the second summand, it is defined as the composite

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C\otimes_{k}k\cong C}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces k}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T(C\otimes_{k}A)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varepsilon}

where
k\rightarrow T(C\otimes_{k}A)
is the unit of
T(C\otimes_{k}A)
.

With this algebraic perspective, a measuring
C\otimes_{k}A\rightarrow B
can now be reformulated as a choice of a cocone over the parallel morphisms in
\mathrm{Alg}_{k}
that we just described

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{T(C\otimes_{k}A^{\otimes 2})}\oplus T(C\otimes_{k}k)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T(C\otimes_{k}A).}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Phi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Psi}

As coequalizers in
\mathrm{Alg}_{k}
exist, we find the following.

Theorem 2.2

Given a
k
-algebra
B
and a
k
-coalgebra
C
, the measuring tensor
(C\triangleright A,u)
exists and is the following coequalizer in
\mathrm{Alg}_{k}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{T(C\otimes_{k}A^{\otimes 2})}\oplus T(C\otimes_{k}k)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T(C\otimes_{k}A)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C\triangleright A.}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Phi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Psi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces u^{\prime}}

Remark 2

The existence of the measuring tensor was already known in
Fox (1976)
.
The notation
C\triangleright A
first appeared in
Anel and Joyal (2013)
.

Given any algebra
B
, a measuring
C\otimes_{k}A\rightarrow B
corresponds to an algebra homomorphism
C\triangleright A\rightarrow B
, and thus we obtain a natural bijection

\mu_{C}(A,B)\cong\mathrm{Alg}_{k}(C\triangleright A,B)

showing that
\mu_{C}(A,-)\colon\mathrm{Alg}_{k}\rightarrow\mathrm{Set}
is represented by the measuring tensor.

Remark 3

The measuring tensor induces a functor

-\triangleright-\colon\mathrm{coAlg}_{k}\times\mathrm{Alg}_{k}\longrightarrow\mathrm{Alg}_{k}.

It is useful to describe
C\triangleright A
as follows.
Given
c\in C
, and
x\in A
, and the universal measuring
u\colon C\otimes_{k}A\rightarrow C\triangleright A
,
put
c\triangleright x\coloneqq u(c\otimes x)
.
Then
C\triangleright A
is the algebra generated by the symbols
c\triangleright x
under the relations

c\triangleright(xy)=\sum_{(c)}(c_{(1)}\triangleright x)(c_{(2)}\triangleright y),\quad\quad c\triangleright 1_{A}=\varepsilon(c)

for all
c\in C
and
x,y\in A
.

We have now shown that the measuring functor
\mu\colon\mathrm{coAlg}_{k}^{\mathrm{op}}\times\mathrm{Alg}_{k}^{\mathrm{op}}\times\mathrm{Alg}\longrightarrow\mathrm{Set}
of
Definition

4
is represented in each of its variables:

\mu_{C}(A,B)\cong\mathrm{Alg}_{k}(A,[C,B])\cong\mathrm{coAlg}_{k}(C,\underline{\mathrm{Alg}}_{k}(A,B))\cong\mathrm{Alg}_{k}(C\triangleright A,B).

We can strengthen the identification in the following section.

2.9
Enrichment in Coalgebras

Sometimes the hom-sets of a category carry extra structure. For instance, the hom-sets of the category
\mathrm{Vect}_{k}
can be seen to have the structure themselves of a vector space. This paper is about the hom-sets of categories of algebras actually having the structure of a coalgebra.

Definition 8
(enriched category)

Consider a monoidal category
(\mathscr{V},\otimes,\mathbb{I})
, the
enriching
category , and a category
\mathscr{C}
, the
underlying
category.
We say that
\mathscr{C}
has an
enrichment
in
\mathscr{V}
if there is a functor

\underline{\hom}_{\mathscr{C}}(-,-)\colon\mathscr{C}^{\mathrm{op}}\times\mathscr{C}\to\mathscr{V}

together with the following.

•

For each
A\in\mathscr{C}
, a morphism called the
identity
on
A
:

\mathrm{id}_{A}\colon\mathbb{I}\rightarrow\underline{\hom}_{\mathscr{C}}(A,A).

•

For each
A,B,C\in\mathscr{C}
, a morphism in
\mathscr{V}
called
composition

\circ\colon\underline{\hom}_{\mathscr{C}}(B,C)\otimes\underline{\hom}_{\mathscr{C}}(A,B)\longrightarrow\underline{\hom}_{\mathscr{C}}(A,C).

•

For each
A,B\in\mathscr{C}
, a bijection:

\hom_{\mathscr{C}}(A,B)\cong\hom_{\mathscr{C}}(\mathbb{I},\underline{\hom}_{\mathscr{C}}(A,B)).

such that the standard associativity and unitality diagrams commute, all the above morphisms are (extra)natural in
A,B,C
, and such the identity and composition
\mathscr{V}
-morphisms are compatible with the identity and composition morphisms of
\mathscr{C}
.
We also call this structure a
\mathscr{V}
-enriched category
, and when we are considering
\mathscr{C}
with this extra structure, we will sometimes write
\underline{\mathscr{C}}
. The notion was introduced in
Bénabou (1965)
, see
Kelly (1982)
for a standard reference.

Remark 4

It is more standard to define a
\mathscr{V}
-enriched category
\underline{\mathscr{C}}
using only the first two bullet points, without introducing a category
\mathscr{C}
, and deducing an underlying category
\mathscr{C}
using third bullet point. See more details in
Kelly (1982)
.

Example 12

If
\mathscr{V}
is a closed symmetric monoidal category, its internal hom, often denoted
\underline{\hom}_{\mathscr{V}}(-,-)
determines
\mathscr{V}
as enriched over itself.

The category
\mathrm{coAlg}_{k}
is symmetric monoidal closed, with monoidal product given by the tensor product
\otimes_{k}
in
\mathrm{Vect}_{k}
. Indeed, given
k
-coalgebras
(C,\Delta_{C},\varepsilon_{C})
and
(D,\Delta_{D},\varepsilon_{D})
, then
C\otimes_{k}D
is a
k
-coalgebra with comultiplication

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C\otimes_{k}D}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(C\otimes_{k}C)\otimes_{k}(D\otimes_{k}D)\cong(C\otimes_{k}D)\otimes_{k}(C\otimes_{k}D)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Delta_{C}\otimes\Delta_{D}}

and counit

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C\otimes_{k}D}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces k\otimes_{k}k\cong k.}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varepsilon_{C}\otimes\varepsilon_{D}}

The monoidal structure on
\mathrm{coAlg}_{k}
remains closed as
C\otimes_{k}-\colon\mathrm{coAlg}_{k}\rightarrow\mathrm{coAlg}_{k}
preserves all colimits as they are computed in
\mathrm{Vect}_{k}
, which is closed.
Its internal hom

¯

coAlg

coAlg

k

(C,D)
is computed as the following equalizer in
\mathrm{coAlg}_{k}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\underline{\mathrm{coAlg}}_{k}(C,D)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T^{\vee}([C,D])}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T^{\vee}([C,D^{\otimes 2}]).}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Phi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Psi}

The morphism
\Phi
is induced by the comultiplication
D\rightarrow D\otimes_{k}D
. The morphism
\Psi
is induced the forgetful-cofree adjunction on a morphism
T^{\vee}([C,D])\rightarrow[C,D^{\otimes 2}]
defined as the composite

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T^{\vee}([C,D])}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T^{\vee}([C,D])^{\otimes 2}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{[C,D]}^{\otimes 2}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{[C^{\otimes 2},D^{\otimes 2}]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{[C,D^{\otimes 2}]}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Delta}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nabla}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Delta_{C}^{*}}

where the unlabeled map is induced by the counit of the forgetful-cofree adjunction.
In fact, if we restrict to the full subcategory
\mathrm{coCAlg}_{k}
spanned by
cocommutative

k
-coalgebras, then the monoidal structure becomes cartesian closed, and thus can be viewed as an extension on the monoidal structure on
\mathrm{Set}
.

If
\mathscr{V}
is a closed symmetric monoidal category, we say a
\mathscr{V}
-enriched category
\mathscr{C}
is tensored over
\mathscr{V}
if there exists a functor
-\odot-\colon\mathscr{V}\times\mathscr{C}\rightarrow\mathscr{C}
together with a natural isomorphism in
\mathscr{V}

\underline{\hom}_{\mathscr{C}}(V\odot A,B)\cong\underline{\hom}_{\mathscr{V}}(V,\underline{\hom}_{\mathscr{C}}(A,B))

for all
V\in\mathscr{V}
, and
A,B\in\mathscr{C}
.
We say
\mathscr{C}
is
cotensored
(or
powered
)
over
\mathscr{V}
if there exists a functor
-\pitchfork-\colon\mathscr{V}^{\mathrm{op}}\times\mathscr{C}\rightarrow\mathscr{C}
together with a natural isomorphism in
\mathscr{V}

\underline{\hom}_{\mathscr{C}}(A,V\pitchfork B)\cong\underline{\hom}_{\mathscr{V}}(V,\underline{\hom}_{\mathscr{C}}(A,B))

for all
V\in\mathscr{V}
, and
A,B\in\mathscr{C}
.

We are now ready to state the main theorem in this section.
The measuring coalgebra
\underline{\mathrm{Alg}}_{k}(A,B)
will provide the hom-objects of the enrichment
\underline{\hom}_{\mathrm{Alg}_{k}}(A,B)
of algebras in coalgebras, with the universal composition that we described earlier:

\underline{\mathrm{Alg}}_{k}(A_{2},A_{3})\otimes_{k}\underline{\mathrm{Alg}}_{k}(A_{1},A_{2})\rightarrow\underline{\mathrm{Alg}}_{k}(A_{1},A_{3}).

Theorem 2.3

The category
\mathrm{Alg}_{k}
of
k
-algebras is enriched in the closed symmetric monoidal category
(\mathrm{coAlg}_{k},\otimes_{k},k,\underline{\mathrm{coAlg}}_{k}(-,-))
of
k
-coalgebras, for which the hom-coalgebra between two algebras
A
and
B
is given by the universal measuring
\underline{\mathrm{Alg}}_{k}(A,B)
.
It is also tensored over
\mathrm{coAlg}_{k}
via the measuring tensor and cotensored via the convolution algebra. In other words, we obtain the following natural isomorphisms in
\mathrm{coAlg}_{k}
:

\underline{\mathrm{coAlg}}_{k}(C,\underline{\mathrm{Alg}}_{k}(A,B))\cong\underline{\mathrm{Alg}}_{k}(C\triangleright A,B)\cong\underline{\mathrm{Alg}}_{k}(A,[C,B]).

Proof

The convolution algebra
[-,-]\colon\mathrm{coAlg}_{k}^{\mathrm{op}}\times\mathrm{Alg}_{k}\rightarrow\mathrm{Alg}_{k}
determines
\mathrm{Alg}_{k}
as a right module over
\mathrm{coAlg}_{k}^{\mathrm{op}}
, in the sense that we have the following natural isomorphisms in
\mathrm{Alg}_{k}
for all coalgebras
C,D
and algebra
A

[C\otimes_{k}D,A]\cong[C,[D,A]]\quad\quad[k,A]\cong A.

Thus
\mathrm{Alg}_{k}^{\mathrm{op}}
is a left module over
\mathrm{coAlg}_{k}
via
[-,-]^{\mathrm{op}}\colon\mathrm{coAlg}_{k}\times\mathrm{Alg}_{k}^{\mathrm{op}}\rightarrow\mathrm{Alg}_{k}^{\mathrm{op}}
.
Moreover, for any algebra
B
, the induced functor
[-,B]^{\mathrm{op}}\colon\mathrm{coAlg}_{k}\rightarrow\mathrm{Alg}_{k}^{\mathrm{op}}
has a right adjoint
\underline{\mathrm{Alg}}_{k}(-,B)\colon\mathrm{Alg}_{k}^{\mathrm{op}}\rightarrow\mathrm{coAlg}_{k}
as we have the natural isomorphism

\mathrm{coAlg}_{k}(C,\underline{\mathrm{Alg}}_{k}(A,B))\cong\mathrm{Alg}_{k}(A,[C,B]).

By
(
Hyland et al., 2017
, 3.1)
, we obtain that
\mathrm{Alg}_{k}^{\mathrm{op}}
is enriched, tensored and cotensored over
\mathrm{coAlg}_{k}
.
Thus
\mathrm{Alg}_{k}
is enriched, tensored and cotensored over
\mathrm{coAlg}_{k}
.

The above result first appeared in
Sweedler (1969)
and was stated later in similar terms as us in
Fox (1976)
.
The result has been extended in many different contexts, see
Anel and Joyal (2013)
;
Hyland et al. (2017)
;
Vasilakopoulou (2019)
;
Grignou (2022)
;
Péroux (2022)
;
McDermott et al. (2022)
.

Remark 5

While
\mathrm{Alg}_{k}
is enriched in
\mathrm{coAlg}_{k}
, we do not have that
\mathrm{coAlg}_{k}
is enriched in
\mathrm{Alg}_{k}
. However, because
\mathrm{coAlg}_{k}
is closed symmetric monoidal, it is enriched in itself.
This actually also defines a notion of measurings and partial homomorphism for coalgebras as we can now define.

Let
(C,\Delta,\varepsilon)
,
(C_{1},\Delta_{1},\varepsilon_{1})
and
(C_{2},\Delta_{2},\varepsilon_{2})
be
k
-coalgebras.
A
C
-measuring
from
C_{1}
to
C_{2}
is a homomorphism of coalgebras
f\colon C\otimes_{k}C_{1}\rightarrow C_{2}
.
We say a map
\widetilde{f}\colon C\rightarrow[C_{1},C_{2}]
is a
C
-indexed partial homomorphism from
C_{1}
to
C_{2}
if its adjunct
C\otimes_{k}C_{1}\rightarrow C_{2}
is a
C
-measuring. In other words, if we denote
f_{c}\coloneqq\widetilde{f}(c)
for all
c\in C
, this means for all
x\in C_{1}
:

\sum_{(f_{c}(x))}{f_{c}(x)}_{(1)}\otimes{f_{c}(x)}_{(2)}=\sum_{(x)}\sum_{(c)}f_{c_{(1)}}(x_{(1)})\otimes f_{c_{(2)}}(x_{(2)})

and

\varepsilon_{2}(f_{c}(x))=\varepsilon(c)\varepsilon_{1}(x).

We see that
f_{c}\colon C_{1}\rightarrow C_{2}
is a homomorphism of coalgebras if
c
is a grouplike element.

With these notions, we interpret the internal hom
\underline{\mathrm{coAlg}}_{k}(C_{1},C_{2})
as the universal measuring coalgebra from
C_{1}
to
C_{2}
, in the sense that, given any
C
-partial homomorphism
f\colon C\rightarrow[C_{1},C_{2}]
, there exists a unique homomorphism of coalgebras
u_{f}\colon C\rightarrow\underline{\mathrm{coAlg}}_{k}(C_{1},C_{2})
such that the following diagram commutes:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{[C_{1},C_{2}]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\underline{\mathrm{coAlg}}_{k}(C_{1},C_{2}).}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\exists!\,u_{f}}

This is making precise the natural identification

\underline{\mathrm{coAlg}}_{k}(C\otimes_{k}C_{1},C_{2})\cong\underline{\mathrm{coAlg}}_{k}(C,\underline{\mathrm{coAlg}}_{k}(C_{1},C_{2}))\cong\underline{\mathrm{coAlg}}_{k}(C_{1},\underline{\mathrm{coAlg}}_{k}(C,C_{2})).

This is the coalgebraic analogue of
Theorem

2.3
.

3
Data Types as (Co)algebras

In this section, we explain how most (data) types in functional programming languages are obtained as initial algebras for endofunctors.

Here, we study the
categorical semantics

Scott (1970)
of functional programming languages. We will not make anything very precise on the programming side but will rather use that as inspiration. Precision will happen on the category theory side.

To simplify things, a functional programming language consists of (data) types, terms, and functions. Often encountered types are, for instance, the type of natural numbers, the type of booleans, and the type of strings; types are roughly analogous to sets. Like sets, types have constituents, their terms. For instance,
0
is a term of the type of natural numbers, and
True
is a term of the type of booleans. In the programming language Haskell, this is written as
0::\mathtt{N}
and
\mathtt{True}::\mathtt{Bool}
. Just as between sets, we have functions between types. For instance, there is a function
\mathtt{Bool}\to\mathtt{N}
that sends
\mathtt{True}\mapsto\mathtt{0}
and
\mathtt{False}\mapsto\mathtt{1}
. In such a language, there is also a unit type
unit
that has exactly one term so that functions
\mathtt{unit}\to T
correspond to terms of
T
, and we thus think of functions as generalized terms.

In the
categorical semantics
of such a programming language, we choose a category
\mathscr{C}
, and we ‘interpret’ types of the programming language as objects of
\mathscr{C}
and functions as morphisms of
\mathscr{C}
. Here, we will not make ‘interpretation’ precise, but instead will gloss over that and make definitions directly in category theory. As mentioned, types usually bear much similarity to sets, so one often chooses
\mathscr{C}
to be
\mathrm{Set}
, and this article is no exception.

3.1
W-Types

In functional programming languages, it is customary to define types via their constructors.
For instance, in Haskell, one defines the type of natural numbers by the following:

\displaystyle\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{0,1,0}data\ }{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}Nat\ }{\color[rgb]{1,0.19,1}=\ }{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}Zero\ }|{\ \color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}Succ\ Nat}}.

The natural numbers are usually defined in this way in functional programming these days, but the basic idea of this definition goes all the way back to Peano
Peano (1889)
.
Lawvere
Lawvere (2004)
gave it its categorical instantiation that we use here.
We read the above definition as saying, first of all, that the type
Nat
has a term
Zero
::
Nat
and a function
\mathtt{Succ}::\mathtt{Nat}\to\mathtt{Nat}
(i.e. terms
\mathtt{Succ}n::\mathtt{Nat}
for every
n::\mathtt{Nat}
).
Second of all, inherent in the
data
keyword,
Nat
is defined to be
freely generated
by these two
constructors
,
Zero
and
Succ
. A type being freely generated is about how the programming language allows one to define functions out of a type. For
Nat
, it suffices to define such a function on the constructors
Zero
and
Succ n
.

When taking the categorical semantics of such a programming language, the body of such a definition is interpreted as specifying an endofunctor; the interpretation of
Nat
is then taken to be the initial algebra of this endofunctor.

That is, for
Nat
, the specification above gives two constructors
\mathtt{Zero}::\mathtt{Nat}
(equivalently a function
\mathtt{Zero}\colon 1\to\mathtt{Nat}
where
1
denotes the unit type) and
\mathtt{Succ}\colon\mathtt{Nat}\to\mathtt{Nat}
(where
1
is a terminal object). Categorically, the existence of such morphisms is equivalent to the existence of one morphism
\mathtt{Zero+Succ}\colon 1+\mathtt{Nat}\to\mathtt{Nat}
. Thus, an object
X
of a category
\mathscr{C}
has the “structure of
Nat
” if it has a morphism
1+X\to X
(assuming that
\mathscr{C}
has a terminal object
1
and coproduct
1+X
). We are led then to consider the endofunctor
1+\mathrm{id}\colon\mathscr{C}\to\mathscr{C}
that sends
X\mapsto 1+X
, and algebras
(X,f\colon 1+X\to X)
of
1+\mathrm{id}
.

Definition 9
(algebra)

Given an endofunctor
F
on a category
\mathscr{C}
, an
algebra
for
F
, or simply
an
F
-algebra
, is a pair
(A,f)
where
A
is an object of
\mathscr{C}
together with a morphism
f\colon FA\to A
in
\mathscr{C}
, with no further assumptions.
An
algebra homomorphism

\eta\colon(A,f)\to(B,g)
consists of a morphism
\eta\colon A\to B
such that the following diagram commutes in
\mathscr{C}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces FA}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces FB}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B.}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F\eta}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\eta}

Let
\mathrm{Alg}_{F}{\mathscr{C}}
, or simply
\mathrm{Alg}
if
\mathscr{C}
and
F
are understood, denote the category of algebras of
F
. We denote by
Alg(A,B)
the set of algebra homomorphisms
A\to B
.

However, there are many sets with the structure of the natural numbers, but we want the specification above to characterize the natural numbers uniquely (up to isomorphism). We want to capture the idea that
Nat
is
freely generated
by the constructors
Zero
and
Succ
– that is, by the existence of an algebra structure. Categorically, this corresponds to taking the initial object of
\mathrm{Alg}_{F}{\mathscr{C}}
, the
initial algebra
(an idea which goes back to
Goguen et al. (1978)
).

For instance, if we take the category
\mathrm{Set}
of sets and the endofunctor
1+\mathrm{id}
, then the initial object of
\mathrm{Alg}_{1+\mathrm{id}}{\mathrm{Set}}
is the usual natural numbers
\mathbb{N}
. Indeed, the natural numbers has an algebra structure: we take the function
1\to\mathbb{N}
to be the one corresponding to
0\in\mathbb{N}
, and we take the function
\mathbb{N}\to\mathbb{N}
to be the one taking
n\mapsto n+1
(where here,
n+1
refers to the usual addition within
\mathbb{N}
, not a coproduct with the terminal object). It is initial because given any other algebra
(A,f)
, we can construct a function
i\colon\mathbb{N}\to A
by sending
0\mapsto f(*)
and inductively sending
n+1\mapsto fi(n)
. Indeed, the algebra structure on
A
is
exactly
the information needed to inductively construct a function
\mathbb{N}\to A
.

In programming languages such as Haskell, the main way of defining types is via their constructors. In this way, one can define any finite type (i.e., type corresponding to a finite set), lists with values of any other type, various varieties of trees, etc.

One widely considered class of such types are called
W-types
, originally introduced with Martin–Löf type theory
Martin-Löf (1982)
. Categorically, W-types correspond to initial algebras for polynomial endofunctors
Dybjer (1997)
;
Moerdijk and Palmgren (2000)
.

Definition 10
(polynomial endofunctor, W-type)

Consider a category
\mathscr{C}
, objects
A,B
of
\mathscr{C}
, and a morphism
f\colon A\to B
. The endofunctor
\lx@glossaries@gls@link{main}{PolyEndo}{{{}}\mathcal{P}_{f}}\colon\mathscr{C}\to\mathscr{C}
is the composition displayed below (if it exists):

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathscr{C}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathscr{C}/A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathscr{C}/B}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathscr{C}.}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces-\times A}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\prod_{f}}
dom

Here,
-\times A
denotes the functor that takes an object
X\in\mathscr{C}
to the object
\pi_{A}\colon X\times A\to A
in the slice category
\mathscr{C}/A
. The functor
\prod_{f}\colon\mathscr{C}/A\to\mathscr{C}/B
denotes the right adjoint to pullback along
f
, and
dom
is the functor that sends an object
X\to B
of
\mathscr{C}/B
to
X
.
By
polynomial endofunctor on
\mathscr{C}
we mean any endofunctor on
\mathscr{C}
obtained in this way.
Given a
f\colon A\to B
, we denote by
W(f)
the initial algebra of the endofunctor
\mathcal{P}_{f}
. A
(categorical) W-type
is an algebra of the form
W(f)
.

Example 13

Consider polynomial endofunctors on
\mathrm{Set}
. Consider two sets
A
and
B
and a function
f\colon A\to B
. Note that for an object
X\in\mathscr{C}
, we have

\displaystyle\mathcal{P}_{f}X

\displaystyle\coloneqq\textsc{dom}\ \prod_{f}(X\times A)

\displaystyle\cong\sum_{b\in B}{\mathrm{Set}/B}(b,\prod_{f}(X\times A))

\displaystyle\cong\sum_{b\in B}{\mathrm{Set}/A}(f^{-1}b,X\times A)

\displaystyle\cong\sum_{b\in B}{\mathrm{Set}}(f^{-1}b,X).

Letting
Fb\coloneqq f^{-1}b
, we find that
\mathcal{P}_{f}X\cong\sum_{b\in B}X^{Fb}
(where
X^{Fb}
denotes exponentiation, i.e., the internal hom, in
\mathrm{Set}
). Now, we find that
\mathcal{P}_{f}
does indeed look like a polynomial.

Example 14

Consider the endofunctor
X\mapsto 1+X
on
\mathrm{Set}
. We claim that we can write this as a polynomial endofunctor. That is, we want to find an
f\colon A\to B
such that
1+X\cong\sum_{b\in B}X^{Fb}
. Since there are two summands in
1+X
, we take
B
to be the set
\{\mathtt{Zero},\mathtt{Succ}\}
. We want
X^{F(\mathtt{Zero})}\cong 1
, so we take
F(\mathtt{Zero})\cong\emptyset
. We want
X^{F(\mathtt{Succ})}\cong X
, so we take
F(\mathtt{Succ})\cong 1
. Thus, we take
f
to be the inclusion
\{\mathtt{Succ}\}\to\{\mathtt{Zero},\mathtt{Succ}\}
.

Example 15

Given a type
\mathtt{a}
, we define the type of lists in
\mathtt{a}
via the following specification in Haskell:

\displaystyle\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{0,1,0}data\ }{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}List\ a\ }{\color[rgb]{1,0.19,1}=\ }{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}Nil\ }|{\ \color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}Cons\ a\ (List\ a)}}.

This has two constructors:
\mathtt{Nil}\colon 1\to\mathtt{List\ a}
and
\mathtt{Cons}\colon\mathtt{a}\times\mathtt{List\ a}\to\mathtt{List\ a}
, in categorical terms. This is equivalently a map
1+\mathtt{a}\times\mathtt{List\ a}\to\mathtt{List\ a}
. That is, we want
\mathtt{List\ a}
to be an algebra of the endofunctor
1+\mathtt{a}\times-
.
We consider this endofunctor on
\mathrm{Set}
. We have that:

\displaystyle 1+\mathtt{a}\times X

\displaystyle\cong X^{0}+\mathtt{a}\times X^{1}

\displaystyle\cong X^{0}+\sum_{t\in\mathtt{a}}X^{1}

\displaystyle\cong\sum_{t\in 1+\mathtt{a}}X^{Ft},

where
F\colon 1+\mathtt{a}\to\mathrm{Set}
sends the element of
1
to
0
, the empty set, and any
t\in\mathtt{a}
to
1
. Thus, this is the polynomial endofunctor associated to the inclusion
i:\mathtt{a}\hookrightarrow 1+\mathtt{a}
; that is
\mathcal{P}_{i}\cong 1+\mathtt{a}\times-
.
That is, where one could say that our Haskell specification has two constructors,
Nil
and
Cons
, the W-type specification has
(1+\mathtt{a})
-many constructors.

We now claim that the initial algebra of this endofunctor is the set of lists with entries from
\mathtt{a}
.
To be a bit more precise, it is
\sum_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\mathtt{a}^{n}
. This has an algebra structure: we take
Nil
to correspond to the single element of
\mathtt{a}^{0}
in the disjoint union, and we take
\texttt{Cons}\colon\mathtt{a}\times\mathtt{List\ a}\to\mathtt{List\ a}
to send
(a\in\mathtt{a},\ell\in\mathtt{a}^{n})
to the element
a.\ell\in\mathtt{a}^{n+1}
where
a.\ell(0)\coloneqq a
and
a.\ell(m+1)\coloneqq\ell(m)
for any
m\in\mathbb{N}
. It is initial because we can use any structure
n+c\colon 1+\mathtt{a}\times L\to L
of any other algebra to “inductively” build a unique algebra homomorphism
u:\mathtt{List\ a}\to L
.
Indeed, we send the single element of the summand
a^{0}
in
\mathtt{List\ a}
to the element corresponding to
n:1\to L
,
and then we inductively send an element
\texttt{Cons}(a,\ell)\in a^{n+1}
to
c(a,u\ell)
. One can check that this is an algebra homomorphism (by construction) and is the unique such.

Theorem 3.1
(
Dybjer (1997)
;
Moerdijk and Palmgren (2000)
)

Given any function
f\colon A\to B
in
\mathrm{Set}
, the categorical W-type
W(f)
exists
(
Dybjer, 1997
, Thm. 1)
.
In fact, all categorical W-types exist in any topos
(
Moerdijk and Palmgren, 2000
, Prop. 3.6)
with a natural numbers object.

There is an important construction of initial algebras that we do not explicitly use in this article, but that provides a lot of intuition.

Theorem 3.2
(Adámek’s fixed point theorem,
Adámek (1974)
)

Consider a category
\mathscr{C}
with an initial object
0
and colimits of sequences
\omega\to\mathscr{C}
, where
\omega
denotes the countably infinite ordinal. Consider an endofunctor
F
that preserves these colimits. Then the colimit of the following sequence is an initial algebra of
F
:

0\xrightarrow{!}F0\xrightarrow{F!}F^{2}0\to\cdots.

One can check that polynomial endofunctors
\mathcal{P}_{f}
preserve such colimits when all fibers of
f
are finite, as in all of our examples. For instance, for the endofunctor
1+\mathrm{id}
on
\mathrm{Set}
, we can compute the initial algebra by taking the colimit of the above sequence, which becomes the following.

0\hookrightarrow 1\hookrightarrow 2\hookrightarrow\cdots

Now, we can easily see that it (or at least its underlying set) is
\mathbb{N}
.

3.2
M-Types

In this previous section, we investigated the categorical semantics of types as initial algebras. This begs the question: is there a dual story, and what does it capture?

Indeed, there is a dual story about
co
algebras,
terminal
coalgebras, and M-types (the letter M being an upside-down W). In this story, we take the specifications of the types to be the same as in the preceding story. That is, we take the same endofunctors – in particular, polynomial endofunctors – and we consider terminal coalgebras of them.

Note that in some categories – for instance the category of DCPOs, used to model languages like Haskell – initial algebras and terminal coalgebras coincide
Smyth and Plotkin (1982)
. When this happens, they are often called
canonical fixed points
. In others – for instance
\mathrm{Set}
, used to model languages like Coq and Agda – they do not.

Definition 11
(coalgebra)

Consider an endofunctor
F
on a category
\mathscr{C}
. A
coalgebra
of
F
, or simply an
F
-coalgebra
, is a pair
(C,f)
where
C
is an object of
\mathscr{C}
and
f\colon C\to FC
is a morphism in
\mathscr{C}
. A
coalgebra homomorphism

h\colon(C,f)\to(D,g)
consists of a morphism
h\colon C\to D
in
\mathscr{C}
such that the following diagram commutes:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces D}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces FC}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces FD.}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces h}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Fh}

The category of coalgebras, denoted
\mathrm{coAlg}_{\mathscr{C}}{F}
or simply
\mathrm{coAlg}
if
\mathscr{C}
and
F
are understood, has coalgebras of
F
as objects and coalgebra homomorphisms as morphisms.
We denote by
Alg(C,D)
the set of
F
-coalgebra homomorphisms.

Definition 12
(M-type)

Given a morphism
f\colon A\to B
in a category
\mathscr{C}
, we denote by
M(f)
the terminal coalgebra of
\mathcal{P}_{f}
. By
(categorical) M-type
we mean any coalgebra of the form
M(f)
.

There is an important relationship between initial algebras and terminal coalgebras. First, the algebra structure morphism for an initial algebra is always an isomorphism (by the next proposition).
This is why initial algebras and terminal coalgebras are often called (least or greatest)
fixed points
for the endofunctor.
This isomorphism makes an initial algebra into a coalgebra, and there is then an induced morphism from the initial algebra to the terminal coalgebra. The dual story holds for terminal coalgebras, but the resulting morphism from the initial algebra to the terminal coalgebra is the same.

Proposition 1
(
Lambek (1968)
)

Consider an endofunctor on a category with an initial algebra
(A,\alpha)
and a terminal coalgebra
(C,\chi)
. Then we find the following.

1.

The morphisms
\alpha
and
\chi
are isomorphisms.

2.

The pair
(A,\alpha^{-1})
is a coalgebra. Dually,
(C,\chi^{-1})
is an algebra.

3.

There is a morphism
A\to C
.

Moreover, M-types are often an infinitary ‘completion’ of a W-type, in the sense suggested by the following examples. Indeed, W-types can be thought of (and are often called) “well-founded trees” while M-types are “non-well-founded trees”. For precise statements, see the discussion of this relationship within homotopy type theory in
Rech (2017)
.

Example 16

Consider the endofunctor
1+\mathrm{id}\colon\mathrm{Set}\to\mathrm{Set}
. We saw above that its initial algebra is
\mathbb{N}
.
We claim that its terminal coalgebra is
ℕ

∞

. The underlying set of
{\mathbb{N}_{\infty}}
is
\mathbb{N}+\{\infty\}
. Its coalgebra structure
{\mathbb{N}_{\infty}}\to 1+{\mathbb{N}_{\infty}}
is given by sending
0
to the unique element of the summand
1
and any other
n
to
n-1
, where
\infty-1\coloneqq\infty
.

Given any other coalgebra
\chi\colon C\to 1+C
, we let
\llbracket{c}\rrbracket
denote the least
n
such that
\chi^{n}(c)
is the unique element of the summand
1
. One can check that this defines the only coalgebra homomorphism
\lx@glossaries@gls@link{main}{TermiCoalgNatMap}{{{}}\llbracket{-}\rrbracket}\colon C\to{\mathbb{N}_{\infty}}
.
The induced map
\mathbb{N}\to{\mathbb{N}_{\infty}}
from
Proposition

1
is the inclusion.

Example 17

Consider the endofunctor
1+\mathtt{a}\times-\colon\mathrm{Set}\to\mathrm{Set}
. We saw in
Example

15
that its initial algebra is the set of (finite) lists with elements from
\mathtt{a}
.
We claim that the underlying set of its terminal coalgebra is the set of potentially infinite lists, often called
streams
, with elements from
\mathtt{a}
, that is
\sum_{n\in{\mathbb{N}_{\infty}}}\mathtt{a}^{n}
.
The coalgebra structure
\sum_{n\in{\mathbb{N}_{\infty}}}\mathtt{a}^{n}\to 1+\mathtt{a}\times\sum_{n\in{\mathbb{N}_{\infty}}}\mathtt{a}^{n}
is given by sending the element of the summand
\mathtt{a}^{0}
to the element of the summand
1
. We send an
\ell\in\mathtt{a}^{\infty}
to the pair
(\ell(0),\ell)
. For other
n
, we send an element
\ell\in\mathtt{a}^{n}
to the pair
(\ell(0),\ell^{\prime})
where
\ell^{\prime}(m)\coloneqq\ell(m-1)
.

There is a unique coalgebra homomorphism from any other coalgebra
C
to this one of streams. Intuitively, one sees the map
\chi\colon C\to 1+\mathtt{a}\times C
as a partially defined function
\chi\colon C\to\mathtt{a}\times C
. For any
c\in C
,
\chi(c)
, if defined, is a pair of a ‘head’ in
\mathtt{a}
and ‘tail’ in
C
. By repeatedly applying
\chi
to any
c
until it is not defined anymore, one can construct a stream from
c
. One can check that this is the unique coalgebra homomorphism.
Just as in the above example, the induced map from the initial algebra to the terminal coalgebra is the natural inclusion of lists into streams.

We also have the dual to Adámek’s fixed point theorem for initial algebras.

Theorem 3.3
(Adámek’s fixed point theorem,
Adámek (1974)
)

Consider a category
\mathscr{C}
with a terminal object
1
and limits of sequences
\omega\to\mathscr{C}
, where
\omega
denotes the countably infinite ordinal. Consider an endofunctor
F
that preserves these limits. Then the limit of the following sequence is a terminal coalgebra of
F
:

\cdots\to F^{2}1\xrightarrow{F!}F1\xrightarrow{!}1.

One can check that polynomial endofunctors
\mathcal{P}_{f}
preserve connected limits. So for instance, for the endofunctor
1+\mathrm{id}
on
\mathrm{Set}
, we can compute the terminal coalgebra by taking the limit of the above sequence, which becomes

\cdots\to 3\to 2\to 1,

where each morphism identifies two elements of the domain.
Now, we can see that it (or at least its underlying set) is
{\mathbb{N}_{\infty}}
.

4
Measuring of Inductive Data Types

In this section, we now show that there is a version of Sweedler’s theory of measuring coalgebras (
