Sect.

2
) for (co)algebras of endofunctors (
Sect.

3
).
So fix a symmetric monoidal closed category
(\mathscr{C},\otimes,\lx@glossaries@gls@link{main}{MonUnit}{{{}}\mathbb{I}},[-,-])

and a lax symmetric monoidal endofunctor
(F,\nabla,\eta)
(defined below in
Definition

13
) on
\mathscr{C}
.

4.1
Lax Symmetric Monoidal Endofunctors

In order to define the general notion of
measuring
for algebras over an endofunctor
F
, we first need to require that
F
is lax symmetric monoidal.

Example 18

Our running example throughout this section will be
\mathrm{Set}
with its cartesian monoidal structure, and the endofunctor
X\mapsto X+1
.

Definition 13
(lax symmetric monoidal endofunctor)

That
(F,\nabla,\eta)
is a
lax symmetric monoidal endofunctor
means that
F
is an endofunctor on
\mathscr{C}
with

(L1):

a natural transformation
\nabla_{X,Y}\colon FX\otimes FY\longrightarrow F(X\otimes Y)
, for all
X,Y\in\mathscr{C}
; and

(L2):

a morphism
\eta\colon\mathbb{I}\rightarrow F\mathbb{I}
in
\mathscr{C}
;

such that
(F,\nabla,\eta)
is associative, unital, and symmetric, in the sense that the following diagrams commute.

(Associativity):

For all objects
X,Y,Z\in\mathscr{C}
, the following diagram commutes:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces FX\otimes FY\otimes FZ}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F(X\otimes Y)\otimes FZ}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces FX\otimes F(Y\otimes Z)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F(X\otimes Y\otimes Z).}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nabla_{X,Y}\otimes\mathrm{id}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{id}\otimes\nabla_{Y,Z}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nabla_{X\otimes Y,Z}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nabla_{X,Y\otimes Z}}

(Unitality):

For all objects
X\in\mathscr{C}
, the following diagrams commute:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces FX}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{I}\otimes FX}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F\mathbb{I}\otimes FX}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces FX}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces FX\otimes\mathbb{I}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces FX\otimes F\mathbb{I}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F(\mathbb{I}\otimes X)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F(X\otimes\mathbb{I})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces FX}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces FX.}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cong}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\eta\otimes\mathrm{id}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nabla_{\mathbb{I},X}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cong}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{id}\otimes\eta}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nabla_{X,\mathbb{I}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cong}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cong}

(Symmetry):

For all objects
X,Y\in\mathscr{C}
, the following diagram commutes

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces FX\otimes FY}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F(X\otimes Y)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces FY\otimes FX}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F(Y\otimes X),}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nabla_{X,Y}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cong}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\tau_{FX,FY}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F(\tau_{X,Y})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cong}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nabla_{Y,X}}

where
\tau_{X,Y}:X\otimes Y\rightarrow Y\otimes X
is the symmetry natural isomorphism in
\mathscr{C}
.

Remark 6

One may also say
symmetric lax monoidal
instead of lax symmetric monoidal.
Notice the symmetry is not truly needed in our work but makes the statement of enrichment in
Theorem

4.3
easier.

Example 19

For the endofunctor
\mathrm{id}+1
, we define

\displaystyle\nabla_{X,Y}\colon(X+1)\times(Y+1)

\displaystyle\longrightarrow(X\times Y)+1

\displaystyle(x,y)

\displaystyle\longmapsto\begin{cases}(x,y)&\text{if }x\neq\mathtt{t},y\neq\mathtt{t}\\
\mathtt{t}&\text{otherwise.}\end{cases}

for
x\in X,y\in Y,\mathtt{t}\in 1
.
We define
\eta\colon 1\to 1+1
to be the inclusion into the first summand.

Note that since
F
is lax monoidal, it is also
lax closed
: that is, there is a map

\widetilde{\nabla}_{X,Y}\colon F\left([X,Y]\right)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle}}{{\longrightarrow}}[FX,FY]

natural in
X,Y\in\mathscr{C}
.
As
-\otimes X\colon\mathscr{C}\rightarrow\mathscr{C}
has the right adjoint
[X,-]\colon\mathscr{C}\rightarrow\mathscr{C}
, the above map is the adjunct of the composition

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F([X,Y])\otimes F(X)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F([X,Y]\otimes X)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F(Y),}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nabla_{[X,Y],X}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F(\mathrm{ev}_{X})}

in which
\mathrm{ev}_{X}
is the counit of the adjunction
-\otimes X\dashv[X,-]
.

4.2
The Convolution Algebra

Let
F\colon\mathscr{C}\rightarrow\mathscr{C}
be any lax symmetric monoidal endofunctor.
Given
(C,\chi)
an
F
-coalgebra and
(B,\beta)
an
F
-algebra, then the object
[C,B]
in
\mathscr{C}
is also an
F
-algebra via the
convolution product

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F([C,B])}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{[FC,FB]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{[FC,B]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{[C,B]}.}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widetilde{\nabla}_{C,B}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\beta_{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\chi^{*}}

We call this
F
-algebra
[C,B]
the
convolution algebra
of
C
with
B
.
The construction is natural in
C
and
B
and defines a functor

\mathrm{coAlg}^{\mathrm{op}}\times\mathrm{Alg}\longrightarrow\mathrm{Alg}.

Example 20

The lax monoidal structure on
F
determines an
F
-coalgebra structure on
\eta\colon\mathbb{I}\rightarrow F(\mathbb{I})
.
One can check that the resulted convolution algebra
[\mathbb{I},B]
is isomorphic as an
F
-algebra to
B
.

4.3
Measurings and Partial Algebra Homomorphisms

Recall that as
\mathscr{C}
is closed, we obtain natural bijections

\mathscr{C}(C\otimes A,B)\cong\mathscr{C}(C,[A,B])\cong\mathscr{C}(A,[C,B]),

for any object
A,B,C
in
\mathscr{C}
.
Given a lax symmetric monoidal endofunctor
F\colon\mathscr{C}\rightarrow\mathscr{C}
, if we require
A
and
B
to be
F
-algebras, and
C
an
F
-coalgebra, then as
[C,B]
is an
F
-algebra via the convolution algebra, we may consider the maps
A\rightarrow[C,B]
in
\mathscr{C}
to be
F
-algebra homomorphisms instead. By the identification above, they correspond to certain maps
C\otimes A\rightarrow B
and
C\rightarrow[A,B]
in
\mathscr{C}
with additional conditions that we now describe.

Definition 14
(measuring)

Consider
F
-algebras
(A,\alpha)
and
(B,\beta)
, and a
F
-coalgebra
(C,\chi)
.
We call a map
\varphi\colon C\otimes A\rightarrow B
a
measuring from
A
to
B
if it makes the following diagram commute:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F(C)\otimes F(A)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F(C\otimes A)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F(B)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C\otimes F(A)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C\otimes A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B.}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nabla_{C,A}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F(\varphi)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\beta}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\chi\otimes\mathrm{id}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{id}\otimes\alpha}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi}

We denote by
\mu_{C}(A,B)
the set of all measurings
C\otimes A\rightarrow B
.

If
\varphi\colon C\otimes A\rightarrow B
is a measuring,
a\colon(A^{\prime},\alpha^{\prime})\rightarrow(A,\alpha)
and
b\colon(B,\beta)\rightarrow(B^{\prime},\beta^{\prime})
are algebra homomorphisms, and
c\colon(C^{\prime},\chi^{\prime})\rightarrow(C,\chi)
is a coalgebra homomorphism, then one can check that the composite

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C^{\prime}\otimes A^{\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C\otimes A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces c\otimes a}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces b}

is a measuring. Therefore, the assignment
(C,A,B)\mapsto\mu_{C}(A,B)
underlies a functor

\mu\colon{\mathrm{coAlg}}^{\mathrm{op}}\times{\mathrm{Alg}}^{\mathrm{op}}\times{\mathrm{Alg}}\longrightarrow\mathrm{Set}.

We shall see that this functor is representable in each of its variables under reasonable hypotheses. The convolution algebra provides a first representing object for
\mu_{C}(-,B)\colon{\mathrm{Alg}}^{\mathrm{op}}\to\mathrm{Set}
. Indeed, we have the following bijection natural in
C,A,B
:

\mu_{C}(A,B)\cong{\mathrm{Alg}}(A,[C,B]).

In other words, a measuring
\varphi\colon C\otimes A\rightarrow B
corresponds to an algebra homomorphism
\widehat{\varphi}\colon A\rightarrow[C,B]
under the bijection
\mathscr{C}(C\otimes A,B)\cong\mathscr{C}(A,\underline{\hom}_{\mathscr{C}}(C,B))
. Indeed, notice that
\widehat{\varphi}
is a homomorphism if and only if the following diagram, the adjunct of the one appearing in
Definition

14
, commutes:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F([C,B])}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{[F(C),F(B)]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F(A)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{[FC,B]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{[C,B].}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widetilde{\nabla}_{C,B}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\beta_{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F(\widehat{\varphi})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\chi}^{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widehat{\varphi}}

Example 21

The monoidal unit
\mathbb{I}
of
\mathscr{C}
is a coalgebra via the lax symmetric monoidal structure
\eta\colon\mathbb{I}\rightarrow F(\mathbb{I})
. Thus morphisms
A\to B
in
\mathscr{C}
are in bijection with morphisms
\mathbb{I}\otimes A\to B
in
\mathscr{C}
, and one can check that a morphism
A\to B
in
\mathscr{C}
is an algebra homomorphism if and only if
\mathbb{I}\otimes A\to B
is a measuring. Thus,
\mu_{\mathbb{I}}(A,B)\cong{\mathrm{Alg}}(A,B)
.

Example 22

Consider algebras
A
and
B
and a coalgebra
C
for the endofunctor
X\mapsto 1+X
.
A measuring from
A
to
B
by
C
is a function
f\colon C\times A\rightarrow B
such that the following three properties hold:

(M1):

f_{c}(0_{A})=0_{B}
for all
c\in C
;

(M2):

f_{c}(a+1)=0_{B}
for all
\llbracket c\rrbracket=0
and for all
a\in A
;

(M3):

f_{c}(a+1)=f_{c-1}(a)+1
for
\llbracket c\rrbracket\geq 1
and for all
a\in A
.

Here, we write
f_{c}(a)
for
f(c,a)
. Since
A
has an algebra structure
A+1\to A
, it has a distinguished element, that we write above as
0_{A}
, and an endofunction, that we write above as
a+1
; similarly for
B
. Since
C
has a coalgebra structure, there is a unique map
\llbracket{-}\rrbracket:C\to{\mathbb{N}_{\infty}}
(recalling that
{\mathbb{N}_{\infty}}
is the terminal coalgebra from
Example

16
). The coalgebra structure map is a partial endofunction that we denote as
c-1
above.

Another formulation of measurings is possible. An algebra homomorphism
A\rightarrow[C,B]
corresponds also to a certain map
C\rightarrow[A,B]
in
\mathscr{C}
that we now describe.

Definition 15
(partial homomorphism)

Given algebras
(A,\alpha)
and
(B,\beta)
and a coalgebra
(C,\chi)
, a
C
-indexed partial homomorphism
from
A
to
B
is a map
\widetilde{\varphi}\colon C\rightarrow[A,B]
in
\mathscr{C}
such that its adjunct
\widehat{\varphi}\colon A\rightarrow[C,B]
is an algebra homomorphism i.e.,
fits in the following commutative diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F(C)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F([A,B])}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{[FA,FB]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{[A,B]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{[FA,B]}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F(\widetilde{\varphi})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widetilde{\nabla}_{A,B}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\beta_{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\chi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widetilde{\varphi}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha^{*}}

where
\alpha^{*}
denotes precomposition by
\alpha
and
\beta_{*}
denotes postcomposition by
\beta
.

By construction, a
C
-indexed partial homomorphism
\widetilde{\varphi}\colon C\rightarrow[A,B]
corresponds to a
C
-measuring
\varphi\colon C\otimes A\rightarrow B
.

Definition 16
(universal measuring coalgebra)

Let
A
and
B
be
F
-algebras. We define the category of measurings
\mathscr{M}(A,B)
from
A
to
B
to be the category whose objects are pairs
(C;\widetilde{\varphi})
of an
F
-coalgebra
C
and a
C
-indexed partial homomorphism
\widetilde{\varphi}\colon C\rightarrow[A,B]
(or equivalently a measuring
\varphi\colon C\otimes A\to B
), and whose morphisms
(C;\widetilde{\varphi})\to(D;\widetilde{\psi})
are coalgebra homomorphisms
\gamma\colon C\to D
such that the following diagram commutes

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces D}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{[A,B].}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\gamma}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widetilde{\varphi}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widetilde{\psi}}

The
universal measuring coalgebra from
A
to
B
, denoted
(\lx@glossaries@gls@link{main}{UnivFMeasuring}{{{}}{\underline{\mathrm{Alg}}\lx@text@underline{\mathrm{Alg}}}(A,B)},\widetilde{\mathrm{ev}})
, is the terminal object (if it exists) in
\mathscr{M}(A,B)
.
This means given an
C
-indexed partial homomorphisms
\widetilde{\varphi}\colon C\rightarrow[A,B]
, there exists a unique coalgebra homomorphism
u_{\varphi}\colon C\rightarrow{\underline{\mathrm{Alg}}}({A},{B})
such that the following diagram commutes

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{[A,B]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\underline{\mathrm{Alg}}}(A,B).}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widetilde{\varphi}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\exists!u_{\varphi}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widetilde{\mathrm{ev}}}

The construction is natural in
A
and
B
and defines a functor:

{\underline{\mathrm{Alg}}}(-,-)\colon\mathrm{Alg}^{\mathrm{op}}\times\mathrm{Alg}\longrightarrow\mathrm{coAlg}.

If a universal measuring
({\underline{\mathrm{Alg}}}(A,B),\mathrm{ev})
exists, then we obtain a representation
{\underline{\mathrm{Alg}}}(A,B)
for the functor
\mu_{-}(A,B)
. That is, we have the following identification, natural in
C,A,B
:

\mathrm{Alg}(A,[C,B])\cong\mu_{C}(A,B)\cong{\mathrm{coAlg}}(C,{\underline{\mathrm{Alg}}}(A,B)).

In the following section, we show that if
\mathscr{C}
is closed and locally presentable and
F
is accessible, then the universal measuring coalgebra always exists.

4.4
Local Presentability and the Existence Theorem

We will now usually require that
\mathscr{C}
be
locally presentable
and
F
be
accessible

(
Adámek and Rosický, 1994
, Def. 1.17 & 2.17)
. Then
{\mathrm{Alg}}
and
{\mathrm{coAlg}}
are also locally presentable, the forgetful functor
{\mathrm{Alg}}\to\mathscr{C}
has a left adjoint
\mathrm{Fr}
, and the forgetful functor
{\mathrm{coAlg}}\to\mathscr{C}
has a right adjoint
\mathrm{Cof}

(
Adámek and Rosický, 1994
, Cor. 2.75 & Ex. 2.j)
. We will also use that these categories, as locally presentable categories, are complete and cocomplete.

Example 23

\mathrm{Set}
is locally presentable and
\mathrm{id}+1
is accessible, as it preserves all filtered colimits (in fact all connected colimits).

Example 24

The following endofunctors on a locally presentable, symmetric monoidal category
\mathscr{C}
are always accessible and lax symmetric monoidal.

(\mathrm{id})
:

The identity endofunctor
\mathrm{id}_{\mathscr{C}}
has a trivial lax symmetric monoidal structure and preserves filtered colimits.

(A)
:

Given a commutative monoid
(A,\eta,\nabla)\in\mathscr{C}
, the constant endofunctor that sends each object to
A
has a lax symmetric monoidal structure. Indeed,
\eta
and
\nabla
are exactly the structure required for this functor to be lax symmetric monoidal. This preserves filtered colimits.

(GF)
:

Suppose that
(F,\eta_{F},\nabla_{F})
and
(G,\eta_{G},\nabla_{G})
are lax symmetric monoidal endofunctors on
\mathscr{C}
. Then so is the composition
GF
. The map
\eta
is the composition
\mathbb{I}\xrightarrow{\eta_{G}}G(\mathbb{I})\xrightarrow{G\eta_{F}}GF(\mathbb{I})
. The map
\nabla
is the composition
GF(X)\otimes GF(Y)\xrightarrow{\nabla_{G}}G(F(X)\otimes F(Y))\xrightarrow{G\nabla_{F}}GF(X\otimes Y)
. If
F
and
G
both preserved filtered colimits, then so does
GF
.

(F\otimes G)
:

Suppose that
\mathscr{C}
is closed and that
(F,\eta_{F},\nabla_{F})
and
(G,\eta_{G},\nabla_{G})
are lax symmetric monoidal endofunctors on
\mathscr{C}
. Then so is
F\otimes G
, by which we mean the functor that takes
X\mapsto FX\otimes GX
. The map
\eta
is the composition
\mathbb{I}\to\mathbb{I}\otimes\mathbb{I}\xrightarrow{\eta_{F}\otimes\eta_{G}}(F\otimes G)(\mathbb{I})
. The map
\nabla
is the composition
(F\otimes G)(X)\otimes(F\otimes G)(Y)\cong(FX\otimes FY)\otimes(GX\otimes GY)\xrightarrow{\nabla_{F}\otimes\nabla_{G}}(F\otimes G)(X\otimes Y)
. If
F
and
G
are accessible, then so is
F\otimes G
(because
\otimes
preserves colimits in each variable, as
\mathscr{C}
is closed).

(F+G)
:

Suppose
\mathscr{C}
is closed and that
(F,\eta_{F},\nabla_{F})
is a lax symmetric monoidal endofunctor, and consider a pair
(G,\nabla_{G})
of an endofunctor
G\colon\mathscr{C}\to\mathscr{C}
and transformation
GX\otimes GY\to G(X\otimes Y)
which is associative and symmetric (i.e. a lax symmetric monoidal endofunctor but without
\eta
), and suppose that
G
is a
module
over
F
, meaning that there is a natural transformation
GX\otimes FY\to G(X\otimes Y)
satisfying the diagrams of
(
Yetter, 2003
, Def. 39)
, where the concept of module over a monoidal functor is defined. Then
F+G
is a lax symmetric monoidal functor, where
F+G
takes
X
to
FX+GX
. We set
\eta
to be the composition
\mathbb{I}\xrightarrow{\eta_{F}}F(\mathbb{I})\rightarrow F(\mathbb{I})+G(\mathbb{I})\cong(F+G)(\mathbb{I})
. The map
\nabla
is given by the following composition

\displaystyle(F+G)(X)\otimes(F+G)(Y)

\displaystyle\cong\

\displaystyle(FX\otimes FY)+(FX\otimes GY)+(GX\otimes FY)+(GX\otimes GY)

\displaystyle\xrightarrow{\langle\nabla_{F},\lambda,\rho,\nabla_{G}\rangle}\

\displaystyle F(X\otimes Y)+G(X\otimes Y)

\displaystyle\cong\

\displaystyle(F+G)(X\otimes Y).

If
F
and
G
preserve filtered colimits, then so does
F+G
.

(\mathrm{id}^{A})
:

Suppose that
\mathscr{C}
is cartesian (so we denote it
(\mathscr{C},1,\times)
). Consider an exponentiable object
A
of
\mathscr{C}
, meaning that the functor
\mathrm{id}\times A
has a right adjoint, denoted
\mathrm{id}^{A}
. This has a lax symmetric monoidal structure. We let
1\to 1^{A}
and
X^{A}\times Y^{A}\to(X\times Y)^{A}
be the isomorphisms we get from the fact that
-^{A}
, as a right adjoint, preserves limits. It is accessible as a right adjoint.

(
W
-types):

Consider the cartesian monoidal structure on
\mathrm{Set}
. Consider a commutative monoid
C\in\mathrm{Set}
, which can be considered as a commutative monoidal discrete category, and a function
f\colon A\to C
such that the preimage functor
f^{-1}\colon C\to\mathrm{Set}
has an oplax symmetric monoidal structure. Then the polynomial endofunctor (
Definition

10
)
\mathcal{P}_{f}\colon\mathrm{Set}\to\mathrm{Set}
has a lax symmetric monoidal structure. The elements of
\mathcal{P}_{f}X
are pairs
(c\in C,g\colon f^{-1}c\to X)
. The natural transformation
\nabla_{X,Y}\colon\mathcal{P}_{f}X\times\mathcal{P}_{f}Y\to\mathcal{P}_{f}(X\times Y)
takes a pair
((c,g\colon f^{-1}c\to X),(d,h\colon f^{-1}d\to X))
to
(cd,(g\times h)\circ\Lambda_{c,d}\colon f^{-1}(cd)\to X\times Y)
where
\Lambda_{c,d}\colon f^{-1}(cd)\to f^{-1}c\times f^{-1}d
is part of the oplax monoidal structure of
f^{-1}
. The natural transformation
\varepsilon\colon 1\to\mathcal{P}_{f}1\cong C
is the unit of the monoidal structure on
C
. This polynomial endofunctor is accessible since it is the composition of left and right adjoints.

(Discrete equational systems):

Suppose that the monoidal structure on
\mathscr{C}
is cocartesian, so we denote it by
(\mathscr{C},0,+)
, and suppose also that
\mathscr{C}
also has binary products that preserve filtered colimits in each variable. Consider a
discrete equational system

Leinster (2011)
on
\mathscr{C}
, that is, a pair
(A,M)
where
A
is a set and
M
is an object of
\mathscr{C}^{A\times A}
. The functor category
\mathscr{C}^{A}
inherits a cocartesian monoidal structure
(\mathscr{C}^{A},0,+)
pointwise. Define an endofunctor
M\otimes-\colon\mathscr{C}^{A}\to\mathscr{C}^{A}
by setting

(M\otimes X)_{a}\coloneqq\sum_{b\in A}M_{b,a}\times X_{b}.

This is lax symmetric monoidal. The morphisms
\eta\colon 0\to M\otimes 0
and
\nabla\colon M\otimes(X+Y)\to M\otimes X+M\otimes Y
are induced by the universal properties of
0
and
+
. It is also accessible since the coproducts and binary products preserve filtered colimits.

If
\mathscr{C}
is locally presentable and
F
is accessible, then given a coalgebra
(C,\chi)
and algebras
(A,\alpha)
and
(B,\beta)
, a map
\widetilde{\varphi}\colon C\rightarrow[C,A]
in
\mathscr{C}
uniquely determines a coalgebra homomorphism
\overline{\varphi}\colon C\rightarrow\mathrm{Cof}([A,B])
. A map
\widetilde{\varphi}\colon C\rightarrow[A,B]
is a
C
-indexed partial homomorphism if and only if both composites from
(C,\chi_{C})
to
\mathrm{Cof}\big(\underline{\hom}_{\mathscr{C}}(FA,B)\big)
in the following diagram coincide:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(C,\chi_{C})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{Cof}\big([A,B]\big)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{Cof}\big([FA,B]\big).}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overline{\varphi}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Phi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Psi}

The morphism
\Phi
is the cofree coalgebra homomorphism induced by the map
\alpha^{*}\colon[A,B]\rightarrow[FA,B]
in
\mathscr{C}
.
The morphism
\Psi
is the adjunct under the cofree-forgetful adjunction of the following composite:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{Cof}([A,B])}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F\big(\mathrm{Cof}([A,B])\big)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F\big([A,B]\big)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{[FA,FB]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{[FA,B]}.}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\chi_{\mathrm{Cof}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F(\varepsilon)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widetilde{\nabla}_{A,B}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\beta_{*}}

Here
\chi_{\mathrm{Cof}}
is the coalgebraic structure on the cofree coalgebra, and
\varepsilon
is the counit of the cofree-forgetful adjunction.

Now we can use this to guarantee the existence of a universal measuring.

Theorem 4.1

Suppose that
\mathscr{C}
is a symmetric monoidal closed category that is locally presentable, and that
F\colon\mathscr{C}\rightarrow\mathscr{C}
is a symmetric lax monoidal, accessible endofunctor.
Given
F
-algebras
A
and
B
, then the universal measuring coalgebra
({\underline{\mathrm{Alg}}}(A,B),\widetilde{\mathrm{ev}})
exists and is determined as the following equalizer diagram in
{\mathrm{coAlg}}
:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\underline{\mathrm{Alg}}}(A,B)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{Cof}\big([A,B]\big)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{Cof}\big([FA,B]\big).}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overline{\mathrm{ev}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Phi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Psi}

Proof

Since every
C
-indexed partial homomorphism
\widetilde{\varphi}\colon C\rightarrow[A,B]
is equivalent to a coalgebra homomorphism
C\rightarrow\mathrm{Cof}([A,B])
that coequalizes the parallel morphisms described above, we see that the category
\mathscr{M}(A,B)
of measurings from
A
to
B
is equivalent to the category of cones over the aforementioned parallel morphisms.
Thus they have equivalent terminal objects
{\underline{\mathrm{Alg}}}(A,B)
.

Definition 17
(dual (co)algebra)

Suppose that
\mathscr{C}
is a symmetric monoidal closed category that is locally presentable, and that
F\colon\mathscr{C}\rightarrow\mathscr{C}
is a symmetric lax monoidal, accessible endofunctor.
The category
{\mathrm{Alg}}
has an initial object which we denote by
N
.

Let
(-)^{*}\colon{\mathrm{coAlg}}^{\mathrm{op}}\rightarrow{\mathrm{Alg}}
denote the functor
[-,N]
, and call
C^{*}
the
dual algebra of
C
for any coalgebra
C
.

Let
(-)^{\circ}\colon{\mathrm{Alg}}^{\mathrm{op}}\rightarrow{\mathrm{coAlg}}
denote the functor
{\underline{\mathrm{Alg}}}(-,N)
, and call
A^{\circ}
the
dual coalgebra of
A
for any algebra
A
.

These functors form a dual adjunction in the sense that
we obtain natural identifications for all
C\in\mathrm{coAlg}
and
A\in\mathrm{Alg}
:

{\mathrm{Alg}}(A,C^{*})\cong{\mathrm{coAlg}}(C,A^{\circ}).

4.5
The Measuring Tensor

We now provide the last representation of the measuring functor.

Definition 18
(measuring tensor)

Let
C
be an
F
-coalgebra and
A
an
F
-algebra.
We define
\mathscr{M}_{C}(A)
to be the category whose objects consist of pairs
(B,\varphi)
where
B
is an
F
-algebra, and
\varphi\colon C\otimes A\rightarrow B
is a
C
-measuring from
A
to
B
.
A morphism
(B_{1},\varphi)\rightarrow(B_{2},\psi)
in
\mathscr{M}_{C}(A)
consists of an algebra homomorphism
B_{1}\rightarrow B_{2}
fitting in the commutative diagram in
\mathscr{C}
:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C\otimes A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B_{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B_{2}.}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\psi}

If it exists, we denote by
(C\triangleright A,u)
the initial object of
\mathscr{M}_{C}(A)
. The
F
-algebra
C⊳A
is called the
measuring tensor of
C
with
A
, and its measuring
u\colon C\otimes A\rightarrow C\triangleright A
is the universal
C
-measuring from
A
.
In other words, given any
C
-measuring
\varphi\colon C\otimes A\rightarrow B
, there exists a unique algebra homomorphism
!\colon C\triangleright A\rightarrow B
such that the following diagram commutes in
\mathscr{C}
:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C\otimes A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C\triangleright A.}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces u}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces!}

The construction is natural in
C
and
A
and thus determines a functor

-\triangleright-\colon\mathrm{coAlg}\times\mathrm{Alg}\longrightarrow\mathrm{Alg}.

The existence of the measuring tensor determines a natural identification

\mu_{C}(A,B)\cong{\mathrm{Alg}}(C\triangleright A,B).

That is, the functor
\mu_{C}(A,-)\colon{\mathrm{Alg}}\to\mathrm{Set}
is represented by
C\triangleright A
.

We can prove the existence of the measuring tensor
C\triangleright A
using a dual argument to the one used to prove the existence of the universal measuring coalgebra
{\underline{\mathrm{Alg}}}(A,B)
.
If
\mathscr{C}
is locally presentable and
F
is accessible, then for a coalgebra
(C,\chi)
, and algebras
(A,\alpha)
and
(B,\beta)
, a map
\varphi\colon C\otimes A\rightarrow B
uniquely determines an algebra homomorphism
\varphi^{\prime}\colon\mathrm{Fr}(C\otimes A)\rightarrow(B,\beta)
.
Notice then that a map
\varphi\colon C\otimes A\rightarrow B
is a measuring if and only if both composites from
\mathrm{Fr}(C\otimes FA)
to
(B,\beta)
coincide in the following diagram:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{Fr}(C\otimes FA)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{Fr}(C\otimes A)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(B,\beta).}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Phi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Psi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi^{\prime}}

The morphism
\Phi
is the free map induced by
\mathrm{id}\otimes\alpha\colon C\otimes FA\rightarrow C\otimes A
.
The morphism
\Psi
is the adjunct under the free-forgetful adjunction of the composition

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C\otimes FA}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces FC\otimes FA}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F(C\otimes A)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F(\mathrm{Fr}(C\otimes A))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{Fr}(C\otimes A),}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\chi\otimes\mathrm{id}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nabla_{C,A}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F(i)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha_{\mathrm{Fr}}}

in which
i
is the unit of the free-forgetful adjunction and
\alpha_{\mathrm{Fr}}
is the algebra structure on the free algebra
\mathrm{Fr}(C\otimes A)
.
Then we see that the category
\mathscr{M}_{C}(A)
is equivalent to the category of cocones over the parallel morphisms described above.
We have now shown the following.

Theorem 4.2

Suppose that
\mathscr{C}
symmetric monoidal closed category that is locally presentable, and that
F\colon\mathscr{C}\rightarrow\mathscr{C}
is an accessible endofunctor.
Consider an
F
-coalgebra
C
and an
F
-algebra
A
.
The measuring tensor
(C\triangleright A,u)
exists and is determined as the following coequalizer in
{\mathrm{Alg}}
:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{Fr}(C\otimes FA)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{Fr}(C\otimes A)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C\triangleright A.}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Phi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Psi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces u^{\prime}}

Remark 7

The convolution algebra also provides an alternative characterization of the algebra
C\triangleright A
and coalgebra
{\underline{\mathrm{Alg}}}(A,B)
.
As limits in
{\mathrm{Alg}}
and colimits in
{\mathrm{coAlg}}
are determined in
\mathscr{C}

Adámek and Porst (2003)
and the internal hom
[-,-]\colon\mathscr{C}^{\mathrm{op}}\times\mathscr{C}\rightarrow\mathscr{C}
preserves limits, the functor
[-,-]\colon{\mathrm{coAlg}}^{\mathrm{op}}\times{\mathrm{Alg}}\rightarrow{\mathrm{Alg}}
also preserves limits.
Moreover, fixing a coalgebra
C
, the induced functor
[C,-]\colon{\mathrm{Alg}}\rightarrow{\mathrm{Alg}}
is accessible since filtered colimits in
{\mathrm{Alg}}
are computed in
\mathscr{C}
(see
(
Adámek and Porst, 2003
, 5.6)
).
Therefore, by the adjoint functor theorem
(
Adámek and Rosický, 1994
, 1.66)
, the functor
[C,-]
is a right adjoint. Its left adjoint is precisely
C\triangleright-\colon{\mathrm{Alg}}\rightarrow{\mathrm{Alg}}
. Indeed, for any algebras
A
and
B
, we obtain the following bijection, natural in
C,A,B
.

{\mathrm{Alg}}\big(C\triangleright A,B\big)\cong{\mathrm{Alg}}\big(A,[C,B]\big).

Notice we can also determine the universal measuring coalgebra by using the adjoint functors.
Fixing now an algebra
B
, the opposite functor
[-,B]^{\mathrm{op}}\colon{\mathrm{coAlg}}\rightarrow{\mathrm{Alg}}^{\mathrm{op}}
preserves colimits, where the domain is locally presentable and the codomain is essentially locally small. By the adjoint functor theorem
(
Adámek and Rosický, 1994
, 1.66)
, this functor is a left adjoint.
Its right adjoint is precisely the functor
{\underline{\mathrm{Alg}}}(-,B)\colon{\mathrm{Alg}}^{\mathrm{op}}\rightarrow{\mathrm{coAlg}}
. Indeed, for any algebra
A
and
B
and any coalgebra
C
, we have the following bijection, natural in
C,A,B
:

\displaystyle{\mathrm{coAlg}}\big(C,{\underline{\mathrm{Alg}}}(A,B)\big)\cong{\mathrm{Alg}}\big(A,[C,B]\big).

Combining the identifications, we see that the measuring functor is representable in each factor:

\displaystyle\mu_{C}(A,B)\cong{\mathrm{coAlg}}\big(C,{\underline{\mathrm{Alg}}}(A,B)\big)\cong{\mathrm{Alg}}\big(A,[C,B]\big)\cong{\mathrm{Alg}}\big(C\triangleright A,B\big).

(4.5.1)

In other words, for any algebra
A
and
B
and any coalgebra
C
, the following data are equivalent.

C\otimes A\rightarrow B

C\rightarrow[A,B]

C\rightarrow{\underline{\mathrm{Alg}}}(A,B)

A\rightarrow[C,B]

C\triangleright A\rightarrow B

measuring

partial

coalgebra

algebra

algebra

homomorphism

homomorphism

homomorphism

homomorphism

4.6
Interlude: Calculations Involving Preinitial Algebras and Subterminal Coalgebras

In this section, we take a brief interlude to develop some theory and examples around preinitial algebras and subterminal coalgebras. These provide good examples with which to work out the theory: calculations involving them are both feasible and illuminating.

In some sense, the initial algebra is the ‘nicest’ algebra, and the terminal coalgebra is the ‘nicest’ coalgebra. But these are a bit too nice and so do not reveal much about the theory. Here, we take one step further into the world of (co)algebras by considering
quotients
of the initial algebra, and
subobjects
of the terminal coalgebra.

The reader who wants to read about the general theory before embarking on understanding these examples can skip to the next section,
Sect.

4.7
.

Definition 19
(preinitial algebra, subterminal coalgebra)

Say that an algebra
A
is
preinitial
if the universal map
I\to A
from the initial algebra
I
is an epimorphism (in
{\mathrm{Alg}}
). Dually, a coalgebra
C
is
subterminal
if the universal map
C\to T
to the terminal coalgebra
C
is a monomorphism (in
{\mathrm{coAlg}}
).

Now we establish recognition theorems for preinitial algebras and subterminal coalgebras.

Proposition 2

Consider a category
\mathscr{C}
, an endofunctor
F\colon\mathscr{C}\to\mathscr{C}
, and an algebra
(A,\alpha)
of
F
.

1.

Given a quotient object
Q
of
A
for which
\alpha
restricts to a map
\alpha_{Q}\colon FQ\to Q
, then the pair
(Q,\alpha_{Q})
is a quotient object of
(A,\alpha)
.

2.

If the forgetful functor
\mathrm{Alg}\to\mathscr{C}
preserves epimorphisms, then for every quotient object
(Q,\alpha_{Q})
of
(A,\alpha)
,
Q
is a quotient object of
A
.

Thus, if the forgetful functor preserves epimorphisms, it induces an isomorphism between the poset of quotient algebras of
(A,\alpha_{A})
and the poset of quotient objects
Q
of
A
for which
\alpha
restricts to a morphism
\alpha_{Q}\colon FQ\to Q
.

Proof

For (1), asking that
\alpha
restricts to a map
\alpha_{Q}
is exactly asking that
(Q,\alpha_{Q})
is an algebra of
F
and that the quotient map constitutes a total algebra homomorphism
q\colon(A,\alpha)\to(Q,\alpha_{Q})
.
Since the forgetful functor
\mathrm{Alg}\to\mathscr{C}
is faithful, it reflects epimorphisms. The second statement (2) is immediate.

Example 25

Consider the endofunctor
\mathrm{id}+1
on
\mathrm{Set}
. Since the forgetful functor
\mathrm{Alg}\to\mathscr{C}
preserves epimorphisms, there is an isomorphism between the poset of quotient algebras of an algebra
(A,\alpha)
and the poset of quotient sets
Q
of
A
for which
\alpha
restricts to a morphism
\alpha_{Q}\colon FQ\to Q
.
Thus, nontrivial preinitial algebras include those of the form

{\mathbb{n}}\coloneqq(\{0,1,...,n\},\alpha_{\mathbb{n}})
for any
n\in\mathbb{N}
where
\alpha_{\mathbb{n}}
is the algebra structure that
\{0,1,...,n\}
inherits as the quotient of
\mathbb{N}
in
\mathrm{Set}
that identifies all
m\geq n
.

Definition 20
(index)

Consider a category
\mathscr{C}
with a terminal object
1
, an endofunctor
F\colon\mathscr{C}\to\mathscr{C}
, and suppose that the category of coalgebras of
F
has a terminal coalgebra
T
.

•

Call a point
1\to T
an
index
. For any index
n
, let
{\mathrm{coAlg}}^{n}
denote the category whose objects are pairs
((X,\chi_{X}),x)
where
(X,\chi_{X})
is a coalgebra and
x\colon 1\to X
such that
\llbracket{x}\rrbracket=n
, using the notation of
Example

16
.

•

Say that a coalgebra is
freely generated by a point of index
n
if it is an initial object in
{\mathrm{coAlg}}^{n}
. Say that a coalgebra is
generated by a point of index
n
if it is the vertex of a cone on the identity endofunctor on
{\mathrm{coAlg}}^{n}
.

Definition 21
(well-equipped)

Say that
F\colon\mathscr{C}\to\mathscr{C}
is
well-equipped
if
\mathscr{C}
is well-pointed and for every index
n
, there is a coalgebra generated by a point of index
n
.

Proposition 3

Consider a category
\mathscr{C}
, an endofunctor
F
, and a coalgebra
(C,\chi)
.

1.

Given a subobject
S
of
C
for which
\chi
restricts to a morphism
\chi_{S}\colon S\to FS
, the pair
(S,\chi_{S})
is a subobject of
(C,\chi)
.

2.

If
F
is well-equipped, then for every subcoalgebra
(S,\chi_{S})
of
(C,\chi)
, we have that
S
is a subobject of
C
.

Thus, if
F
is well-equipped, there is an isomorphism between the poset of subobjects of
(C,\chi_{C})
and the poset of subobjects of
C
for which
\chi_{C}
restricts to a morphism
\chi_{S}\colon FS\to S
.

Proof

For (1), consider
\iota\colon S\rightarrowtail C
. Asking that
\chi
restricts to a morphism
\chi_{S}
means exactly that
\iota
constitutes a total algebra homomorphism
\iota\colon(S,\chi_{C})\to(C,\chi)
. This is a monomorphism since the forgetful functor
U\colon\mathrm{Alg}\to\mathscr{C}
is faithful and thus reflects monomorphisms.

For (2), consider a monomorphism
\iota\colon(S,\chi_{S})\rightarrowtail(C,\chi)
. In order to show that
\iota\colon S\to C
is also a monomorphism, consider two points
x,y\colon 1\to S
in
\mathscr{C}
such that
\iota x=\iota y
. Since
\mathscr{C}
is assumed to be well-pointed, it suffices to show that
x=y
. Note that
\llbracket{x}\rrbracket=\llbracket{\iota x}\rrbracket=\llbracket{\iota y}\rrbracket=\llbracket{y}\rrbracket
, and let
((I,\chi_{I}),\top_{n})
denote a coalgebra generated by a point of this index. Then we get two maps
\overline{x},\overline{y}\colon(I,\chi_{I})\to(S,\chi_{S})
and a map
\overline{\iota x}=\overline{\iota y}\colon(I,\chi_{I})\to(C,\chi)
such that
\iota\overline{x}=\overline{\iota x}=\overline{\iota y}=\iota\overline{y}
. Since
\iota
is a monomorphism,
\overline{x}=\overline{y}
, but then
x=\overline{x}\top_{n}=\overline{y}\top_{n}=y\colon*\to\mathscr{C}
.

Example 26

Consider the endofunctor
\mathrm{id}+1
on
\mathrm{Set}
. The nontrivial subsets of
{\mathbb{N}_{\infty}}
that are closed under the coalgebra morphism are
\{0,1,...,n\}
,
\{\infty\}
,
\mathbb{N}
(and unions of these); let
\lx@glossaries@gls@link{main}{subterm1}{{{}}{\mathbb{n}^{\circ}}},\mathbb{I},\lx@glossaries@gls@link{main}{subterm2}{{{}}\mathbb{N}^{c}}
denote the corresponding subterminal coalgebras. By the preceding proposition, these are the generating sub-coalgebras of
{\mathbb{N}_{\infty}}
.
The indices are the elements of
{\mathbb{N}_{\infty}}
. Each coalgebra
{\mathbb{n}^{\circ}}
is freely generated by a point of index
n
for finite
n
, and
\mathbb{I}
is freely generated by a point of index
\infty
.

Now, we can use preinitial algebras and terminal coalgebras of the endofunctor
1+\mathrm{id}
on
\mathrm{Set}
to calculate some measurings.

Example 27

Consider algebras
A
and
B
of the endofunctor
1+\mathrm{id}
on
\mathrm{Set}
.
Suppose that
A
is preinitial, so that in particular every element of
A
is of the form
0_{A}
or
a+1
.
If we want to construct an algebra homomorphism
f\colon A\to B
, we can proceed by induction. First, we send
0_{A}\mapsto 0_{B}
. Next, we can try to send
1_{A}\mapsto 1_{B}
(where
-_{A}
denotes the algebra morphism
\mathbb{N}\to A
). Note that this only works if
1_{A}
is different from
0_{A}
or if
0_{B}=1_{B}
. If this works, we can try to continue inductively, sending
n_{A}\mapsto n_{B}
. If this procedure succeeds, then we will have constructed an algebra homomorphism
f\colon A\to B
by induction.

We can turn this construction into a sequence of functions that we define in the following way.

Initial step (P1):

Define
f_{0}\colon A\rightarrow B
by
f_{0}(a)\coloneqq 0_{B}
for all
a\in A
.

Inductive step:

Define
f_{c+1}\colon A\rightarrow B
by:

(P2):

f_{c+1}(0_{A})\coloneqq 0_{B}
;

(P3):

f_{c+1}(a+1)\coloneqq f_{c}(a)+1
.

If we have defined
f_{c}
for all
c\in\mathbb{N}
, then we will say that we have defined an
\infty
-partial homomorphism
. Note that in this case, one can then form a total algebra homomorphism which we will denote
f_{\infty}
.
Otherwise, if we have only defined
f_{c}
for all
c\in\{0,...,n\}
, we will say that we have defined an
n
-partial homomorphism
.
Now compare this induction with the definition of measuring (
Example

22
).
There is a measuring from
A
to
B
by
{\mathbb{n}^{\circ}}
if the induction above creates an
n
-partial homomorphism, and in this case the functions of the form
f_{c}
constructed are the same as those specified in
Example

22
.

If we denote
\mathrm{Alg}_{n}(A,B)\coloneqq\mu_{\mathbb{n}^{\circ}}(A,B)\cong\mathrm{coAlg}(\mathbb{n}^{\circ},{\underline{\mathrm{Alg}}}(A,B))
the set of all
\mathbb{n}^{\circ}
-measurings from
A
to
B
, the natural inclusions
\mathbb{n}^{\circ}\hookrightarrow(\mathbb{n}+1)^{\circ}
are homomorphisms of coalgebras, so after applying the functor
\mathrm{coAlg}(-,{\underline{\mathrm{Alg}}}(A,B))\colon\mathrm{coAlg}^{\mathrm{op}}\rightarrow\mathrm{Set}
, we obtain a tower

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{Alg}_{\infty}(A,B)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdots}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{Alg}_{2}(A,B)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{Alg}_{1}(A,B)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{Alg}_{0}(A,B),}

whose limit is
\mathrm{Alg}_{\infty}(A,B)\coloneqq\mathrm{coAlg}(\mathbb{N}^{c},\mathrm{Alg}(A,B))
as the right adjoint functor
\mathrm{coAlg}(-,{\underline{\mathrm{Alg}}}(A,B))
sends colimit in
\mathrm{coAlg}
to a limit in
\mathrm{Set}
, and the colimit the coalgebra homomorphisms
\mathbb{n}^{\circ}\hookrightarrow(\mathbb{n}+1)^{\circ}
is precisely the coalgebra
\mathbb{N}^{c}
.
Our construction above of
f_{\infty}
precisely builds, layer by layer in the tower, an element of
\mathrm{Alg}_{\infty}(A,B)
.

There is a measuring by
\mathbb{N}^{c}
if the induction never fails, and again the functions
f_{c}
from the induction above and
Example

22
coincide. Now, note that exhibiting a measuring by
{\mathbb{N}_{\infty}}
amounts to exhibiting a measuring by
\mathbb{N}^{c}
together with a total algebra homomorphism
f_{\infty}
. For such an
A
, then, exhibiting a measuring by
\mathbb{N}^{c}
is (logically) equivalent to exhibiting one by
{\mathbb{N}_{\infty}}
.

Now, we show that the universal measuring coalgebra from
A
to
B
is subterminal and easy to calculate if
A
is preinitial.

Proposition 4

Consider two algebras
A,B
such that
A
is preinitial. Then
{\underline{\mathrm{Alg}}}(A,B)
is a subterminal coalgebra, and it is the maximum subterminal coalgebra
C
such that
{\mathrm{Alg}}(A,[C,B])
is nonempty.

Proof

We first claim that
{\mathrm{Alg}}(A,[C,B])
is empty or a singleton for any coalgebra
C
.
Since
!_{A}\colon\mathbb{N}\to A
is an epimorphism, the function

{\mathrm{Alg}}(A,[C,B])\xrightarrow{!^{*}_{A}}{\mathrm{Alg}}(\mathbb{N},[C,B])

is an injection, and since
\mathbb{N}
is initial,
{\mathrm{Alg}}(\mathbb{N},[C,B])\cong*
.
Thus,
{\mathrm{Alg}}(A,[C,B])
is a singleton, in the case where the only map
\mathbb{N}\to[C,B]
factors through
A
, and otherwise is empty.

We now claim that the canonical map
\llbracket{-}\rrbracket\colon{\underline{\mathrm{Alg}}}(A,B)\to{\mathbb{N}_{\infty}}
is a monomorphism. Suppose there are maps
a,b\colon C\to{\underline{\mathrm{Alg}}}(A,B)
from a coalgebra
C
such that
\llbracket{a}\rrbracket=\llbracket{b}\rrbracket
. Recall that the coalgebra
{\underline{\mathrm{Alg}}}(A,B)
is defined by the following universal property for any coalgebra
C

{\mathrm{coAlg}}(C,{\underline{\mathrm{Alg}}}(A,B))\cong{\mathrm{Alg}}(A,[C,B]).

Thus,
{\mathrm{coAlg}}(C,{\underline{\mathrm{Alg}}}(A,B))
is a singleton, so
a=b
, and
\llbracket{-}\rrbracket
is monic.

Example 28

Again, consider algebras
A
and
B
of the endofunctor
1+\mathrm{id}
on
\mathrm{Set}
where
A
is preinitial.
In this case, the universal measuring is a subterminal coalgebra by the above. If the induction of
Example

27
succeeds in constructing
f_{i}
for
i\leq n
but fails to construct
f_{n+1}
, then the maximum subterminal coalgebra that measures
A\to B
is
{\mathbb{n}^{\circ}}
, so this is the universal measuring. And if the induction of
Example

27
creates an
\infty
-partial homomorphism, i.e., a total homomorphism, then the maximum subterminal coalgebra that measures
A
to
B
is
{\mathbb{N}_{\infty}}
itself, so this is the universal measuring.
We will also show this fact more directly (i.e., without reference to
Proposition

4
) below.

Example 29

We compute
{\underline{\mathrm{Alg}}}({\mathbb{n}},B)
for any algebra
B
of
1+\mathrm{id}
using
Eq.

4.5.1
.
We first observe the following for any algebra
Z
:

{\mathrm{Alg}}({\mathbb{n}},Z)\cong\begin{cases}*&\text{if }n_{Z}=(n+1)_{Z}\\
\emptyset&\text{otherwise}.\end{cases}

Since we are considering
Z\coloneqq[C,B]
, we need to understand when
n_{[C,B]}=(n+1)_{[C,B]}
. By definition,
0_{[C,A]}
is the constant function at
0_{A}
. Then
1_{[C,A]}
is the function that takes every
c\in C
of index
0
to
0_{B}
, and every other
c\in C
to
1_{B}
. Inductively, we can show that
n_{[C,B]}(c)=\min(\llbracket c\rrbracket,n)_{B}
.
Thus,
n_{[C,B]}=(n+1)_{[C,B]}
means that
\min(\llbracket c\rrbracket,n)_{B}=\min(\llbracket c\rrbracket,n+1)_{B}
for all
c\in C
, and this holds if and only if
\llbracket c\rrbracket\leq n
for all
c\in C
or
n_{B}=(n+1)_{B}
. Now we have the following:

\displaystyle{\mathrm{coAlg}}(C,{\underline{\mathrm{Alg}}}({\mathbb{n}},B))\cong{\mathrm{Alg}}({\mathbb{n}},[C,B])\cong\begin{cases}*&\text{if }\llbracket c\rrbracket\leq n\text{ for all }c\in C\\
*&\text{if }n_{B}=(n+1)_{B}\\
\emptyset&\text{otherwise.}\end{cases}

(4.6.1)

In the case that
n_{B}=(n+1)_{B}
, we find that
{\underline{\mathrm{Alg}}}({\mathbb{n}},B)
has the universal property of the terminal object,
{\mathbb{N}_{\infty}}
.
Now suppose that
n_{B}\neq(n+1)_{B}
. Since
{\mathrm{coAlg}}(C,{\mathbb{n}^{\circ}})=*
if and only if
\llbracket c\rrbracket\leq n
for all
c\in C
,
{\underline{\mathrm{Alg}}}({\mathbb{n}},B)
has the universal property of
{\mathbb{n}^{\circ}}
.

Now we have calculated the following

{\underline{\mathrm{Alg}}}({\mathbb{n}},B)=\begin{cases}{\mathbb{N}_{\infty}}\text{ if }n_{B}=(n+1)_{B}\\
{\mathbb{n}^{\circ}}\text{ otherwise.}\end{cases}

This aligns with our expectations, since there is a total homomorphism
{\mathbb{n}}\to B
if
n_{B}=(n+1)_{B}
but there is only an
n
-partial homomorphism
{\mathbb{n}}\to B
otherwise.
Finally, note that taking
B\coloneqq\mathbb{N}
, we have calculated
{\underline{\mathrm{Alg}}}({\mathbb{n}},\mathbb{N})
, the
dual
(
Definition

17
) of
{\mathbb{n}}
, to be
{\mathbb{n}^{\circ}}
.

4.7
Measuring as an Enrichment

We now come to the main punchline of the general theory presented in this paper: that
{\underline{\mathrm{Alg}}}(-,-)
gives the category of algebras an enrichment in coalgebras. First, we describe how to compose measurings.

Proposition 5

Let
\mathscr{C}
be a (symmetric) monoidal category.
Let
F\colon\mathscr{C}\rightarrow\mathscr{C}
be a lax (symmetric) monoidal functor.
Then the category
\mathrm{coAlg}
has a (symmetric) monoidal structure for which the forgetful functor
\mathrm{coAlg}\rightarrow\mathscr{C}
is strong (symmetric) monoidal.
Moreover, if
\mathscr{C}
is locally presentable and closed, and
F
is accessible, then
\mathrm{coAlg}
is also closed, with internal hom denoted
\underline{\mathrm{coAlg}}(-,-)
.

Proof

Suppose
(C,\chi_{C})
and
(D,\chi_{D})
are coalgebras. Then
C\otimes D
has the following coalgebra structure:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{C\otimes D}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{F(C)\otimes F(D)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{F(C\otimes D).}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\chi_{C}\otimes\chi_{D}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nabla_{C,D}}

The morphism
\eta\colon\mathbb{I}\rightarrow F(\mathbb{I})
provides the coalgebraic structure on
\mathbb{I}
.

To see that
({\mathrm{coAlg}},\otimes,(\mathbb{I},\eta))
is a symmetric monoidal category, we first need to check that
(C,\chi_{C})\otimes(\mathbb{I},\eta)\cong(C,\chi_{C})
. On objects, we have
C\otimes\mathbb{I}\cong C
as
\mathbb{I}
is a monoidal unit. The coalgebraic structures correspond as we have the following commutative diagram by unitality of
(F,\nabla,\eta)
:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C\otimes\mathbb{I}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F(C)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F(C)\otimes\mathbb{I}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F(C)\otimes F(\mathbb{I})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F(C\otimes\mathbb{I})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F(C).}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cong}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\chi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\chi\otimes\eta}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cong}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{id}\otimes\eta}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nabla}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cong}

Similarly, we have a natural isomorphism
(\mathbb{I},\eta)\otimes(C,\chi_{C})\cong(C,\chi_{C})
from the identification
\mathbb{I}\otimes C\cong C
.
Moreover, we also have the following natural isomorphism

((C,\chi)\otimes(C^{\prime},\chi^{\prime}))\otimes(C^{\prime\prime},\chi^{\prime\prime})\cong(C,\chi)\otimes((C^{\prime},\chi^{\prime})\otimes(C^{\prime\prime},\chi^{\prime\prime})),

lifted from
(C\otimes C^{\prime})\otimes C^{\prime\prime}\cong C\otimes(C^{\prime}\otimes C^{\prime\prime})
.
Lastly, the symmetry
C\otimes C^{\prime}\cong C^{\prime}\otimes C
lifts to
{\mathrm{coAlg}}
. Therefore as
\mathscr{C}
is symmetric monoidal, the above natural identifications assemble to a symmetric monoidal structure on
{\mathrm{coAlg}}
as the pentagon, triangle, hexagon and symmetric identities remain valid in
{\mathrm{coAlg}}
.

Suppose now that
\mathscr{C}
is locally presentable and closed, and
F
is accessible. Then for all objects
X\in\mathscr{C}
, we have that
X\otimes-\colon\mathscr{C}\rightarrow\mathscr{C}
is a left adjoint functor, with right adjoint given by its the internal hom
[X,-]\colon\mathscr{C}\rightarrow\mathscr{C}
.
Since
\mathrm{coAlg}
is also locally presentable and colimits are computed in
\mathscr{C}
, we get that for all
C\in\mathrm{coAlg}
, the induced functor
C\otimes-\colon\mathrm{coAlg}\rightarrow\mathrm{coAlg}
also preserves all colimits and thus must be a left adjoint as
\mathrm{coAlg}
is locally presentable.
We denote by
\underline{\mathrm{coAlg}}(C,-)\colon\mathrm{coAlg}\rightarrow\mathrm{coAlg}
its right adjoint. The construction is natural in
C
and thus defines the desired closed structure on
\mathrm{coAlg}
.

We can build explicitly the internal hom
\underline{\mathrm{coAlg}}(-,-)
of
\mathrm{coAlg}
as follows.
Given coalgebras
(C,\chi_{C})
and
(D,\chi_{D})
, the coalgebra

¯

coAlg

coAlg
(C,D)
is the following equalizer in
\mathrm{coAlg}
:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\underline{\mathrm{coAlg}}(C,D)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{Cof}([C,D])}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{Cof}([C,FD]).}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Phi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Psi}

The morphism
\Phi
is induced by the map
(\chi_{D})_{*}\colon[C,D]\to[C,FD]
in
\mathscr{C}
.
The morphism
\Psi
is induced by the forgetful-cofree adjunction on a morphism the following morphism:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{Cof}([C,D])}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F\mathrm{Cof}([C,D])}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F{[C,D]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{[FC,FD]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{[C,FD]}.}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\chi_{\mathrm{Cof}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F\varepsilon}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widetilde{\nabla}_{C,D}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\chi_{C}^{*}}

Now we can state and prove our main theorem.

Theorem 4.3

Suppose that
\mathscr{C}
is a locally presentable, symmetric monoidal closed category. Let
F\colon\mathscr{C}\to\mathscr{C}
be an accessible lax symmetric monoidal endofunctor.
Then the category
{\mathrm{Alg}}
is enriched, tensored, and cotensored over the closed symmetric monoidal category
{\mathrm{coAlg}}
respectively via

{\mathrm{Alg}}^{\mathrm{op}}\times{\mathrm{Alg}}\xrightarrow{{\underline{\mathrm{Alg}}}(-,-)}{\mathrm{coAlg}},\quad{\mathrm{coAlg}}\times{\mathrm{Alg}}\xrightarrow{-\triangleright-}{\mathrm{Alg}},\quad{\mathrm{coAlg}}^{\mathrm{op}}\times{\mathrm{Alg}}\xrightarrow{[-,-]}{\mathrm{Alg}}.

In other words, for any coalgebra
C
, and algebras
A
and
B
, we obtain the natural identifications in
\mathrm{coAlg}
:

\underline{\mathrm{coAlg}}(C,{\underline{\mathrm{Alg}}}(A,B))\cong{\underline{\mathrm{Alg}}}(C\triangleright A,B)\cong{\underline{\mathrm{Alg}}}(A,[C,B]).

Proof

The convolution algebra functor
[-,-]\colon{\mathrm{coAlg}}^{\mathrm{op}}\times{\mathrm{Alg}}\rightarrow{\mathrm{Alg}}
presents
{\mathrm{Alg}}
as a right module over
{\mathrm{coAlg}}^{\mathrm{op}}
.
Thus
{\mathrm{Alg}}^{\mathrm{op}}
is a left module over
{\mathrm{coAlg}}
via

[-,-]^{\mathrm{op}}\colon{\mathrm{coAlg}}\times{\mathrm{Alg}}^{\mathrm{op}}\rightarrow{\mathrm{Alg}}^{\mathrm{op}}.

Moreover, for any
A\in{\mathrm{Alg}}
, the functor
[-,B]^{\mathrm{op}}\colon{\mathrm{coAlg}}\rightarrow{\mathrm{Alg}}^{\mathrm{op}}

has a right adjoint
{\underline{\mathrm{Alg}}}(-,B)\colon{\mathrm{Alg}}^{\mathrm{op}}\rightarrow{\mathrm{coAlg}}
as shown in
Remark

7
.
Thus, by
(
Hyland et al., 2017
, 3.1)
, the category
{\mathrm{Alg}}^{\mathrm{op}}
is enriched over
{\mathrm{coAlg}}
with tensor
[-,-]^{\mathrm{op}}
.
Therefore
{\mathrm{Alg}}
is enriched over
{\mathrm{coAlg}}
with power
[-,-]
.

Remark 8

While
\mathrm{Alg}
is enriched in
\mathrm{coAlg}
, we cannot dualize our arguments to show that
\mathrm{coAlg}
is enriched in
\mathrm{Alg}
.
This is because if
\mathscr{C}
is locally presentable, then
\mathscr{C}^{\mathrm{op}}
is generally not locally presentable
(
Adámek and Rosický, 1994
, 1.64)
.
Instead, just as in
Remark

5
, the internal hom in
\mathrm{coAlg}
provides that
\mathrm{coAlg}
is enriched, tensored, and cotensored over itself. Given
F
-coalgebras
(C,\chi)
,
(C_{1},\chi_{1})
and
(C_{2},\chi_{2})
, we say a morphism
\widetilde{\varphi}\colon C\rightarrow[C_{1},C_{2}]
is a

C
-indexed partial homomorphism
if its adjunct
\varphi\colon C\otimes C_{1}\rightarrow C_{2}
is a homomorphism of coalgebras (which can be renamed as a
C
-measuring from
C_{1}
to
C_{2}
).
In other words, this means we obtain the commutative diagram in
\mathscr{C}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F(C)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F([C_{1},C_{2}])}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{[FC_{1},FC_{2}]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{[C_{1},C_{2}]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{[C_{1},FC_{2}]}.}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F(\widetilde{\varphi})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widetilde{\nabla}_{C_{1},C_{2}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\chi_{1}^{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\chi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widetilde{\varphi}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\chi_{2})_{*}}

Then the internal hom
\underline{\mathrm{coAlg}}(C_{1},C_{2})
can be viewed as the universal measuring coalgebra in the sense that given any
C
-indexed partial homomorphism
\widetilde{\varphi}\colon C\rightarrow[C_{1},C_{2}]
(or equivalently a measuring
\varphi\colon C\otimes C_{1}\rightarrow C_{2}
), there exists a unique coalgebra homomorphism
u_{\varphi}\colon C\rightarrow\underline{\mathrm{coAlg}}(C_{1},C_{2})
such that the diagram commutes:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{[C_{1},C_{2}]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\underline{\mathrm{coAlg}}(C_{1},C_{2}).}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widetilde{\varphi}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\exists!\,u_{\varphi}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widetilde{\mathrm{ev}}}

This is making precise the coalgebraic variant of
Theorem

4.3
: there is a natural identification in
\mathrm{coAlg}

\underline{\mathrm{coAlg}}(C,\underline{\mathrm{coAlg}}(C_{1},C_{2}))\cong\underline{\mathrm{coAlg}}(C\otimes C_{1},C_{2})\cong\underline{\mathrm{coAlg}}(C_{1},\underline{\mathrm{coAlg}}(C,C_{2})).

The last identification just signifies there is a symmetry in the coalgebraic case: a
C
-measuring
C\otimes C_{1}\rightarrow C_{2}
can be viewed as a
C_{1}
-measuring
C_{1}\otimes C\rightarrow C_{2}
.

Example 30

We can strengthen
Example

27
as follows.
Given algebras
A
and
B
over endofunctor
-+1
, denote by
{\underline{\mathrm{Alg}}}_{n}(A,B)
the coalgebra
\underline{\mathrm{coAlg}}(\mathbb{n}^{\circ},{\underline{\mathrm{Alg}}}(A,B))
.
The inclusions
\mathbb{n}^{\circ}\hookrightarrow(\mathbb{n}+1)^{\circ}
define a tower in
\mathrm{coAlg}
:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\underline{\mathrm{Alg}}}_{\infty}(A,B)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdots}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\underline{\mathrm{Alg}}}_{2}(A,B)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\underline{\mathrm{Alg}}}_{1}(A,B)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\underline{\mathrm{Alg}}}_{0}(A,B).}

Just as in
Example

27
, we compute that its limit is given by
{\underline{\mathrm{Alg}}}_{\infty}(A,B)\coloneqq\underline{\mathrm{coAlg}}(\mathbb{N}^{c},{\underline{\mathrm{Alg}}}(A,B))
.
The coalgebras
{\underline{\mathrm{Alg}}}_{n}(A,B)
should be considered as the universal measuring of
n
-partial homomorphisms, while
{\underline{\mathrm{Alg}}}_{\infty}(A,B)
is
the universal measuring of
\infty
-partial homomorphisms
: these are functions
f_{\infty}\colon A\rightarrow B
for which
f_{\infty}((n+1)_{A})=f_{\infty}(n_{A})+1
, and thus are equivalent to total algebra homomorphisms if
A
is preinitial.

Remark 9

The previous example suggests the following generalization.
Denote by
1
the terminal object of a locally presentable, symmetric monoidal closed category
\mathscr{C}
.
Let
F
be an accessible lax symmetric monoidal functor.
The unique map
!\colon F1\rightarrow 1
in
\mathscr{C}
determines an algebra structure on
1
.
In fact,
1
is also the terminal object in
\mathrm{Alg}
.
The induced map
F!\colon F^{2}1\rightarrow F1
defines also an algebra structure on
F1
such that the map
!\colon F1\rightarrow 1
is homomorphism of
F
-algebras.
More generally, we obtain algebra homomorphisms
F^{n}!\colon F^{n+1}1\rightarrow F^{n}1
for all
n\geq 0
.
We have successfully built a tower in
\mathrm{Alg}
:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdots}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F^{3}1}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F^{2}1}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F1}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 1,}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F^{2}!}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F!}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces!}

the same one that appeared in Adámek’s fixed point theorem (
Theorem

3.2
), but considered in the category
\mathrm{Alg}
instead of
\mathscr{C}
.
We now apply the dual coalgebra functor
(-)^{\circ}\colon\mathrm{Alg}^{\mathrm{op}}\rightarrow\mathrm{coAlg}
from
Definition

17
.
We obtain a filtered diagram in
\mathrm{coAlg}
:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 1^{\circ}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(F1)^{\circ}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(F^{2}1)^{\circ}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(F^{3}1)^{\circ}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdots.}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces!^{\circ}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F!^{\circ}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F^{2}!^{\circ}}

(4.7.1)

Denote by
C_{\infty}
the colimit of the above diagram in
\mathrm{coAlg}
(which is always computed in
\mathscr{C}
).
For all
n\geq 0
, given
F
-algebras
A
and
B
, we define the
n
-partial measuring coalgebra from
A
to
B
to be the coalgebra determined as:

{\underline{\mathrm{Alg}}}_{n}(A,B)\coloneqq\underline{\mathrm{coAlg}}((F^{n}1)^{\circ},{\underline{\mathrm{Alg}}}(A,B)).

The
\infty
-partial measuring coalgebra from
A
to
B
is the coalgebra defined as

{\underline{\mathrm{Alg}}}_{\infty}(A,B)\coloneqq\underline{\mathrm{coAlg}}(C_{\infty},{\underline{\mathrm{Alg}}}(A,B)).

If we apply the right adjoint functor
\underline{\mathrm{coAlg}}(-,{\underline{\mathrm{Alg}}}(A,B))\colon\mathrm{coAlg}^{\mathrm{op}}\to\mathrm{coAlg}
to the filtered diagram of
Eq.

4.7.1
, we obtain the tower in
\mathrm{coAlg}
:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\underline{\mathrm{Alg}}}_{\infty}(A,B)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdots}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\underline{\mathrm{Alg}}}_{2}(A,B)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\underline{\mathrm{Alg}}}_{1}(A,B)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\underline{\mathrm{Alg}}}_{0}(A,B).}

As right adjoints preserve limits, we indeed obtain that
{\underline{\mathrm{Alg}}}_{\infty}(A,B)
is the limit of the tower:

\displaystyle{\underline{\mathrm{Alg}}}_{\infty}(A,B)

\displaystyle:=\underline{\mathrm{coAlg}}(C_{\infty},{\underline{\mathrm{Alg}}}(A,B))

\displaystyle=\underline{\mathrm{coAlg}}(\mathrm{colim}_{n}\,(F^{n}1)^{\circ},{\underline{\mathrm{Alg}}}(A,B))

\displaystyle\cong\lim_{n}\underline{\mathrm{coAlg}}((F^{n}1)^{\circ},{\underline{\mathrm{Alg}}}(A,B))

\displaystyle=\lim_{n}{\underline{\mathrm{Alg}}}_{n}(A,B).

On some occasions, the category of coalgebras of
F
can be interesting while its category of algebras is less so.
For instance, given an alphabet
\Sigma
, coalgebras over the endofunctor
F(X)=2\times X^{\Sigma}
in
\mathrm{Set}
are automata but the initial algebra remains
\emptyset
. To remedy this, we can extend our main result into the following theorem.

Theorem 4.4

Let
\mathscr{C}
be a locally presentably and closed symmetric monoidal category.
Let
F,G\colon\mathscr{C}\to\mathscr{C}
be accessible endofunctors.
Assume that
F
is lax symmetric monoidal, and that
G
is an
F
-module, in the sense that there is a transformation

FX\otimes GY\to G(X\otimes Y)

natural in
X,Y
and satisfying the diagrams of
(
Yetter, 2003
, Def. 39)
.
Then the category
{\mathrm{Alg}}_{G}
is enriched, tensored and cotensored over
{\mathrm{coAlg}}_{F}
.

Proof

We obtain a convolution algebra
{\mathrm{coAlg}}_{F}^{\mathrm{op}}\times{\mathrm{Alg}}_{G}\to{\mathrm{Alg}}_{G}
by taking a coalgebra
(C,\chi)
and an algebra
(A,\alpha)
to the following composite

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces G[C,A]}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{[FC,GA]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{[C,FA]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{[C,A]},}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\chi^{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha_{*}}

where
the unmarked arrow is the adjunct of the following composite

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces FC\otimes G([C,A])}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces G(C\otimes[C,A])}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces GA}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces G(\mathrm{ev})}

where
\mathrm{ev}
denotes evaluation. The action on morphisms is given by the naturality of constituent arrows of this constructed algebra structure. The functoriality is inherited from the functoriality of
[-,-]
.

The diagrams of
(
Yetter, 2003
, Def. 39)
exactly correspond to the opposite

[-,-]^{\mathrm{op}}\colon{\mathrm{coAlg}}_{F}\times{\mathrm{Alg}}_{G}^{\mathrm{op}}\to{\mathrm{Alg}}_{G}^{\mathrm{op}},

this functor being a left action in the sense of
Hyland et al. (2017)
.
Since
[-,A]^{\mathrm{op}}
preserves colimits, so does
[-,A]^{\mathrm{op}}\colon{\mathrm{coAlg}}_{F}\to{\mathrm{Alg}}_{G}^{\mathrm{op}}
. Since
{\mathrm{coAlg}}_{F}
is locally presentable, and
\mathrm{Alg}_{G}^{\mathrm{op}}
is essentially small, the functor has a right adjoint.
Thus, by
(
Hyland et al., 2017
, Prop. 3.1)
, we see that
{\mathrm{Alg}}_{G}^{\mathrm{op}}
is enriched, tensored and cotensored in
{\mathrm{coAlg}}_{F}
. Thus,
{\mathrm{Alg}}_{G}
is enriched, tensored and cotensored in
{\mathrm{coAlg}}_{F}
.

Corollary 1

Suppose that
\mathscr{C}
is a locally presentable, closed symmetric monoidal category. Suppose
F
is an accessible lax symmetric monoidal functor.
Let
G\colon\mathscr{C}\rightarrow\mathscr{C}
be a
\mathscr{C}
-enriched functor.
Then
{\mathrm{Alg}}_{GF}
is enriched and cotensored over
{\mathrm{coAlg}}_{F}
.

Proof

To apply previous result, we only need to show there exists an
F
-module structure on
GF
, that is, we need a certain natural map

FX\otimes GFY\to GF(X\otimes Y).

The
\mathscr{C}
-enrichment of
G
produces a map
X\otimes GY\rightarrow G(X\otimes Y)
for any objects
X
and
Y
in
\mathscr{C}
. Indeed, this is the adjunct of

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{[Y,X\otimes Y]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{[GY,G(X\otimes Y)]},}

where the first map is the coevaluation of the internal hom, and the second map is induced by the
\mathscr{C}
-enrichment. We apply this map to
FX
and
FY
to get the following composition

FX\otimes GFY\rightarrow G(FX\otimes FY)\xrightarrow{G\nabla}GF(X\otimes Y).

One can check that this makes the diagrams of
(
Yetter, 2003
, Def. 39)
commute.

Example 31

If
F(X)=2\times X^{\Sigma}
, we could consider
G=\mathrm{id}+1
, and thus
{\mathrm{Alg}}_{GF}
has a nonempty initial object and is enriched in automata.

The enrichment of algebras in coalgebras specifies a pairing of coalgebras

{\underline{\mathrm{Alg}}}(A_{2},A_{3})\otimes{\underline{\mathrm{Alg}}}(A_{1},A_{2})\longrightarrow{\underline{\mathrm{Alg}}}(A_{1},A_{3}),

regarded as an enriched composition, for any algebras
A_{1}
,
A_{2}
and
A_{3}
.
In more details, the above coalgebra homomorphism is induced by the measuring of

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\big({\underline{\mathrm{Alg}}}(A_{2},A_{3})\otimes{\underline{\mathrm{Alg}}}(A_{1},A_{2})\big)\otimes A_{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\underline{\mathrm{Alg}}}(A_{2},A_{3})\otimes A_{2}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A_{3}.}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{id}\otimes\mathrm{ev}_{A_{1},A_{2}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{ev}_{A_{2},A_{3}}}

In other words, the enrichment is recording precisely that we can compose a measuring
C\otimes A_{1}\rightarrow A_{2}
with
D\otimes A_{2}\rightarrow A_{3}
to obtain a measuring
(D\otimes C)\otimes A_{1}\rightarrow A_{3}
.

Remark 10

Theorem

4.3
and
Corollary

1
appeared in
North and Péroux (2023b)
. This current paper contextualizes and provides more details to the original work.

Theorem

4.4
is new and is an extension of the previous results, and moreover, the tower appearing in
Remark

9
provides a new formalization of partial induction that did not appear in
North and Péroux (2023b)
.

Section

4.8
below provides also new results that did not appear in
North and Péroux (2023b)
.

Remark 11

Recall that if
F
is an endofunctor in
\mathcal{C}
, we can define the free monad
\widetilde{F}
in
\mathscr{C}
on
F
, and the (Eilenberg–Moore) category of
\widetilde{F}
-algebras in
\mathscr{C}
is equivalent to the category of
F
-algebras
\mathrm{Alg}_{F}
in
\mathscr{C}
. A similar dual statement exists for coalgebras over endofunctors.
Algebras over monads are enriched in coalgebras over comonads given some compatibility
(
Grignou, 2022
, Thm. 6)
, and our results fit into this framework.
Given a field
k
, then
k
-algebras are algebras over the free
k
-algebra monad and
k
-coalgebras are coalgebras over the cofree
k
-coalgebra comonad, and thus the results of
Sect.

2
also fit in this framework.

4.8
General
C
-Initial Objects

Within the theory of categorical W-types, the category of algebras is mostly used to find the initial algebra, the W-type. Here, we explore how the extra structure that we have found in categories of algebras can be used to generalize the notion of initial algebras, inspired by the generalization of (co)limits in categories by the notion of weighted (co)limits in enriched categories.

We can use the extra structure in the enriched category of algebras to specify more algebras than we could in the unenriched category of algebras.

Definition 22
(
C
-initial algebra)

Suppose that
\mathscr{C}
is locally presentable and closed and that
F
is accessible.
Given a coalgebra
C
,
we say an algebra
A
is a
C
-initial algebra
if there exists a unique map
C\rightarrow{\underline{\mathrm{Alg}}}(A,X)
, for all algebras
X
. That is,
\mu_{C}(A,X)\cong*
.
The
terminal

C
-initial algebra is the terminal object, if it exists, in the subcategory of
{\mathrm{Alg}}
spanned by the
C
-initial algebras.

Remark 12

One may wonder what would happen if for a
set

S
, we defined an
S
-initial algebra to be an algebra
A
such that there is a unique
function

S\to{\mathrm{Alg}}(A,X)
for all
X\in{\mathrm{Alg}}
. But every algebra is an
\emptyset
-initial algebra, and an
S
-initial algebra is an initial algebra for any
S\neq\emptyset
(because functions
S\to T
are unique only when
S=\emptyset
or
T\cong 1
). Thus, we need to consider
{\underline{\mathrm{Alg}}}(A,X)
and not just
{\mathrm{Alg}}(A,X)
to obtain interesting
C
-initial algebras.

Example 32

We have shown in
Example

29
that
{\mathbb{n}}
is an
{\mathbb{n}^{\circ}}
-initial algebra.
Since
{\mathrm{Alg}}(A,X)\cong{\mathrm{coAlg}}(\mathbb{I},{\underline{\mathrm{Alg}}}(A,X))
, the initial algebra
\mathbb{N}
is the
\mathbb{I}
-initial algebra. In fact, since
{\underline{\mathrm{Alg}}}(\mathbb{N},X)\cong{\mathbb{N}_{\infty}}
for all
X
by
Proposition

4
or by a similar computation to
Example

29
, we find that
\mathbb{N}
is a
C
-initial algebra for any subterminal coalgebra (i.e.,
\emptyset,{\mathbb{n}^{\circ}},\mathbb{N}^{c},{\mathbb{N}_{\infty}}
).

Example 33

Since the only
\mathbb{I}
-initial algebra is
\mathbb{N}
, it is also the terminal
\mathbb{I}
-initial algebra.

The following result helps us calculate
C
-initial algebras in examples.

Proposition 6

Suppose that
\mathscr{C}
is locally presentable and closed and that
F
is accessible.
There is a unique map from any
C
-initial algebra to
C^{*}
.

Proof

We consider an object
\lim^{C}\mathrm{id}_{\underline{\mathrm{Alg}}}
with the following universal property:

{\mathrm{Alg}}(A,{\lim}^{C}\mathrm{id}_{\underline{\mathrm{Alg}}})\cong\lim_{X\in{\mathrm{Alg}}}\ {\mathrm{coAlg}}(C,{\underline{\mathrm{Alg}}}(A,X)).

(This is the limit of
\mathrm{id}_{\underline{\mathrm{Alg}}}
weighted by the constant functor at
C
.)
Since the hom
{\mathrm{coAlg}}(C,{\underline{\mathrm{Alg}}}(A,-))
preserves limits and
\lim_{X\in{\mathrm{Alg}}}X\cong\mathbb{N}
, we have

\displaystyle\lim_{X\in{\mathrm{Alg}}}\ {\mathrm{coAlg}}(C,{\underline{\mathrm{Alg}}}(A,X))

\displaystyle\cong{\mathrm{coAlg}}(C,{\underline{\mathrm{Alg}}}(A,\lim_{X\in{\mathrm{Alg}}}X))

\displaystyle\cong{\mathrm{coAlg}}(C,{\underline{\mathrm{Alg}}}(A,\mathbb{N}))

\displaystyle\cong{\mathrm{Alg}}(A,[C,\mathbb{N}])

\displaystyle={\mathrm{Alg}}(A,C^{*}).

Thus,
C^{*}
has the universal property of
\lim^{C}\mathrm{id}_{\underline{\mathrm{Alg}}}
.

Note that for any
C
-initial algebra
A
, we have
\lim_{X\in{\mathrm{Alg}}}\ {\mathrm{coAlg}}(C,{\underline{\mathrm{Alg}}}(A,X))
is the terminal object. Thus, by this universal property of
C^{*}
, there is a unique map
A\to C^{*}
.

In the following two examples, we calculate that
{\mathbb{n}}
is the terminal
{\mathbb{n}^{\circ}}
-initial algebra.

Example 34

Let
C:={\mathbb{n}^{\circ}}
. Then elements of
({\mathbb{n}^{\circ}})^{*}
are sequences of
n+1
natural numbers.
The successor of a sequence
(a_{i})_{i=0}^{n}
is
(b_{i})_{i=0}^{n}
where
b_{0}=0
and
b_{i+1}=a_{i}+1
. Notice that the successor of
(b_{i})_{i=0}^{n}
is
(c_{i})_{i=0}^{n}
where
c_{0}=0
,
c_{1}=1
and otherwise
c_{i+2}=a_{i}+2
. Thus, we can inductively show that the
(n+1)
-st successor of any element of
[{\mathbb{n}^{\circ}},\mathbb{N}]
is the sequence
(i)_{i=0}^{n}
, and the successor of this sequence is itself.

We claim that the unique morphism
!_{[{\mathbb{n}^{\circ}},\mathbb{N}]}:\mathbb{N}\to[{\mathbb{n}^{\circ}},\mathbb{N}]
factors through
{\mathbb{n}}
. We have
m_{[{\mathbb{n}^{\circ}},\mathbb{N}]}=(\min(i,m))_{i=0}^{n}
. Thus, the restriction of the map
!_{[{\mathbb{n}^{\circ}},\mathbb{N}]}
to
\{0,...,n\}\subset\mathbb{N}
is injective, and
n_{[{\mathbb{n}^{\circ}},\mathbb{N}]}=m_{[{\mathbb{n}^{\circ}},\mathbb{N}]}
for all
m\geq n
.

Example 35

Now we can show that
{\mathbb{n}}
is the terminal
{\mathbb{n}^{\circ}}
-initial algebra. In this calculation, we use the law of excluded middle for the only time in this paper.
Consider an
{\mathbb{n}^{\circ}}
-initial algebra
A
.

First, we show that every
a\in A
is either the basepoint or a successor. So suppose that there is an element
a\in A
that is not a basepoint or successor, and consider an algebra
B
with more than one element. Then for any
b\in B
and any measure
f:{\mathbb{n}^{\circ}}\to A\to B
, we can form a measure
\widetilde{f}:{\mathbb{n}^{\circ}}\to A\to B
such that
\widetilde{f}_{n}(a)=b
and
\widetilde{f}
agrees with
f
everywhere else, since
Definition

2
imposes no requirements on
\widetilde{f}_{n}(a)
. Thus, there are multiple measures
{\mathbb{n}^{\circ}}\to A\to B
, equivalently total algebra homomorphisms
{\mathbb{n}^{\circ}}\to{\underline{\mathrm{Alg}}}(A,B)
, so we find a contradiction.

Now, we consider the unique map
A\to[{\mathbb{n}^{\circ}},\mathbb{N}]
and claim that this factors through the injection
{\mathbb{n}}\to[{\mathbb{n}^{\circ}},\mathbb{N}]
, so that there is a unique
A\to{\mathbb{n}}
. Since every element of
A
is either a basepoint or a successor, every element of
A
is either of the form
n_{A}
or has infinitely many predecessors. The elements of the form
n_{A}
are mapped those to of the form
n_{[{\mathbb{n}^{\circ}},\mathbb{N}]}
, and the elements who have infinitely many predecessors can only be mapped to the ‘top element’
n_{[{\mathbb{n}^{\circ}},\mathbb{N}]}=(i)_{i=0}^{n}
, since this is the only element which has an
m
-th predecessor for any
m\in\mathbb{N}
. Thus, the unique
A\to[{\mathbb{n}^{\circ}},\mathbb{N}]
indeed factors through
{\mathbb{n}}
.

5
Examples

In the previous section we have built a general theory showing that the category of algebras of an endofunctor
F\colon\mathscr{C}\to\mathscr{C}
is enriched in the category of coalgebras of that same endofunctor.
We also saw several objects representing the measuring functor
\mu\colon\mathrm{coAlg}^{\mathrm{op}}\times\mathrm{Alg}^{\mathrm{op}}\times\mathrm{Alg}\to\mathrm{Set}
in each of its arguments.
Lastly, we assigned significance to
C
-initial algebras, which are algebras that have the same desirable properties as initial algebras, but are more abundant.

In this section, we would like to bring the theory to life by studying some examples.
Fixing an endofunctor
F
in each example, we will consider its initial algebra and terminal coalgebra, the representing objects of
\mu
and
C
-initial algebras.
Starting with some straightforward examples to get a feel, we will work towards those which have
those that serve as semantics for something in computer science.
For most of the examples we will only give the results and the necessary ingredients to obtain them.
However, there are two selected examples where we will go into more detail, showing intermediary steps and confirming the results.
The first of the two selected examples is the monoid type, which is the first example where we see the monoidal structure in action.
The second is the list type, which is the first example which has semantics commonly used in computer science.

In all our examples we consider the monoidal category
(\mathrm{Set},\times,1)
which is locally presentable and closed.
The endofunctors
F\colon\mathrm{Set}\to\mathrm{Set}
in the examples will all be accessible, and we will provide the monoidal structure of the endofunctor explicitly in each example.
Once an endofunctor is fixed we will denote the category of
F
-algebras by
\mathrm{Alg}
and the category of
F
-coalgebras by
\mathrm{coAlg}
.
Recall that due to
\mathrm{Set}
being locally presentable and
F
being accessible, the left adjoint of the forgetful functor
U\colon\mathrm{Alg}\to\mathrm{Set}
exists.
For the same reasons the right adjoint to the forgetful functor
U\colon\mathrm{coAlg}\to\mathrm{Set}
.
The free and cofree functors will be denoted by
\operatorname{Fr}
and
\operatorname{Cof}
respectively, and will be used throughout this section.
Finally, we will usually denote
F
-algebras by
(A,\alpha),(B,\beta)\in\mathrm{Alg}
and
F
-coalgebras by
(C,\chi),(D,\delta)\in\mathrm{coAlg}
.

Throughout, given a set map
\varphi\colon C\times A\rightarrow B
, we may write
\varphi_{c}(a)
for
\varphi(c,a)
for
c\in C
and
a\in A
. Given sets
S
and
T
, we write
[S,T]
for the set of all functions
S\rightarrow T
. We shall only focus on describing the measurings
\varphi\colon C\times A\to B
, and the associated
C
-indexed partial homomorphism
\widehat{\varphi}\colon C\to[A,B]
is defined as
\widehat{\varphi}(c)=\varphi_{c}
, and each
\varphi_{c}\colon A\to B
is regarded as a function
A\to B
that only partially respect the algebra structures on
A
and
B
, for all
c\in C
.

5.1
The Unit Type

First off, we wish to start with a simple example.
We will see that this endofunctor has the singleton
1
as its initial algebra.
Hence, this example corresponds to the unit type in functional programming, the type with only one value.
Let
F\colon\mathrm{Set}\to\mathrm{Set}
be the functor given by

\displaystyle F\colon\mathrm{Set}

\displaystyle\longrightarrow\mathrm{Set}

\displaystyle X

\displaystyle\longmapsto 1.

It has a trivial lax monoidal structure.

The category of
F
-algebras, denoted
\mathrm{Alg}
, has elements

\alpha\colon 1\to A,*\mapsto a_{0}

denoted
(A,a_{0})
and morphisms

f\colon(A,a_{0})\to(B,b_{0})

satisfying
f(a_{0})=b_{0}
.
It can be regarded as the category of pointed sets, or as the coslice category
1/\mathrm{Set}
.
If it is clear we are considering a pointed set, we will sometimes simply write
A\in\mathrm{Alg}
.
The category of
F
-coalgebras, denoted
\mathrm{coAlg}
, has elements

\chi\colon C\to 1

and morphisms

f\colon C\to D,

and so is isomorphic to the category of sets.

5.1.1
Initial and Terminal Objects

The initial algebra is given by
\mathrm{id}\colon(1,*)\to(1,*)
, since for every algebra
(A,a_{0})
there exists exactly one pointed morphism out of
\mathrm{id}\colon 1\to 1
.
For any preinitial algebra
(I,i_{0})
the unique morphism
(1,*)\to(I,i_{0})
must be epic.
This can only be the case whenever the underlying function
1\to I
is surjective.
The only set which satisfies this condition is
1
, so we conclude there are no preinitial algebras besides the initial algebra.

The terminal coalgebra is given by 1 since the
\mathrm{coAlg}
is isomorphic to
\mathrm{Set}
.
A subterminal coalgebra is any set
X
such that
X\rightarrowtail 1
is injective.
The only sets satisfying this are
\emptyset
and
1
, so we conclude these underlie the only subterminal coalgebras.

5.1.2
Measurings

Let
(A,a_{0}),(B,b_{0})\in\mathrm{Alg}
and
(C,\chi)\in\mathrm{coAlg}
.
A measuring from
A
to
B
by
C
is a function
\varphi\colon C\times A\to B
, such that

\varphi_{c}(a_{0})=b_{0}

for all
c\in C
.
We find that in this case, a measuring
\varphi\colon C\times A\to B
is nothing but a family of algebra morphisms
A\to B
indexed by
C
.

5.1.3
Free and Cofree Functors

In order to compute the representing objects of
\mu
, we wish to make use of the constructions provided in
Sect.

4
.
These constructions make use of the free and cofree functor.

The free functor is given by

\displaystyle\operatorname{Fr}\colon\mathrm{Set}

\displaystyle\longrightarrow\mathrm{Alg}

\displaystyle X

\displaystyle\longmapsto(X+1,\operatorname{inr})

\displaystyle f

\displaystyle\longmapsto f+\mathrm{id}_{1}

where
\operatorname{inr}
is the inclusion
1\to X+1
.
The unit and counit are given by

\eta^{\operatorname{Fr}}_{X}\colon X\to X+1,x\mapsto x

for all set
X
,
and

\varepsilon^{\operatorname{Fr}}_{A}\colon A+1\to A,*\mapsto a_{0},a\mapsto a
, for all pointed set
(A,a_{0})
.
One can easily check these satisfy the triangle identities and hence form an adjunction.

The forgetful functor
U\colon\mathrm{coAlg}\to\mathrm{Set}
is an isomorphism.
Hence the cofree functor is the identity as well.

5.1.4
Representing Objects

Now we have everything in place to compute the representing objects of
\mu
.
The first representing object is the universal measuring coalgebra
\underline{\mathrm{Alg}}(A,B)
.
Its underlying set is given by
[A\setminus\{a_{0}\},B]
with the measuring structure

\displaystyle\mathrm{ev}\colon[A\setminus\{a_{0}\},B]\times A

\displaystyle\to B

\displaystyle(f,a)

\displaystyle\mapsto\begin{cases}f(a)&\text{ if }a\neq a_{0}\\
b_{0}&\text{ if }a=a_{0}.\end{cases}

Indeed, given a measuring
\varphi\colon C\times A\to B
, there is exactly one coalgebra morphism
f\colon C\to\underline{\mathrm{Alg}}(A,B)
which results in a morphism of measurings, namely

\displaystyle f\colon C

\displaystyle\to\underline{\mathrm{Alg}}(A,B)

\displaystyle c

\displaystyle\mapsto{\varphi_{c}}_{|_{A\setminus\{a_{0}\}}}.

Conversely, given a coalgebra morphism
f\colon C\to\underline{\mathrm{Alg}}(A,B)
, we get a measuring
\mathrm{ev}\circ(f\times\mathrm{id})\colon C\times A\to B
.

The second representing object is the measuring tensor
C\triangleright A
, and has as underlying set

C\times A/(c,a_{0})\sim(c^{\prime},a_{0}),

for all
c,c^{\prime}\in C
, with the equivalence class of
(c,a_{0})
giving the algebra structure.
A measuring
\varphi
always uniquely factorizes since
\varphi(c,a_{0})=\varphi(c^{\prime},a_{0})=b_{0}
for all
c,c^{\prime}\in C
.
We can now state that
\mu_{C}(A,-)
is represented by
C\triangleright A
.

Lastly, given
C\in\mathrm{coAlg},B\in\mathrm{Alg}
, we can turn
[C,B]
into an algebra by taking the constant function
\operatorname{const}_{b_{0}}\colon C\to B,c\mapsto b_{0}

to be the preferred point in
[C,B]
.
This is the convolution algebra.

5.1.5
C
-Initial Algebras

If
C=\emptyset
, every algebra is
C
-initial, since
\emptyset
is the initial object in
\mathrm{Set}
.
For
C\neq\emptyset
, being
C
-initial implies the underlying set of
\underline{\mathrm{Alg}}(A,X)
given by
[A\setminus\{a_{0}\},X]
contains only a single element.
This is because for any other
1\neq D\in\mathrm{coAlg}
, there exists more than one function
C\to D
.
For
[A\setminus\{a_{0}\},X]
