to contain only one function for all
X
, it must be that
A\setminus\{a_{0}\}=\emptyset
, hence
A\cong 1
.
The only algebra satisfying this is the initial algebra
\mathrm{id}\colon 1\to 1
.
We conclude for
C\neq\emptyset
, the only
C
-initial algebra is
\mathrm{id}\colon 1\to 1
, i.e., the terminal object with its unique point as basepoint.

5.2
The Empty Type

Perhaps an even more fundamental example is that of the empty type.
The empty type is the type with no values, which means we wish to consider a functor which has the empty set as initial algebra.
Let
F\colon\mathrm{Set}\to\mathrm{Set}
be the functor given by

\displaystyle F\colon\mathrm{Set}

\displaystyle\longrightarrow\mathrm{Set}

\displaystyle X

\displaystyle\longmapsto X.

Its lax monoidal structure is given given by identities.

The category of
F
-algebras, denoted
\mathrm{Alg}
has elements

\alpha\colon A\to A

denoted
(A,\alpha)
.
Morphisms
f\colon(A,\alpha)\to(B,\beta)
are given by functions
f\colon A\to B

which make the following diagram commute:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B.}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\beta}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}

The category of
F
-coalgebras, denoted
\mathrm{coAlg}
has elements

\chi\colon C\to C

denoted
(C,\chi)
.
Morphisms
f\colon(C,\chi)\to(D,\delta)
are given by functions
f:C\to D

which make the following diagram commute:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces D}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces D.}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\chi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\delta}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}

We observe the category
\mathrm{coAlg}
is isomorphic to the category of
\mathrm{Alg}
by definition.
Nevertheless, we will distinguish between these categories to see which role they play in the upcoming constructions.

5.2.1
Initial and Terminal Objects

The initial algebra is given by
\mathrm{id}\colon\emptyset\to\emptyset
, since for every algebra
(A,\alpha)
there exists exactly morphism out of
\mathrm{id}\colon\emptyset\to\emptyset
.
The only preinitial algebra is given by the initial algebra itself, since there exist no epimorphisms out of the empty set other than the identity.

The terminal coalgebra is given by
\mathrm{id}\colon 1\to 1
since there exists exactly one morphism into
\mathrm{id}\colon 1\to 1
for every coalgebra
(C,\chi)
.
A subterminal coalgebra is then any coalgebra
(C,\chi)
such that
(C,\chi)\rightarrowtail(1,\mathrm{id})
is injective.
The only coalgebras satisfying this are
(\emptyset,\mathrm{id})
and
(1,\mathrm{id})
and we conclude
\emptyset
is the only subterminal coalgebra besides the terminal algebra.

5.2.2
Measurings

Let
(A,\alpha),(B,\beta)\in\mathrm{Alg}
and
(C,\chi)\in\mathrm{coAlg}
.
A measuring from
A
to
B
by
C
is a function
\varphi\colon C\times A\to B
, such that

\varphi_{c}(\alpha(a))=\beta(\varphi_{\chi(c)}(a))

for all
(c,a)\in C\times A
.
Another way to state the condition on measurings is to say the diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi_{\chi(c)}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\beta}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi_{c}}

must commute for all
c\in C
.

Example 36

Consider the algebras
(\mathbb{N},(\cdot 4)),(\mathbb{N},(\cdot 8))
and the coalgebra
(\mathbb{N},(\cdot 2))
.
A measuring from
(\mathbb{N},(\cdot 4))
to
(\mathbb{N},(\cdot 8))
by
(\mathbb{N},(\cdot 2))
is given by

\displaystyle\varphi\colon\mathbb{N}\times\mathbb{N}

\displaystyle\longrightarrow\mathbb{N}

\displaystyle(i,j)

\displaystyle\longmapsto i\cdot j^{2}.

To verify this is a measuring, we must check

\varphi(i,4j)=8\cdot\varphi(2i,j),

which is the case since

\varphi(i,4j)=i\cdot(4j)^{2}=16ij^{2}=8\cdot 2i\cdot j^{2}=8\cdot\varphi(2\cdot i,j).

5.2.3
Free and Cofree Functors

In order to compute the representing objects in the upcoming section, we need the left and right adjoint of the forgetful functors.
They are the free and cofree functor and are respectively given by

\displaystyle\operatorname{Fr}\colon\mathrm{Set}

\displaystyle\longrightarrow\mathrm{Alg}

\displaystyle X

\displaystyle\longmapsto\begin{pmatrix}\coprod_{i\in\mathbb{N}}X_{i}&\xrightarrow{\operatorname{Sh}}\coprod_{i\in\mathbb{N}}X_{i}\\
X_{i}\ni x&\mapsto x\in X_{i+1}\end{pmatrix},

where
X_{i}=X
,
and

\displaystyle\operatorname{Cof}\colon\mathrm{Set}

\displaystyle\longrightarrow\mathrm{coAlg}

\displaystyle X

\displaystyle\longmapsto\begin{pmatrix}\prod_{i\in\mathbb{N}}X_{i}&\xrightarrow{\operatorname{CoSh}}\prod_{i\in\mathbb{N}}X_{i}\\
(x_{0},x_{1},x_{2},\dots)&\mapsto(x_{1},x_{2},\dots)\end{pmatrix},

where
X_{i}=X
.
Their behavior on functions is in both cases given by applying the function component-wise.

To verify these are adjoints, one must verify

[X,A]\cong\mathrm{Alg}(\operatorname{Fr}(X),A)

and

[C,X]\cong\mathrm{Alg}(C,\operatorname{Cof}(X))
.
This can be done by constructing explicit natural bijections.
The first bijection is given by

[X,A]\to\mathrm{Alg}(\operatorname{Fr}(X),A),f\mapsto\coprod_{i\in\mathbb{N}}\alpha^{i}\circ f

with inverse

\mathrm{Alg}(\operatorname{Fr}(X),A)\to[X,A],\coprod_{i\in\mathbb{N}}f_{i}\mapsto f_{0}.

The second bijection is given by

[C,X]\to\mathrm{coAlg}(C,\operatorname{Cof}(X)),f\mapsto\langle f\circ\chi^{i}\rangle_{i\geq 0}

with inverse

\mathrm{coAlg}(C,\operatorname{Cof}(X))\to[C,X],\langle f_{i}\rangle_{i\geq 0}\mapsto f_{0}.

One can check those bijections are indeed natural.

5.2.4
Representing Objects

The universal measuring coalgebra
\underline{\mathrm{Alg}}(A,B)
has as underlying set

\left.\left\{(f_{i})_{i\geq 0}=(f_{0},f_{1},\dots)\in\prod_{i\in\mathbb{N}}[A,B]\,\right|\,\beta\circ f_{i+1}=f_{i}\circ\alpha\right\}.

Its coalgebra structure is given by shifting the entries to the left, or more precisely by the restriction of
\operatorname{CoSh}
to
\underline{\mathrm{Alg}}(A,B)
.
The terminal object in the category of measurings from
A
to
B
is then given by

\displaystyle\mathrm{ev}\colon\underline{\mathrm{Alg}}(A,B)\times A

\displaystyle\longrightarrow B

\displaystyle((f_{i})_{i\geq 0},a)

\displaystyle\longmapsto f_{0}(a).

Indeed, given a measuring
\varphi\colon C\times A\to B
, there is exactly one coalgebra morphism
f\colon C\to\underline{\mathrm{Alg}}(A,B)
which results in a morphism of measurings, namely

f\colon C\to\underline{\mathrm{Alg}}(A,B),c\mapsto(\varphi_{\chi^{i}(c)})_{i\geq 0}.

Conversely, given a morphism
f\colon C\to\underline{\mathrm{Alg}}(A,B)
, composing with
\mathrm{ev}
yields a measuring
\mathrm{ev}\circ(f\times\mathrm{id})\colon C\times A\to B
.
This yields a bijective correspondence between morphisms
f\colon C\to\underline{\mathrm{Alg}}(A,B)
and measurings
\varphi\colon C\times A\to B
.

The measuring tensor
C\triangleright A
has as underlying set

\coprod_{i\in\mathbb{N}}(C\times A)_{i}\big/\sim,

where the equivalence relation
\sim
is given by

(\chi(c),a)_{i+1}\sim(c,\alpha(a))_{i}.

Its algebra structure is given by shifting to the right, namely by the function
\operatorname{Sh}
.
A measuring
\varphi
corresponds to the algebra morphism

\widetilde{\varphi}\colon C\triangleright A\to B,(c,a)_{i}\mapsto\varphi_{\chi^{i}(c)}(a)

and an algebra morphism
\widetilde{\varphi}\colon C\triangleright A\to B
corresponds to a measuring
\varphi=\widetilde{\varphi}|_{(C\times A)_{0}}
.
We can then state
\mu_{C}(A,-)
is represented by
C\triangleright A
.

Finally, given
C\in\mathrm{coAlg},B\in\mathrm{Alg}
, we define the convolution algebra
[C,B]
to be the algebra structure given by

\displaystyle\beta_{*}\chi^{*}\colon[C,B]

\displaystyle\to[C,B]

\displaystyle f

\displaystyle\mapsto\beta\circ f\circ\chi.

5.2.5
C
-Initial Algebras

If
C=\emptyset
every algebra is
C
-initial, with
(1,\mathrm{id})
being the terminal
\emptyset
-initial algebra.
For
C\neq\emptyset
the only
C
-initial algebra is
(\emptyset,\mathrm{id})
, since every other algebra would give too much freedom in constructing maps from
C
to
\mathrm{Alg}(A,X)
for there to be a unique morphism.
This means
(\emptyset,\mathrm{id})
is also the terminal
C
-initial algebra for all
C\in\mathrm{coAlg}
.

5.3
The Monoid Type

In this example, we will begin to see the flexibility measurings provide.
This is also the motivation for discussing more details regarding the representing objects than previously done.
We will be considering the type which takes values in a fixed monoid
(M,\bullet,e)
.
This type is not as frequently used in computer science, but it does give us insight into the general theory.
To study this type, we must find a functor which has it as an initial algebra.

Let
F\colon\mathrm{Set}\to\mathrm{Set}
be the functor given by

\displaystyle F\colon\mathrm{Set}

\displaystyle\longrightarrow\mathrm{Set}

\displaystyle X

\displaystyle\longmapsto M

\displaystyle f

\displaystyle\longmapsto\mathrm{id}_{M}

where
(M,\bullet,e)
is some fixed monoid in
\mathrm{Set}
.
The lax monoidal structure is given by monoid structure on
M
, namely

\nabla_{A,B}\colon M\times M\to M

where
\nabla_{A,B}(m,m^{\prime})=m\bullet m^{\prime},

and

\eta\colon 1\to M
, where
\eta(*)=e
.
Since we will need it later, we also remark the lax closed structure is given by

\widetilde{\nabla}_{A,B}\colon M\to[M,M]
where
\widetilde{\nabla}_{A,B}(m)=r_{m}
,
where
r_{m}\colon M\to M
is defined as
r_{m}(m^{\prime})=m^{\prime}\bullet m
, i.e., it is the function which multiplies an element of
M
by
m\in M
from the right.

The category
\mathrm{Alg}
has elements
\alpha\colon M\to A
, with morphisms
f\colon(A,\alpha)\to(B,\beta)
given by commuting squares (or triangles if you prefer)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B.}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\beta}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{id}_{M}}

The category
\mathrm{coAlg}
has elements
\chi\colon C\to M
, with morphisms
f\colon(C,\chi)\to(D,\delta)
given by commuting squares

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces D.}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{id}_{M}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\chi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\delta}

Notice how these categories correspond to under category
M/\mathrm{Set}
and the over category
\mathrm{Set}/M
respectively.

5.3.1
Initial and Terminal Objects

The initial algebra and the terminal coalgebra are both given by

\mathrm{id}_{M}\colon M\to M.

Any algebra for which the function
\alpha\colon M\to A
is surjective gives a preinitial algebra.
Similarly, any coalgebra for which the function
\chi\colon C\to M
is injective will give a subterminal coalgebra.

5.3.2
Measuring

Let
(A,\alpha),(B,\beta)\in\mathrm{Alg}
and
(C,\chi)\in\mathrm{coAlg}
.
A measuring from
A
to
B
by
C
is a function
\varphi\colon C\times A\to B
, such that

\varphi_{c}(\alpha(m))=\beta(\chi(c)\bullet m)

for all
c\in C
and
m\in M
.
Comparing this to the condition on a regular algebra morphism
f\colon A\to B
, given by

f(\alpha(m))=\beta(m),

we see a coalgebra
C
introduces some sort of twisting.
The above condition on
\varphi
is equivalent to asking the following diagram commutes

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{M\times M}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{C\times M}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{C\times A}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B.}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\chi\times\mathrm{id}_{M}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{id}_{C}\times\alpha}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\beta}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bullet}

Example 37

Consider the algebra
(M,\mathrm{id}_{M})
and the coalgebra
(M,\mathrm{id}_{M})
.
A trivial example of a measuring is the monoid multiplication
\bullet\colon M\times M\to M
.

Example 38

Let
A\in\mathrm{Set}
be arbitrary and consider the algebra structure on
[A,M]
given by

\displaystyle\alpha\colon M

\displaystyle\to[A,M]

\displaystyle m

\displaystyle\mapsto\operatorname{const}_{m}

and the coalgebra
(M,\mathrm{id}_{M})
.
A measuring from
([A,M],\alpha)
to itself by
(M,\mathrm{id}_{M})
is given by

\displaystyle\varphi\colon M\times[A,M]

\displaystyle\longrightarrow[A,M]

\displaystyle(m,f)

\displaystyle\longmapsto(a\mapsto f(a)\bullet m).

Indeed, we can verify

\varphi(m^{\prime},\operatorname{const}_{m})=\operatorname{const}_{m^{\prime}\bullet m}=\alpha(m^{\prime}\bullet m).

Example 39

Let
M

∗

denote the set of finite lists with entries in the monoid
M
.
We shall be slightly informal in this example, but the
the reader can see more precise statements in
Sect.

5.5
.
We can give it an algebra structure

\alpha\colon M\to M^{*}

by mapping
m
to the list
[m]
that contains the single element
m
.
We can also give it a coalgebra structure

\chi\colon M^{*}\to M

by sending a list to the product of all entries in the list.
If acquainted with functional programming, one might recognize the definition of
\mathtt{fold}
in the coalgebra structure.

Let
[]
denote the empty list and
m:ℓ
denote the concatenation of
m\in M
and
\ell\in M^{*}
. A measuring from
(M^{*},\alpha)
to itself by
(M^{*},\chi)
is given by

\displaystyle\varphi\colon M^{*}\times M^{*}

\displaystyle\longrightarrow M^{*}

\displaystyle(\ell^{\prime},[\>])

\displaystyle\longmapsto[\>]

\displaystyle(\ell^{\prime},m:\ell)

\displaystyle\longmapsto(\chi(\ell^{\prime})\bullet m):\ell,

collapsing the entire list
\ell^{\prime}
onto the first element of the list
m:\ell
using the monoidal structure on
M
.
To verify this is a measuring we check

\varphi_{\ell^{\prime}}(\alpha(m))=\varphi_{\ell^{\prime}}([m])=[\chi(\ell^{\prime})\bullet m]=\alpha(\chi(\ell^{\prime})\bullet m).

Note this is not the only measuring we could have defined.
Another measuring is given by

\displaystyle\varphi^{\prime}\colon M^{*}\times M^{*}

\displaystyle\longrightarrow M^{*}

\displaystyle\ell^{\prime},[\>]

\displaystyle\longmapsto[\>]

\displaystyle\ell^{\prime},m:\ell

\displaystyle\longmapsto[\chi(\ell^{\prime})\bullet m],

simply ignoring the last part of the list
m:\ell
.
Again we can verify this is a measuring, using the exact same equation as before to find

\varphi_{\ell^{\prime}}(\alpha(m))=\varphi_{\ell^{\prime}}([m])=[\chi(\ell^{\prime})\bullet m]=\alpha(\chi(\ell^{\prime})\bullet m).

5.3.3
Free and Cofree Functors

The left adjoint free functor is given by

\displaystyle\operatorname{Fr}\colon\mathrm{Set}

\displaystyle\longrightarrow\mathrm{Alg}

\displaystyle X

\displaystyle\longmapsto(X+M,\operatorname{\alpha_{\operatorname{Fr}}})

\displaystyle f

\displaystyle\longmapsto f+\mathrm{id}_{M}.

where
\alpha_{\operatorname{Fr}}=\operatorname{inr}
, the inclusion of
M
into
X+M
.
The unit and counit are given by

\eta^{\operatorname{Fr}}_{X}\colon X\to X+M
where
\eta^{\operatorname{Fr}}_{X}(x)=x
, for any set
X
,
and

\varepsilon^{\operatorname{Fr}}_{A}\colon A+M\to A

is defined by
\varepsilon^{\operatorname{Fr}}_{A}(m)=\alpha(m)
and
\varepsilon^{\operatorname{Fr}}_{A}(a)=a
, for all
m\in M
and
a\in A
, where
(A,\alpha)
is an algebra.
One can easily check these satisfy the triangle identities and hence form an adjunction.

The right adjoint cofree functor is given by

\displaystyle\operatorname{Cof}\colon\mathrm{Set}

\displaystyle\longrightarrow\mathrm{coAlg}

\displaystyle X

\displaystyle\longmapsto(X\times M,\chi_{\operatorname{Cof}})

\displaystyle f

\displaystyle\longmapsto(f\times\mathrm{id}_{M}).

where
\chi_{\operatorname{Cof}}=\operatorname{pr}_{M}
.
The unit and counit are given by

\eta^{\operatorname{Cof}}_{C}\colon C\to C\times M
where
\eta^{\operatorname{Cof}}_{C}(c)=(c,\chi(c))
for all coalgebra
(C,\chi)
,
and

\varepsilon^{\operatorname{Cof}}_{X}\colon X\times M\to X
where
\varepsilon^{\operatorname{Cof}}_{X}(x,m)=x
for all set
X
.
Again, one can check the triangle identities to verify the adjunction.

5.3.4
Representing Objects

The universal measuring coalgebra
\underline{\mathrm{Alg}}(A,B)
is given by the following equalizer

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\underline{\mathrm{Alg}}(A,B)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{[A,B]\times M}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{[M,B]\times M}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{eq}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces[\alpha,B]\times\mathrm{id}_{M}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Phi}

where
\Phi
is the transpose of

\widetilde{\Phi}\colon[A,B]\times M\xrightarrow{\chi_{\operatorname{Cof}}}M\xrightarrow{F(\varepsilon^{\operatorname{Cof}})}M\xrightarrow{\widetilde{\nabla}_{A,B}}[M,M]\xrightarrow{\beta_{*}}[M,B].

If we compute the composition we find

\widetilde{\Phi}\colon[A,B]\times M\to[M,B]
is defined explicitly by
\widetilde{\Phi}(f,m)=r_{m}
,
where we recall
r_{m}\colon M\to M
was multiplication by
m
on the right.
Taking its transpose
\Phi=\operatorname{Cof}(\widetilde{\Phi})\circ\eta
we find the explicit formula for
\Phi
to be

\displaystyle\Phi\colon[A,B]\times M

\displaystyle\longrightarrow[M,B]\times M

\displaystyle(f,m)

\displaystyle\longmapsto(m^{\prime}\mapsto\beta(m^{\prime}\bullet m),m).

Now that we know explicitly which maps we want to equalize, we can compute

\underline{\mathrm{Alg}}(A,B)=\{(f,m)\in[A,B]\times M\mid(f\circ\alpha)(m^{\prime})=\beta(m\bullet m^{\prime})\}.

with coalgebra structure given by the projection

\operatorname{pr}_{M}\colon\underline{\mathrm{Alg}}(A,B)\to M
where
\operatorname{pr}_{M}(f,m)=m
.
We would like to verify
\underline{\mathrm{Alg}}(A,B)
is indeed the universal measuring.
In other words, we would like to construct a natural bijection

\Psi\colon\mu_{C}(A,B)\xrightarrow{\cong}\mathrm{coAlg}(C,\underline{\mathrm{Alg}}(A,B)).

This bijection is given by sending a measuring
\varphi\in\mu_{C}(A,B)
to

\Phi(\varphi)(c)=(a\mapsto\varphi(c,a),\chi(c)).

This is a well-defined coalgebra morphism since

\chi(c)=\operatorname{pr}_{M}(\Phi(\varphi)(c)).

Its inverse
\Psi^{-1}
sends a coalgebra morphism
f\colon C\to\underline{\mathrm{Alg}}(A,B)
to the measuring

\displaystyle\Psi^{-1}(f)\colon C\times A

\displaystyle\to B

\displaystyle(c,a)

\displaystyle\mapsto(\operatorname{pr}_{[A,B]}\circ f)(c)(a).

We must verify this is a measuring, which we can do using that
f
is a coalgebra morphism.
Observe

(\operatorname{pr}_{[A,B]}\circ f)(c)(\alpha(m))=\beta((\operatorname{pr}_{M}\circ f)(c)\bullet m)=\beta(\chi(c),m).

It is easy to verify that
\Psi
and
\Psi^{-1}
are inverses of each other and that the bijection is natural.

The measuring tensor
C\triangleright A
is given by the coequalizer

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(C\times M)+M}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(C\times A)+M}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{C\triangleright A},}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Psi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{id}_{C}\times\alpha+\mathrm{id}_{M}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{coeq}}

where
\Psi
is the transpose of

\widetilde{\Psi}\colon C\times M\xrightarrow{\chi\times\mathrm{id}_{M}}M\times M\xrightarrow{\nabla_{A,B}}M\xrightarrow{F(\eta^{\operatorname{Fr}})}M\xrightarrow{\alpha_{\operatorname{Fr}}}(C\times A)+M.

If we compute the composition we find

\widetilde{\Phi}\colon C\times M\to(C\times A)+M,(c,m)\mapsto\chi(c)\bullet m.

Taking its transpose
\Psi=\varepsilon\circ\operatorname{Fr}(\widetilde{\Phi})
we find the explicit formula for
\Phi
to be

\displaystyle\Psi\colon{(C\times M)+M}

\displaystyle\longrightarrow{(C\times A)+M}

\displaystyle(c,m)

\displaystyle\longmapsto\chi(c)\bullet m

\displaystyle m^{\prime}

\displaystyle\longmapsto m^{\prime}.

Now that we know explicitly which maps we want to coequalize, we can compute

C\triangleright A\cong\big((C\times A)+M\big)/(c,\alpha(m))\sim(\chi(c)\bullet m).

with algebra structure given by

M\to C\triangleright A,m\mapsto[m].

A slightly more intuitive may be to write
C\triangleright A
as the pushout

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{C\times M}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{C\times A}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{C\triangleright A}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{id}_{C}\times\alpha}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lrcorner}

where
f\colon C\times M\to M,(c,m)\mapsto\chi(c)\bullet m
.
To verify
C\triangleright A
is measuring tensor, we construct a natural bijection

\Xi\colon\mu_{C}(A,B)\xrightarrow{\cong}\mathrm{Alg}(C\triangleright A,B).

This bijection is given by sending a measuring
\varphi\in\mu_{C}(A,B)
to

\displaystyle\Xi(\varphi)\colon C\triangleright A

\displaystyle\longrightarrow B

\displaystyle[c,a]

\displaystyle\longmapsto\varphi(c,a)

\displaystyle[m]

\displaystyle\longmapsto\beta(m).

We must check this is well-defined, which is the case since

\Xi(\varphi)([c,\alpha(m)])=\varphi(c,\alpha(m))=\beta(\chi(c)\bullet m)=\Xi(\varphi)([\chi(c)\bullet m])

and the equivalence relation defining
C\triangleright A
is generated by
(c,\alpha(m))\sim\chi(c)\bullet m
.
It is a well-defined algebra morphism since
[m]=\beta(m)
by definition.
Its inverse
\Xi^{-1}
sends an algebra morphism
f\colon C\triangleright A\to B
to the measuring

\displaystyle\Xi^{-1}(f)\colon C\times A

\displaystyle\to B

\displaystyle(c,a)

\displaystyle\mapsto f([c,a]).

We must verify this is a measuring, which we can do using that
f
is an algebra morphism.
Observe

f([c,\alpha(m)])=f([\chi(c)\bullet m])=\beta([\chi(c)\bullet m]),

which shows
\Xi^{-1}(f)
is a measuring.
It is easy to verify the
\Xi
and
\Xi^{-1}
are inverses of each other and that the bijection is natural.

Lastly, the convolution algebra
[C,B]
has algebra structure given by the composition

M\xrightarrow{\widetilde{\nabla}_{C,B}}[M,M]\xrightarrow{\beta_{*}\circ\chi^{*}}[C,B].

It sends
m\in M
to
(c\mapsto\beta(\chi(c)\bullet m))
.

5.3.5
C
-Initial Algebras

One of the characterizations of a
C
-initial algebra
A
is that
C\triangleright A
is isomorphic to the initial object.
In our case this would mean that

M\cong\left.\Big((C\times A)+M\Big)\right/{}_{\displaystyle(c,\alpha(m))\sim(\chi(c)\bullet m)},

which is the case if and only if
\alpha\colon M\to A
is surjective.
We conclude any preinitial algebra
\alpha\colon M\twoheadrightarrow A
is also
C
-initial for any coalgebra
C
.
This also implies the terminal algebra
M\to 1
is the terminal
C
-initial algebra for all coalgebras
C
.

5.4
The Natural Numbers Type

In
Sect.

4
, the main example provided was the example of the natural numbers type.
In this section we will repeat and elaborate on that example.
Finally, we will add a new constructive proof regarding
C
-initial algebras.
To study this type, we must find a functor which has it as an initial algebra.

Consider the functor

\displaystyle F\colon\mathrm{Set}

\displaystyle\longrightarrow\mathrm{Set}

\displaystyle X

\displaystyle\longmapsto 1+X

\displaystyle f

\displaystyle\longmapsto\mathrm{id}_{1}+f.

The lax monoidal structure is given by

\displaystyle\nabla_{X,Y}\colon 1+X\times 1+Y

\displaystyle\longrightarrow 1+(X\times Y)

\displaystyle(*,y)

\displaystyle\longmapsto*

\displaystyle(x,*)

\displaystyle\longmapsto*

\displaystyle(x,y)

\displaystyle\longmapsto(x,y)

and

\operatorname{inr}\colon 1\to 1+1.

The category of
F
-algebras has elements

\alpha\colon 1+A\to A

denoted
(A,\alpha)
.
Sometimes we will denote
\alpha(*)
by
0_{A}
, and more generally
\alpha^{n}(0_{A})
will be denoted as
n_{A}
.
Morphisms
f\colon(A,\alpha)\to(B,\beta)
are given by functions
f\colon A\to B

which make the following diagram commute:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 1+A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 1+B}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B.}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{id}+f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\beta}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}

The category of
F
-coalgebras has elements

\chi\colon C\to 1+C,

denoted
(C,\chi)
.
Morphisms
f\colon(C,\chi)\to(D,\delta)
are given by functions
f\colon C\to D

which make the following diagram commute

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 1+C}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 1+D}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces D.}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{id}_{1}+f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\chi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\delta}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}

5.4.1
Initial and Terminal Objects

The initial algebra is the given by the set of natural numbers
\mathbb{N}
.
Its algebra structure is given by succession, as we recalled in
Sect.

3.1
.
To be more precise, it is given by

\displaystyle 1+\mathbb{N}

\displaystyle\to\mathbb{N}

\displaystyle*

\displaystyle\mapsto 0

\displaystyle n

\displaystyle\mapsto n+1.

We denote by
-_{A}\colon\mathbb{N}\to A
the unique algebra morphism.
The sets of the form
\mathbb{n}\coloneqq\{0,1,\dots,n\}
are preinitial algebras with algebra structure given by

\displaystyle 1+\mathbb{n}

\displaystyle\to\mathbb{n}

\displaystyle*

\displaystyle\mapsto 0

\displaystyle i

\displaystyle\mapsto\min(i,n).

Recall from
Example

16
that the terminal coalgebra is given by
{\mathbb{N}_{\infty}}\coloneqq\mathbb{N}+\{\infty\}
.
Its coalgebra structure is given by

\displaystyle{\mathbb{N}_{\infty}}

\displaystyle\to 1+{\mathbb{N}_{\infty}}

\displaystyle i

\displaystyle\mapsto\begin{cases}*&\text{ if }i=0\\
i-1&\text{ otherwise}\end{cases}

\displaystyle\infty

\displaystyle\mapsto\infty.

The unique coalgebra morphism
C\to{\mathbb{N}_{\infty}}
is denoted
\llbracket{-}\rrbracket\colon C\to{\mathbb{N}_{\infty}}
.
Subterminal coalgebras are given by subsets of
{\mathbb{N}_{\infty}}
which are closed under the coalgebra structure:
\emptyset
,
\mathbb{n}^{\circ}
,
\mathbb{N}^{c}
,
{\mathbb{N}_{\infty}}
as well as the union of
\{\infty\}
with any of the these sets.
The coalgebra structure is simply a restriction of the coalgebra structure of
{\mathbb{N}_{\infty}}
.
Finally, we would like to point out that
\mathbb{n}^{\circ}
has a universal property.
Since
\mathbb{n}^{\circ}
is subterminal, there exists at most one morphism
C\to\mathbb{n}^{\circ}
into it for every
C\in\mathrm{coAlg}
.
This morphism exists if and only if
\llbracket{c}\rrbracket\leq n
for all
c\in C
, which is the universal property of
\mathbb{n}^{\circ}
.

5.4.2
Measuring

Let
(A,\alpha),(B,\beta)\in\mathrm{Alg}
and
(C,\chi)\in\mathrm{coAlg}
.
A measuring from
A
to
B
by
C
is a function
\varphi\colon C\times A\to B
satisfying

1.

\varphi_{c}(0_{A})=0_{B}
for all
c\in C

2.

\varphi_{c}(\alpha(a))=0_{B}
if
\chi(c)=*

3.

\varphi_{c}(\alpha(a))=\beta(\varphi_{c^{\prime}}(a))
if
\chi(c)=c^{\prime}
.

Intuitively,
the coalgebra
C
determines up to which point we are computing the algebra morphism.

Example 40

Notice that the algebra structures involved can be quite limiting.
We claim there does not exist a total algebra morphism
f\colon\mathbb{n}\to\mathbb{N}
.
Denoting the algebra structure of
\mathbb{n}
by
\alpha_{n}\colon 1+\mathbb{n}\to\mathbb{n}
, we can create a contradiction by noting that for
n\in\mathbb{n}
, we must have

f(n)=f(\alpha_{n}(n))=1+f(n)

which cannot be the case.
However, we are able to define a measuring from
\mathbb{n}
to
\mathbb{N}
.
We define the measuring

\displaystyle\varphi\colon{\mathbb{n}}^{\circ}\times\mathbb{n}

\displaystyle\to\mathbb{N}

\displaystyle(i,j)

\displaystyle\mapsto\min(i,j).

We note that checking this is a measuring can be done by comparing the definition of measuring and that of
\varphi
.
Moreover, comparing with the definition also reveals this morphism is unique.

Example 41

Say we would like to define a measuring
\varphi\colon\mathbb{n}^{\circ}\times(\mathbb{n}-1)\to\mathbb{N}
.
By definition
\varphi
must satisfy

\displaystyle\varphi(0,j)

\displaystyle=0

\displaystyle\text{ for all }j\in\mathbb{n}-1

\displaystyle\varphi(i,0)

\displaystyle=0

\displaystyle\text{ for all }i\in\mathbb{n}^{\circ}

\displaystyle\varphi(i,\alpha_{n-1}(j))

\displaystyle=1+\varphi(i-1,j)

\displaystyle\text{ for all }i\neq 0,j\in\mathbb{n}-1.

This forces the definition of
\varphi
to be
\varphi(i,j)=\min(i,j)
for all
0\leq i,j\leq n-1
.
However, we run into contradictions when computing
\varphi(n,n-1)
.
Since
\alpha_{n-1}(n-2)=\alpha_{n-1}(n-1)=n-1
, we must have that

\varphi(n,n-1)=\varphi(n,\alpha_{n-1}(n-2))=1+\varphi(n-1,n-2)=1+\min(n-1,n-2)=n-1,

but also

\varphi(n,n-1)=\varphi(n,\alpha_{n-1}(n-1))=1+\varphi(n-1,n-1)=1+\min(n-1,n-1)=n.

We see there does not exist a measuring
\varphi\colon\mathbb{n}^{\circ}\times(\mathbb{n}-1)\to\mathbb{N}
.
This is due to the fact that we tried to take the inductive definition of
\varphi
one step too far.

5.4.3
Free and Cofree Functors

The free functor is defined by mapping
X\in\mathrm{Set}
to the algebra

\displaystyle\alpha\colon 1+\mathbb{N}\times(X+\{0_{X}\})

\displaystyle\to\mathbb{N}\times(X+\{0_{X}\})

\displaystyle*

\displaystyle\mapsto(0,0_{X})

\displaystyle(i,0_{X})

\displaystyle\mapsto(i+1,0_{X})

\displaystyle(i,x)

\displaystyle\mapsto(i+1,x).

The behavior of
\operatorname{Fr}
on functions
f\colon X\to Y
is given by

\displaystyle\operatorname{Fr}(f)\colon\mathbb{N}\times(X+\{0_{X}\})

\displaystyle\to\mathbb{N}\times(Y+\{0_{Y}\})

\displaystyle(i,x)

\displaystyle\mapsto(i,f(x))

\displaystyle(i,0_{X})

\displaystyle\mapsto(i,0_{Y}).

Checking this is a left adjoint to the forgetful functor can be done by constructing the natural bijection

[X,A]\cong\mathrm{Alg}(\operatorname{Fr}(X),A),

for any set
X
and algebra
(A,\alpha)

The bijection is given by

f\mapsto\widetilde{f},

where
\widetilde{f}
is defined inductively as

\displaystyle\widetilde{f}\colon\mathbb{N}\times(X+\{0_{X}\})

\displaystyle\to B

\displaystyle(0,0_{X})

\displaystyle\mapsto 0_{B}

\displaystyle(0,x)

\displaystyle\mapsto f(x)

\displaystyle(i,0_{X})

\displaystyle\mapsto\beta(\widetilde{f}(i-1,0_{X}))

\displaystyle(i,x)

\displaystyle\mapsto\beta(\widetilde{f}(i-1,x)).

The inverse of the bijection
[X,A]\to\mathrm{Alg}(\operatorname{Fr}(X),A)
is given by restriction to
X
.
One can check this bijection is natural.

The cofree functor is defined by mapping
X\in\mathrm{Set}
to the following coalgebra:

\displaystyle\delta\colon X\times X^{\leq\infty}

\displaystyle\to 1+X\times X^{\leq\infty}

\displaystyle(x_{0},[\>])

\displaystyle\mapsto*

\displaystyle(x_{0},x:\ell)

\displaystyle\mapsto(x,\ell)

Here,
X^{\leq\infty}
is the set of finite or infinite lists with entries in
X
(see
Sect.

5.5.1
for details).
The behavior of
\operatorname{Cof}
on functions
f\colon X\to Y
is forced and given by applying
f
to all elements of
X
in any element of
X\times X^{\leq\infty}
.
Checking this is a right adjoint to the forgetful functor can be done by constructing the natural bijection

[C,X]\cong\mathrm{coAlg}(C,\operatorname{Cof}(X)),

The bijection is given by

[C,X]\to\mathrm{coAlg}(C,\operatorname{Cof}(X)),f\mapsto\widetilde{f},

where

\displaystyle\widetilde{f}=\langle\widetilde{f}_{0},\widetilde{f}_{1}\rangle\colon C

\displaystyle\longrightarrow X\times X^{\leq\infty}

\displaystyle c

\displaystyle\longmapsto\begin{cases}(f(c),f(c^{\prime}):\widetilde{f}_{1}(c^{\prime}))\text{ if }\chi(c)=c^{\prime}\\
(f(c),[\>])\text{ if }\chi(c)=*.\end{cases}

Notice the function
\widetilde{f}_{0}=f
, which also gives the inverse for the bijection.
Verifying
\widetilde{f}
is a coalgebra morphism is left to the reader.

5.4.4
Representing Objects

For more details, we refer the reader to the upcoming section about lists, since it is a direct generalization of what we are considering here.

First we wish to construct the universal measuring coalgebra
\underline{\mathrm{Alg}}(A,B)
.
The underlying set of
\underline{\mathrm{Alg}}(A,B)
is a subset of
[A,B]\times[A,B]^{\leq\infty}
, given by

\displaystyle\{(f_{0},f_{i+1})_{i\in I}\mid f_{i}(0_{A})=0_{B},f_{i}(\alpha(a))

\displaystyle=\beta(f_{i+1}(a)),

\displaystyle f_{i}(\alpha(a))=\beta(f_{i+1}(a))\text{ for all }a\in A\},

where by
f_{\max}
we mean the last element of the stream
(f_{i+1})_{i\in I}
, if it exists.
Unpacking the above, we find elements of
\underline{\mathrm{Alg}}(A,B)
are non empty streams
(f_{0},f_{i+1})_{i\in I}
such that

f_{i}(\alpha(a))=\beta(f_{i+1}(a)),

and the last element of the stream (if it exists) should be the function mapping all elements of the form
\alpha(a)
to the element
0_{B}\in B
.
The coalgebra structure of
\underline{\mathrm{Alg}}(A,B)
is given by shifting to the left,

(f_{0},(f_{i+1})_{i})\mapsto(f_{1},(f_{i+2})_{i}),(f_{0},[\>])\mapsto*.

Lastly, we give the evaluation map

\displaystyle\mathrm{ev}\colon\underline{\mathrm{Alg}}(A,B)\times A

\displaystyle\to B

\displaystyle((f_{0},(f_{i+1})_{i\in I}),a)

\displaystyle\mapsto f_{0}(a).

The evaluation map is measuring from
A
to
B
by
\underline{\mathrm{Alg}}(A,B)
by construction.
To verify
\mu_{C}(A,B)\cong\mathrm{coAlg}(C,\underline{\mathrm{Alg}}(A,B))
, we refer the reader to the next section.

Next we would like to determine the measuring tensor
C\triangleright A
.
By
Theorem

2.2
,
\mu_{C}(A,B)\cong\mathrm{Alg}(C\triangleright A,B)
, where
C\triangleright A
has as underlying set

\operatorname{Fr}(C\times A)/\sim\>\>=\mathbb{N}\times((C\times A)+\{e_{C\times A}\})/\sim.

Here the equivalence relation
\sim
is generated by

\displaystyle(i,c,0_{A})

\displaystyle\sim(i,e_{C\times A})

\displaystyle(i,c,\alpha(a))

\displaystyle\sim(i,e_{C\times A})\text{ if }\chi(c)=*

\displaystyle(i,c,\alpha(a))

\displaystyle\sim(i+1,c^{\prime},a)\text{ if }\chi(c)=c^{\prime}.

The algebra structure is induced by the function

\displaystyle 1+\operatorname{Fr}(C\times A)

\displaystyle\to\operatorname{Fr}(C\times A)

\displaystyle*

\displaystyle\mapsto(0,e_{C\times A})

\displaystyle(i,e_{C\times A})

\displaystyle\mapsto(i+1,e_{C\times A})

\displaystyle(i,c,a)

\displaystyle\mapsto(i+1,c,a)

which respects the quotient map
\operatorname{Fr}(C\times A)\to\operatorname{Fr}(C\times A)/\sim
.
To verify
\mu_{C}(A,B)\cong\mathrm{Alg}(C\triangleright A,B)
we again refer the reader to the upcoming section.

Finally, we define the convolution algebra
[C,B]
. Its algebra structure is given by

\displaystyle 1+[C,B]

\displaystyle\to[C,B]

\displaystyle*

\displaystyle\mapsto(c\mapsto 0_{B})

\displaystyle f

\displaystyle\mapsto\left(c\mapsto\begin{cases}\beta(f(c^{\prime}))\text{ if }\chi(c)=c^{\prime}\\
0_{A}\text{ if }\chi(c)=*\end{cases}\right).

We would like to calculate
\underline{\mathrm{Alg}}(\mathbb{n},B)
for arbitrary
B\in\mathrm{Alg}
.
Our aim is to leverage the isomorphism

\mathrm{coAlg}(C,\underline{\mathrm{Alg}}(\mathbb{n},B))\cong\mu_{C}(\mathbb{n},B)\cong\mathrm{Alg}(\mathbb{n},[C,B]).

To do so, we make an observation about
\mathrm{Alg}(\mathbb{n},Z)
for an arbitrary algebra
(Z,\zeta)\in\mathrm{Alg}
.
Since
\mathbb{n}
is preinitial, any morphism out of
\mathbb{n}
is unique.
So,
\mathrm{Alg}(\mathbb{n},Z)
has at most one element.
The question now becomes if there is a condition on
Z
for
\mathrm{Alg}(\mathbb{n},Z)
to be inhabited.
We claim this condition is

\zeta(i_{Z})=\zeta((\min(n-1,i))_{Z})\text{ for all }i\in\mathbb{N}.

(5.4.1)

Now that we have an indication on whether
\mathrm{Alg}(\mathbb{n},Z)
is inhabited for arbitrary
Z\in\mathrm{Alg}
, we can focus on
Z=[C,B]
.
Observing the algebra structure on
[C,B]
, we see the morphism
-_{[C,B]}
is given by

\displaystyle-_{[C,B]}:\mathbb{N}

\displaystyle\to[C,B]

\displaystyle 0

\displaystyle\mapsto(c\mapsto 0_{B})

\displaystyle i+1

\displaystyle\mapsto\left(c\mapsto\begin{cases}\beta(i_{[C,B]}(c^{\prime}))\text{ if }\chi(c)=c^{\prime}\\
0_{B}\text{ if }\chi(c)=*\end{cases}\right).

Denoting the algebra structure on
[C,B]
by
\alpha
, we would like to know under which conditions on
C
and
B

\alpha(i_{[C,B]})=\alpha((\min(n-1)(i))_{[C,B]})
.
The above condition can be satisfied if
\llbracket{c}\rrbracket\leq n
for all
c\in C
or if condition
5.4.1
holds for
B
.
So, we have the following:

\mathrm{Alg}(\mathbb{n},[C,B])\cong\begin{cases}\{*\}\text{ if }\beta(i_{B})=\beta((\min(n-1)(i))_{B})\text{ for all }i\in\mathbb{N}\\
\{*\}\text{ if }\llbracket{c}\rrbracket\leq n\text{ for all }c\in C\\
\emptyset\text{ otherwise}.\end{cases}

Now we can make use of the isomorphism
\mathrm{coAlg}(C,\underline{\mathrm{Alg}}(\mathbb{n},B))\cong\mathrm{Alg}(\mathbb{n},[C,B])

We observe that in the case of
\beta(i_{B})=\beta((\min(n-1)(i))_{B})
,
\underline{\mathrm{Alg}}(\mathbb{n},B)
has the universal property of the terminal coalgebra
{\mathbb{N}_{\infty}}
.
Whenever this is not the case,
\underline{\mathrm{Alg}}(\mathbb{n},B)
has the universal property of
\mathbb{n}^{\circ}\in\mathrm{coAlg}
.
We conclude

\underline{\mathrm{Alg}}(\mathbb{n},B)=\begin{cases}\mathbb{N}_{\infty}\text{ if }\beta(i_{B})=\beta((\min(n-1)(i))_{B})\text{ for all }i\in\mathbb{N}\\
\mathbb{n}^{\circ}\text{ otherwise}.\end{cases}

5.4.5
C
-Initial Algebras

In this section we would like to give a proof of the fact that
\mathbb{n}
is the terminal
\mathbb{n}^{\circ}
-initial algebra.
The proof is different from the simpler proof of
Example

35
, with the main advantage that it does not use the law of excluded middle.
Where in the previous examples we could make a complete classification of
C
-initial algebras, here we cannot.
The algebra structures involved are more complex, and hence we will restrict ourselves to the most useful cases.

We first show there exists a morphism
A\to\mathbb{n}
for any
\mathbb{n}^{\circ}
-initial algebra
A
using induction.
After that, we will show the morphism is unique.
We start with a lemma which allows us to use induction later on.

Lemma 1

Let
A
be a
\mathbb{n}^{\circ}
-initial algebra. Then
A
is also
(\mathbb{n}-1)^{\circ}
initial.

Proof

Consider the coalgebra morphism
m\colon(\mathbb{n}-1)^{\circ}\to\mathbb{n}^{\circ},i\mapsto i
.
This induces a morphism

\displaystyle m\triangleright A\colon(\mathbb{n}-1)^{\circ}\triangleright A

\displaystyle\to\mathbb{n}^{\circ}\triangleright A

\displaystyle[n,i,a]

\displaystyle\mapsto[n,i,a].

This morphism is monomorphic by definition.
Since
A
is
\mathbb{n}^{\circ}
-initial, we know
\mathbb{n}\triangleright A
is an initial object.
This means
m\triangleright A
is a monomorphism into the initial object, hence an isomorphism.

As an immediate consequence we have the following corollary.

Corollary 2

Let
A
be a
\mathbb{n}^{\circ}
-initial algebra. Then
A
is also
\mathbb{k}^{\circ}
initial for all
k\leq n
.

The next lemma is a technical lemma which we will be able to leverage during the induction step.

Lemma 2

Let
A
be a
\mathbb{n}^{\circ}
-initial algebra and let
\varphi\colon\mathbb{n}^{\circ}\times A\to\mathbb{N}
be the unique measuring from
A
to
\mathbb{N}
by
\mathbb{n}^{\circ}
.
Then for all
i\in\mathbb{n}^{\circ}
and
0\leq j\leq n-i
we have

\varphi(i,a)=\varphi(i,\varphi(i+j,a)_{A}).

Proof

Let
k\leq n
.
Define the coalgebra morphism

\displaystyle p_{j}\colon\mathbb{k}^{\circ}

\displaystyle\to\mathbb{n}^{\circ}\times\mathbb{n}^{\circ}

\displaystyle i

\displaystyle\mapsto(i,i+j)

for all
0\leq j\leq n-k
.
Consider the composition of measurings
\varphi\circ(\mathrm{id}\times-_{A}\circ\varphi)\in\mu_{\mathbb{n}^{\circ}\times\mathbb{n}^{\circ}}(A,\mathbb{N})
.
We can precompose this measuring with the coalgebra morphism
p_{j}
to obtain a measuring

\mathbb{k}^{\circ}\times A\xrightarrow{p_{j}\times\mathrm{id}_{A}}\mathbb{n}^{\circ}\times\mathbb{n}^{\circ}\times A\xrightarrow{\varphi\circ(\mathrm{id}\times-_{A}\circ\varphi)}\mathbb{N}.

We also have the coalgebra morphism
f\colon\mathbb{k}^{\circ}\to\mathbb{n}^{\circ},i\mapsto i
which we can precompose with
\varphi
.
This gives us a measuring

\mathbb{k}^{\circ}\times A\xrightarrow{f\times\mathrm{id}_{A}}\mathbb{n}^{\circ}\times A\xrightarrow{\varphi}\mathbb{N}.

Since
A
is also
\mathbb{k}^{\circ}
-initial, we know these measurings must coincide.
Hence we can state

\varphi(i,a)=\varphi(i,-_{A}\circ\varphi(i+j,a))

for all
i\in\mathbb{k}^{\circ}
and
0\leq j\leq n-k
.
The only restriction placed on
k
was that
k\leq n
.
Iterating over all
0\leq k\leq n
, we arrive at the desired result

\varphi(i,a)=\varphi(i,-_{A}\circ\varphi(i+j,a))

for all
0\leq i\leq n
and
0\leq j\leq n-i
.

Corollary 3

Let
A
be
\mathbb{n}^{\circ}
-initial and let
\varphi
be the unique measuring to
\mathbb{N}
.
For all
i\in\mathbb{n}^{\circ}
and
a\in A
,
\varphi(i,a)\leq i
.

Proof

Since
\varphi(i,a)=\varphi(i,(\varphi(i,a))_{A})
by the previous lemma, we know

[0,i,a]=[0,i,(\varphi(i,a))_{A}]

in
\mathbb{n}^{\circ}\triangleright A
.
We also have
[\varphi(i,a),z]=[0,i,a]
, and by definition of the equivalence relation
\sim
, we have

[0,i,(\varphi(i,a))_{A}]=[\min(i,\varphi(i,a)),z].

From this we conclude

\varphi(i,a)=\min(i,\varphi(i,a)),

hence
\varphi(i,a)\leq i
for all
i\in\mathbb{n}^{\circ}
.

Now we are ready to define the a family of functions which will culminate in an algebra morphism
A\to\mathbb{n}
for any
\mathbb{n}^{\circ}
-initial algebra
A
.

Proposition 7

Let
A
be an
\mathbb{n}^{\circ}
-initial algebra.
Let
\varphi\colon\mathbb{n}^{\circ}\times A\rightarrow\mathbb{N}
be the unique measuring. The functions
\varphi_{k}\colon A\to\mathbb{k}

are total algebra morphisms for all
0\leq k\leq n
.

Proof

We proceed by induction over
k
.
For
k=0
, we have that
\mathbb{k}\cong 1
, the terminal object in the category of algebras.
Hence
\varphi_{0}:A\to 1
is an algebra morphism.
For the inductive step, assume
\varphi_{k-1}:A\to\mathbb{k}-1
is an algebra morphism.
We wish to show
\alpha_{k}(\varphi_{k}(a))=\varphi_{k}(\alpha(a))
for all
a\in A
.
If
\varphi_{k}(a)=0
, we know

\displaystyle\varphi(k,\alpha(a))

\displaystyle=1+\varphi(k-1,a)

\displaystyle=1+\varphi(k-1,(\varphi(k,a))_{A})

\displaystyle=1+\varphi(k-1,0_{A})

\displaystyle=1=\alpha_{k}(0)

\displaystyle=\alpha_{k}(\varphi(k,a)).

If
\varphi_{k}(a)\geq 0
we can make the following deduction

\displaystyle\alpha_{k}(\varphi_{k}(a))

\displaystyle=\alpha_{k}(\varphi(k,a))

\displaystyle=1+\alpha_{k-1}(\varphi(k,a)-1)

\displaystyle=1+\alpha_{k-1}(\varphi(k,(\varphi(k,a))-1)_{A})

\displaystyle=1+\alpha_{k-1}(\varphi(k-1,(\varphi(k,a)-1)_{A}))

\displaystyle=1+\varphi(k-1,\varphi(k,a)_{A})

\displaystyle=1+\varphi(k-1,a)

\displaystyle=\varphi(k,\alpha(a)).

In particular, this lemma shows
\varphi_{n}\colon A\to\mathbb{n}
is an algebra morphism.
It still remains to show this algebra morphism is unique.

Lemma 3

For any
\mathbb{n}^{\circ}
-initial algebra
A
, there exist at most one algebra morphism
A\to\mathbb{n}
.

Proof

By
Proposition

6
, there exists a unique morphism
A\to[\mathbb{n}^{\circ},\mathbb{N}]
for any
\mathbb{n}^{\circ}
-initial algebra
A
.
Since
\mathbb{n}
is
\mathbb{n}^{\circ}
-initial we know there exists a unique morphism
m\colon\mathbb{n}\rightarrowtail[\mathbb{n}^{\circ},\mathbb{N}]
.
Upon closer inspection
m
turns out to be a monomorphism.
Given any two morphisms
f,g\colon A\to\mathbb{n}
, we can draw the following diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{[\mathbb{n}^{\circ},\mathbb{N}]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{n}.}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces!}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces m}

Since the morphism
A\to[\mathbb{n}^{\circ},\mathbb{N}]
is unique, we know the composites
m\circ f=m\circ g
, and by
m
being mono we conclude
f=g
.
Hence, there can be at most one algebra morphism from a
\mathbb{n}^{\circ}
-initial algebra
A
to
\mathbb{n}
.

Putting all the above together, we arrive at the following result.

Theorem 5.1

The algebra
\mathbb{n}
is the terminal
\mathbb{n}^{\circ}
-initial algebra.

Proof

Given an
\mathbb{n}^{\circ}
initial algebra
A
, by Proposition
7
we obtain an algebra morphism
\varphi_{n}\colon A\to\mathbb{n}
.
By Lemma
3
it is unique.
We conclude
\mathbb{n}
is the terminal
\mathbb{n}^{\circ}
-initial algebra.

5.5
The List Type

In
Sect.

5.3
we have seen how introducing a monoidal structure allows measurings to introduce some “twist”.
On the other hand in
Sect.

5.4
we have seen how measurings are able to control “shape” in a sense.
In this section we are combining the two, which comes together nicely in the list type.
Given a monoid
(M,\bullet,e)
, we can consider the type which contains all possible finite lists with elements in
M
.
Lists are used ubiquitously throughout computer science and exhibits the flexibility gained when considering not just algebra morphisms, but measurings as well.

To study the list type we must find a functor which has it as an initial algebra.
This functor exists and is defined as follows.
Let
(M,\bullet,e)
be a commutative monoid in
\mathrm{Set}
and consider the functor

\displaystyle F\colon\mathrm{Set}

\displaystyle\longrightarrow\mathrm{Set}

\displaystyle X

\displaystyle\longmapsto 1+X\times M

\displaystyle f

\displaystyle\longmapsto\mathrm{id}_{1}+f\times\mathrm{id}_{M}.

The lax symmetric monoidal structure is given by

\displaystyle\nabla_{X,Y}\colon(1+M\times X)\times(1+M\times Y)

\displaystyle\longrightarrow 1+M\times X\times Y

\displaystyle(*,(m,y))

\displaystyle\longmapsto*

\displaystyle((m,x),*)

\displaystyle\longmapsto*

\displaystyle((m,x),(m^{\prime},y))

\displaystyle\longmapsto(m\bullet m^{\prime},x,y)

and

\eta\colon 1\to 1+M,*\mapsto e.

Since we will use it explicitly later, we remark the lax closed structure is given by

\displaystyle\widetilde{\nabla}_{X,Y}\colon 1+M\times[X,Y]

\displaystyle\longrightarrow[1+M\times X,1+M\times Y]

\displaystyle*

\displaystyle\longmapsto\operatorname{const}_{*}

\displaystyle(m,f)

\displaystyle\longmapsto\widetilde{\nabla}_{X,Y}(m,f),

where

\displaystyle\widetilde{\nabla}_{X,Y}(m,f)\colon 1+M\times X

\displaystyle\longrightarrow 1+M\times Y

\displaystyle*

\displaystyle\longmapsto*

\displaystyle(m^{\prime},x)

\displaystyle\longmapsto m\bullet m^{\prime},f(x).

The category of
F
-algebras has elements

\alpha\colon 1+M\times A\to A

denoted
(A,\alpha)
. We will write
\alpha(*)=e_{A}
, thinking of
\alpha(*)
as an “empty element”.
Sometimes when considering an algebra we will speak of a “list-like algebra”.
Morphisms
f\colon(A,\alpha)\to(B,\beta)
are given by functions
f\colon A\to B

which make the following diagram commute:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 1+M\times A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 1+M\times B}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B.}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F(f)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\beta}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}

The category of
F
-coalgebras has elements

\chi\colon C\to 1+M\times C,

denoted
(C,\chi)
.
Sometimes when considering a coalgebra we will speak of a “stream-like coalgebra”.
Morphisms
f\colon(C,\chi)\to(D,\delta)
are given by functions
f\colon C\to D

which make the following diagram commute:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 1+M\times C}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 1+M\times D}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces D.}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F(f)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\chi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\delta}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}

5.5.1
Initial and Terminal Objects

We denote the set of lists of length
n
with elements in
M
by
M^{n}
.
The underlying set of the initial algebra is given by

\lx@glossaries@gls@link{main}{List}{{{}}M^{*}}\coloneqq\coprod_{i\in\mathbb{N}}M^{i}.

Again borrowing notation from functional programming, we will write
\lx@glossaries@gls@link{main}{EmptList}{{{}}[\>]}\in M^{0}
for the empty list and given a list
\ell\in M^{*}
and an element
m\in M
, appending
m
to the list
\ell
is denoted by
m:ℓ
.
Moreover, we will call the element which was appended to the list most recently the first element, and conversely the element which was appended to the list earliest the last element.
The algebra structure of
M^{*}
is given by

\displaystyle 1+M\times M^{*}

\displaystyle\longrightarrow M^{*}

\displaystyle*

\displaystyle\longmapsto[\>]

\displaystyle(m,\ell)

\displaystyle\longmapsto m:\ell.

This also explains the term “list-like algebra”, since every algebra
\alpha\colon 1+M\times A\to A
has an empty element
e_{A}=\alpha(*)
and a way of appending an element
m\in M
to a “list-like”
a\in A
.
To verify
M^{*}
is indeed the initial algebra, let
(A,\alpha)
be an arbitrary algebra.
The unique algebra morphism
M^{*}\to A
, denoted
\mathord{\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\mathinvertedexclamationmark$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\mathinvertedexclamationmark$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\mathinvertedexclamationmark$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathinvertedexclamationmark$}}}_{A}\colon M^{*}\to A
is forced by the commutative square

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 1+M\times M^{*}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 1+M\times A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M^{*}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A,}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F(f)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathord{\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\displaystyle\mathinvertedexclamationmark$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\textstyle\mathinvertedexclamationmark$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\scriptstyle\mathinvertedexclamationmark$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\scriptscriptstyle\mathinvertedexclamationmark$}}}_{A}}

and defined as

\displaystyle\mathord{\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\mathinvertedexclamationmark$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\mathinvertedexclamationmark$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\mathinvertedexclamationmark$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathinvertedexclamationmark$}}}_{A}\colon M^{*}

\displaystyle\to A

\displaystyle[\>]

\displaystyle\mapsto e_{A}

\displaystyle m:\ell

\displaystyle\mapsto\alpha(m,f(\ell)).

Preinitial algebras correspond to equivalence relations
\sim
on
M^{*}
which satisfy

\ell\sim\ell^{\prime}\Rightarrow m:\ell\sim m:\ell^{\prime}\text{ for all }m\in M,

by
Proposition

2
.
An example is the equivalence relation which identifies all lists with length greater than
n
with the list of their last
n
elements.
This results in a preinitial algebra which we will denote by
\lx@glossaries@gls@link{main}{Listn}{{{}}M^{\leq n}}\coloneqq\coprod_{0\leq i\leq n}M^{i}
.
Its algebra structure is given by

\displaystyle 1+M\times M^{\leq n}

\displaystyle\to M^{\leq n}

\displaystyle*

\displaystyle\mapsto[\>]

\displaystyle(m,\ell)

\displaystyle\mapsto\mathtt{take}(n)(m:\ell),

where
\mathtt{take}
takes the first
n
elements of a list.
Another example is the equivalence relation generated by

m:\ell\sim\ell\text{ if }m\not\in M^{\prime},

where
M^{\prime}\subseteq M
.
This results in the preinitial algebra
(M^{\prime})^{*}
for any
M^{\prime}\subseteq M
.
The unique algebra morphism
M^{*}\to(M^{\prime})^{*}
corresponds to filtering the elements of a list.

One can check that in the above cases the unique morphisms
M^{*}\to M^{\leq n}
and
M^{*}\to(M^{\prime})^{*}
are epic.
The above two can be combined to yield filtered lists of length at most
n
, leading to even more preinitial algebras.
However, keep in mind this list is not exhaustive, and that there are even more preinitial algebras.

The terminal coalgebra is given by lists in
M
of finite and infinite length and is denoted
M^{\leq\infty}
.
Formally, it is given by
M^{\leq\infty}=M^{*}+(\prod_{n\in\mathbb{N}}M)
.
To verify this is indeed the terminal coalgebra, let
(C,\chi)
be an arbitrary coalgebra.
The unique algebra morphism
C\to M^{\leq\infty}
, denoted
\mathexclamationmark_{C}\colon C\to M^{\leq\infty}
, is forced by the commutative square

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 1+M\times C}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 1+M\times M^{\leq\infty}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M^{\leq\infty},}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F(f)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\chi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathexclamationmark_{C}}

and defined as

\displaystyle\mathexclamationmark_{C}\colon C

\displaystyle\longrightarrow M^{\leq\infty}

\displaystyle c

\displaystyle\longmapsto\begin{cases}[\>]\text{ if }\chi(c)=*\\
m:f(c^{\prime})\text{ if }\chi(c)=(m,c^{\prime}).\\
\end{cases}

Subterminal coalgebras correspond to subsets
X\subseteq M^{\leq\infty}
satisfying

m:\ell\in X\Rightarrow\ell\in X

by
Proposition

3
.
As with with the case of the natural numbers, some of the subterminal coalgebras share the same underlying set as preinitial algebras.
We will use
(-)^{\circ}
to denote the coalgebra whenever it might be ambiguous.
Some examples are

\emptyset,{(M^{\leq 0})}^{\circ}\cong 1,{(M^{\leq n})}^{\circ},{M^{*}}^{\circ},\prod_{n\in\mathbb{N}}M
and

(M^{\prime})^{\leq\infty},M^{\prime}\subseteq M
,
with their coalgebra structure inherited from the coalgebra structure on
M^{\leq\infty}
.
One can verify the all maps from subterminal coalgebra into
M^{\leq\infty}
are injective.

5.5.2
Functions as Algebra Morphisms

In this section we would like to demonstrate the power of the algebraic approach to types.
We wish to give the definitions of some basic functions frequently seen in computer science as algebra morphisms.

Example 42

The first function is the length function
\mathtt{len}
, which takes a list and returns its length.
We want to word it in the language of algebras, so we first define an algebra

\displaystyle 1+M\times\mathbb{N}

\displaystyle\to\mathbb{N}

\displaystyle*

\displaystyle\mapsto 0

\displaystyle(m,n)

\displaystyle\mapsto n+1.

The function
len
on lists
\ell\in M^{*}
is then given by the unique algebra morphism
M^{*}\to\mathbb{N}
.

Example 43

The second function considered takes two lists and concatenates them.
Classically, this would be denoted as a function
M^{*}\times M^{*}\to M^{*}
, but to frame it as an algebra morphism, we consider it as a function
M^{*}\to[M^{*},M^{*}]
.
To this end, consider the the algebra

\displaystyle\alpha\colon 1+M\times[M^{*},M^{*}]

\displaystyle\to[M^{*},M^{*}]

\displaystyle*

\displaystyle\mapsto\mathrm{id}

\displaystyle(m,f)

\displaystyle\mapsto(\ell\mapsto m:f(\ell)).

The concatenation function is denoted by
(++)
and is defined uniquely by the following diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 1+M\times M^{*}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 1+M\times[M^{*},M^{*}]}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M^{*}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces[M^{*},M^{*}]}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F(\>++\>)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(++)}

where uniqueness follows from the fact
M^{*}
is the initial algebra.
Given
\ell\in M^{*}
, the function
(++)(\ell)\in[M^{*},M^{*}]
is the function which takes a list
\ell^{\prime}
and concatenates it with
\ell
,
resulting in the list
(++)(\ell)(\ell^{\prime})=\ell++\>\ell^{\prime}
.

Example 44

The next function is
\mathtt{head}
, which is a partially defined function which attempts to extract the first element of a list-like.
We define the algebra

\displaystyle\alpha\colon 1+M\times(M+\{\bot\})

\displaystyle\to M+\{\bot\}

\displaystyle*

\displaystyle\mapsto\bot

\displaystyle(m,\ell)

\displaystyle\mapsto m.

The function
\mathtt{head}
is then defined as the following algebra morphism

\displaystyle\mathtt{head}\colon M^{*}

\displaystyle\to(M+\{\bot\},\alpha)

\displaystyle[\>]

\displaystyle\mapsto\bot

\displaystyle m:\ell

\displaystyle\mapsto m.

Example 45

The next function is
\mathtt{take}(n)
, which is a function that takes the first
n\in{\mathbb{N}_{\infty}}
elements of a list.
We have already defined this function “by hand” when defining the algebra structure on
M^{\leq n}
, but we would like view it from our algebraic perspective.
As maybe suspected, it is given by the unique function

\displaystyle\mathtt{take}(n)\colon M^{*}

\displaystyle\to M^{\leq n}

\displaystyle\ell

\displaystyle\mapsto\mathtt{take}(n)(\ell).

Example 46

The
\mathtt{filter}
function takes a predicate
p\in P_{M}\coloneqq[M,\{\top,\bot\}]
and a list
\ell\in M^{*}
and returns only the elements in
\ell
which satisfy
p
.
Classically, this would be written as
\mathtt{filter}\colon M^{*}\times P_{M}\to M^{*}
, but in our case we want to consider it as a function

\mathtt{filter}\colon M^{*}\to[P_{M},M^{*}]
.
First, we define an algebra structure on
[P_{M},M^{*}]
by

\displaystyle\beta\colon 1+M\times[P_{M},M^{*}]

\displaystyle\to[P_{M},M^{*}]

\displaystyle*

\displaystyle\mapsto\operatorname{const}_{[\>]}

\displaystyle(m,f)

\displaystyle\mapsto\left(p\mapsto\begin{cases}m:f(p)\text{ if }p(m)\\
f(p)\text{ otherwise}\end{cases}\right).

The function
\mathtt{filter}
is given by the unique algebra morphism
M^{*}\to[P_{M},M^{*}]
.
Another way to construct the filter function using a predicate
p\in P_{M}
is to compute the set
M_{p}=\{m\in M\mid p(m)=\top\}
.
Since
M_{p}\subset M
,
M_{p}^{*}
has the algebra structure corresponding to filtering.
The function
\mathtt{filter}(p)
is given by the unique function
\mathtt{filter}(p)\colon M^{*}\to M_{p}^{*}
.

Notice that all functions above can be described as folds.
Constructing the target algebra
(A,\alpha)
as done above corresponds to finding the accumulator and the binary operator of the fold.

5.5.3
Measuring

Let
(A,\alpha),(B,\beta)\in\mathrm{Alg}
and
(C,\chi)\in\mathrm{coAlg}
.
A measuring from
A
to
B
by
C
is a function
\varphi\colon C\times A\to B
satisfying

1.

\varphi_{c}(e_{A})=e_{B}
for all
c\in C
;

2.

\varphi_{c}(\alpha(m,a))=e_{B}
if
\chi(c)=*
;

3.

\varphi_{c}(\alpha(m,a))=\beta(m^{\prime}\bullet m,\varphi_{c^{\prime}}(a))
if
\chi(c)=(m^{\prime},c^{\prime})
.

Intuitively, a measuring is a function that takes a stream-like coalgebra and a list-like algebra and combines their elements using the monoidal structure on
M
, then wraps them up in a new list-like.
We see the coalgebra introduces some element wise “twist”, as well as limits the “shape” of the list-like.

Example 47

Give any commutative monoid
(M,e,\bullet)
, we remark there does not exist a total algebra morphism
f\colon M^{\leq n}\to M^{*}
, since for a list
\ell\in M^{\leq n}
of length
n
, we must have that

m:f(\mathtt{take}(n-1)(\ell))=f(m:\mathtt{take}(n-1)(\ell))=f(m:\ell)=m:f(\ell),

which cannot be the case.
Note that in the above computation we have used
\mathtt{take}(n)(m:\ell)=m:\mathtt{take}(n-1)(\ell)
.
The problem here is that we are disregarding the last element of the list, where an algebra morphism into
M^{*}
does need that information.

However, we are able to define a measuring from
M^{\leq n}
to
M^{*}
.
We define the measuring

\displaystyle\varphi\colon({M^{\leq n}})^{\circ}\times M^{\leq n}

\displaystyle\to M^{*}

\displaystyle([\>],\ell)

\displaystyle\mapsto[\>]

\displaystyle(\ell^{\prime},[\>])

\displaystyle\mapsto[\>]

\displaystyle(m^{\prime}:\ell^{\prime},m:\ell)

\displaystyle\mapsto(m^{\prime}\bullet m):\varphi(\ell^{\prime},\ell).

We note that checking this is a measuring can be done by comparing the definition of measuring and that of
\varphi
.
This example is valid because the coalgebra
{(M^{\leq n})}^{\circ}
limits up to which point we are considering elements of the algebra
M^{\leq n}
.
The problem we had earlier, caused by disregarding the last element of a list due to the maximum length of the list, is mitigated by the introduction of the coalgebra
{(M^{\leq n})}^{\circ}
.
Since the elements of
{(M^{\leq n})}^{\circ}
all have length at most
n
, we can truly disregard the last element of a list whenever its length exceeds
n
.

Example 48

Next, we investigate another interesting preinitial algebra given by
(M^{\prime})^{*}
for some subset
M^{\prime}\subseteq M
.
We would like to find a measuring
\varphi\colon C\times(M^{\prime})^{*}\to B
for some coalgebra
C\in\mathrm{coAlg}
and some algebra
B\in\mathrm{Alg}
.
This measuring must satisfy

\varphi_{c}(m:\ell)=\begin{cases}\beta((m\bullet m^{\prime}),\varphi_{c^{\prime}}(\ell))\text{ if }\chi(c)=(m^{\prime},c^{\prime})\\
e_{B}\text{ otherwise}.\end{cases}

By the coalgebra structure on
(M^{\prime})^{*}
, we have that for any
m\not\in M^{\prime}
,
m:\ell=\ell
.
In the case that
\chi(c)=(m^{\prime},c^{\prime})
and
m\not\in M^{\prime}
, the above condition becomes

\varphi_{c}(\ell)=\varphi_{c}(m:\ell)=\beta((m\bullet m^{\prime}),\varphi_{c^{\prime}}(\ell)),

giving us a contradiction since we need
\varphi_{c}(\ell)=\beta((m\bullet m^{\prime}),\varphi_{c^{\prime}}(\ell))
for all
m\not\in M^{\prime}
.
The problem here is a loss of information.
This loss stems from the fact that
m:m:\dots:m:\ell=\ell
whenever
m\not\in M^{\prime}
,
whereas the measuring does need to take into account the added elements
m
.

Example 49

Using measurings, we are also able to define a more flexible version of the
\mathtt{map}
function.
Traditionally,
\mathtt{map}
takes a function
f
and a list and applies
f
to all elements of the list element wise.
Using measurings, we can actually specify a list of functions which gets applied to elements of a list in a pairwise fashion.
For each
m\in M
, define the function
r_{m}\colon M\to M
which multiplies an element of
M
by
m
from the right.
We define the algebra

\displaystyle 1+M\times[M,M]^{\leq\infty}

\displaystyle\longrightarrow[M,M]^{\leq\infty}

\displaystyle*

\displaystyle\longmapsto r_{e}

\displaystyle(m,\ell)

\displaystyle\longmapsto r_{m}:\ell.

We can now define the measuring

\displaystyle\varphi\colon{M^{*}}^{\circ}\times[M,M]^{\leq\infty}

\displaystyle\to M^{*}

\displaystyle([\>],\ell)

\displaystyle\mapsto[\>]

\displaystyle(\ell,[\>])

\displaystyle\mapsto[\>]

\displaystyle(m:\ell,f:k)

\displaystyle\mapsto f(m):\varphi(\ell,k).

We verify this is a measuring by checking

\varphi(m^{\prime}:\ell,r_{m}:k)=r_{m}(m^{\prime}):\varphi(\ell,k)=(m^{\prime}\bullet m):\varphi(\ell,k).

Note that we really need to define an algebra structure on
[M,M]^{\leq\infty}
instead of a coalgebra structure, since there is no obvious way to extract
m\in M
from a function
f\colon M\to M
in such a way that the conditions on a measuring are satisfied.

5.5.4
Free and Cofree Functors

The free functor is defined by mapping
X\in\mathrm{Set}
to

\displaystyle\alpha_{\operatorname{Fr}}\colon 1+M\times M^{*}\times(X+\{e_{X}\})

\displaystyle\to M^{*}\times(X+\{e_{X}\})

\displaystyle*

\displaystyle\mapsto([\>],e_{X})

\displaystyle(m,\ell,e_{X})

\displaystyle\mapsto(m:\ell,e_{X})

\displaystyle(m,\ell,x)

\displaystyle\mapsto(m:\ell,x).

The behavior of
\operatorname{Fr}
on functions
f\colon X\to Y
is forced and given by

\displaystyle\operatorname{Fr}(f)\colon M^{*}\times(X+\{e_{X}\})

\displaystyle\to M^{*}\times(Y+\{e_{Y}\})

\displaystyle(\ell,x)

\displaystyle\mapsto(\ell,f(x))

\displaystyle(\ell,e_{X})

\displaystyle\mapsto(\ell,e_{Y}).

Checking this is a left adjoint to the forgetful functor can be done by constructing the natural bijection

[X,A]\cong\mathrm{Alg}(\operatorname{Fr}(X),A).

The bijection is given by

[X,A]\to\mathrm{Alg}(\operatorname{Fr}(X),A),f\mapsto\widetilde{f},

where

\displaystyle\widetilde{f}\colon M^{*}\times(X+\{e_{X}\})

\displaystyle\to Y

\displaystyle([\>],e_{X})

\displaystyle\mapsto e_{Y}

\displaystyle([\>],x)

\displaystyle\mapsto f(x)

\displaystyle(m:\ell,e_{X})

\displaystyle\mapsto\beta(m,\widetilde{f}(\ell,e_{X}))

\displaystyle(m:\ell,x)

\displaystyle\mapsto\beta(m,\widetilde{f}(\ell,x)).

The inverse of the bijection
[X,A]\to\mathrm{Alg}(\operatorname{Fr}(X),A)
is given by restriction to
X
.
Informally,
\widetilde{f}
takes a list
\ell\in M^{*}
and an element
x\in X
and appends
\ell
to
f(x)
in
Y
.
A quick check will also show this bijection is natural.
The unit and counit are given by

\eta^{\operatorname{Fr}}_{X}\colon X\to M^{*}\times(X+\{e_{X}\}),x\mapsto([\>],x)

for any set
X
,
and

\displaystyle\varepsilon^{\operatorname{Fr}}_{A}\colon M^{*}\times(A+\{e_{A}\})

\displaystyle\to A

\displaystyle([\>],e_{A})

\displaystyle\mapsto\alpha(*)

\displaystyle([\>],a)

\displaystyle\mapsto a

\displaystyle(m:\ell,a)

\displaystyle\mapsto\alpha(m,\varepsilon^{\operatorname{Fr}}_{A}(\ell,a)),

for an algebra
A
.

The cofree functor is defined by mapping
X\in\mathrm{Set}
to

\displaystyle\delta_{\operatorname{Cof}}\colon X\times(M\times X)^{\leq\infty}

\displaystyle\to 1+M\times X\times(M\times X)^{\leq\infty}

\displaystyle(x,[\>])

\displaystyle\mapsto*

\displaystyle(x,(m,y):\ell)

\displaystyle\mapsto(m,y,\ell).

The behavior of
\operatorname{Cof}
on functions
f\colon X\to Y
is forced and given by applying
f
to all elements of
X
in any element of
X\times(M\times X)^{\leq\infty}
.
Checking this is a right adjoint to the forgetful functor can be done by constructing the natural bijection

[C,Y]\cong\mathrm{coAlg}(C,\operatorname{Cof}(Y)),

The bijection is given by

[C,Y]\to\mathrm{coAlg}(C,\operatorname{Cof}(Y)),f\mapsto\widetilde{f},

where

\displaystyle\widetilde{f}=\langle\widetilde{f}_{0},\widetilde{f}_{1}\rangle\colon C

\displaystyle\to Y\times(M\times Y)^{\leq\infty}

\displaystyle c

\displaystyle\mapsto\begin{cases}(f(c),(m,f(c^{\prime})):\widetilde{f}_{1}(c^{\prime}))\text{ if }\chi(c)=(m,c^{\prime})\\
(f(c),[\>])\text{ if }\chi(c)=*.\end{cases}

Notice the function
\widetilde{f}_{0}=f
, and hence this also gives the inverse for the bijection.
Verifying
\widetilde{f}
is an coalgebra morphism is left to the reader.
Informally,
\widetilde{f}
takes a stream-like
c\in C
and applies
f
to all its elements.
The unit and counit are given by

\eta^{\operatorname{Cof}}_{C}\colon C\to C\times(M\times C)^{\leq\infty},c\mapsto(c,[\>])
for any coalgebra
C
,
and

\varepsilon^{\operatorname{Cof}}_{X}\colon X\times(M\times X)^{\leq\infty}\to x,(x,\ell)\mapsto x,
for any set
X
.

5.5.5
Representing Objects

Here, we will give full details when constructing the representing objects.
We will finally see which roles the free and cofree functor play in this construction.

First we wish to construct the universal measuring coalgebra
\underline{\mathrm{Alg}}(A,B)
.
The representing object
\underline{\mathrm{Alg}}(A,B)
is given as the equalizer of the following parallel morphisms

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{[A,B]\times(M\times[A,B])^{\leq\infty}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{[1+M\times A,B]\times(M\times[1+M\times A,B])^{\leq\infty}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{Cof}(\alpha^{*})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Psi}

where
\Psi
is the transpose of

\widetilde{\Psi}\colon[A,B]\times(M\times[A,B])^{\leq\infty}\xrightarrow{\chi_{\operatorname{Cof}}}1+M\times[A,B]\times(M\times[A,B])^{\leq\infty}\xrightarrow{F(\varepsilon^{\operatorname{Cof}})}\\
1+M\times[A,B]\xrightarrow{\widetilde{\nabla}_{A,B}}[1+M\times A,1+M\times B]\xrightarrow{\beta_{*}}[1+M\times A,B].

If we compute the composition we find

\displaystyle\widetilde{\Psi}\colon[A,B]\times(M\times[A,B])^{\leq\infty}

\displaystyle\to[1+M\times A,B]

\displaystyle(f^{\prime},[\>])

\displaystyle\mapsto\operatorname{const}_{e_{B}}

\displaystyle(f^{\prime},(m,f):\ell)

\displaystyle\mapsto\begin{cases}*&\mapsto e_{B}\\
(m^{\prime},a)&\mapsto\beta(m\bullet m^{\prime},f(a)).\end{cases}

We see
\widetilde{\Psi}
only depends on the first element of the list
\ell
, so for brevity we may write
\widetilde{\Psi}((f^{\prime},(m,f):\ell))=\widetilde{\Psi}((m,f))
.
Taking its transpose
\Psi=\operatorname{Cof}(\widetilde{\Psi})\circ\eta
we find the explicit formula for
\Psi
to be

\displaystyle\Psi\colon{[A,B]\times([A,B]\times M)^{\leq\infty}}

\displaystyle\mapsto{[1+M\times A,B]\times([1+M\times A,B]\times M)^{\leq\infty}}

\displaystyle(f^{\prime},[\>])

\displaystyle\mapsto(\operatorname{const}_{e_{B}},[\>])

\displaystyle(f^{\prime},(m,f):\ell)

\displaystyle\mapsto(\widetilde{\Phi}((m,f)),[\>]).

Taking the equalizer, we find the underlying set of
\underline{\mathrm{Alg}}(A,B)
to be a subset of
[A,B]\times(M\times[A,B])^{\infty}
, given by

\displaystyle\{(f_{0},(m_{i},f_{i+1})_{i\in I})\mid f_{i}(e_{A})=e_{B},\,

\displaystyle(f_{i}\circ\alpha)(m,a)=\beta((m_{i}\bullet m),f_{i+1}(a)),

\displaystyle f_{\max}(\alpha(m,a))=e_{B}\text{ for all }a\in A,m\in M\},

where
f_{\max}
is the last function in the stream
(f_{i})_{i\in I}
if the stream is finite.
Unpacking the above, we find
\underline{\mathrm{Alg}}(A,B)
consists of two streams
(m_{i})_{i\in I}\in M^{\leq\infty}
and
(f_{i})_{i\in I+1}\in[A,B]^{\leq\infty}
, with the condition

f_{i}(\alpha(m,a))=\beta((m_{i}\bullet m),f_{i+1}(a)),

and the last element of the stream
(f_{i})
should be the function mapping all elements of the form
\alpha(m,a)
to the element
e_{B}\in B
.
The coalgebra structure of
\underline{\mathrm{Alg}}(A,B)
is given by shifting to the left,

(f_{0},(m_{i},f_{i+1})_{i})\mapsto(m_{0},(f_{1},(m_{i+1},f_{i+2})_{i})),(f_{0},[\>])\mapsto*.

Lastly, we give the evaluation map

\displaystyle\mathrm{ev}\colon\underline{\mathrm{Alg}}(A,B)\times A

\displaystyle\to B

\displaystyle((f_{0},(m_{i},f_{i+1})_{i\in I}),a)

\displaystyle\mapsto f_{0}(a).

The evaluation map is measuring from
A
to
B
by
\underline{\mathrm{Alg}}(A,B)
by definition, making it an element of the category of measurings from
A
to
B
.

To verify
\mu_{C}(A,B)\cong\mathrm{coAlg}(C,\underline{\mathrm{Alg}}(A,B))
, we explicitly construct the bijection.
It is given by the function

\Xi\colon\mu_{C}(A,B)\to\mathrm{coAlg}(C,\underline{\mathrm{Alg}}(A,B))

where given
\varphi\in\mu_{C}(A,B)
:

\displaystyle\Xi(\varphi)\colon C

\displaystyle\to\underline{\mathrm{Alg}}(A,B)

\displaystyle c

\displaystyle\mapsto\left(\varphi_{c},\left(\chi^{i}_{0}(c),\varphi_{\chi^{i}_{1}(c)}\right)_{1\leq i\leq\mathtt{len}(c)}\right),

where we write
\chi=\langle\chi_{0},\chi_{1}\rangle\colon C\to 1+M\times C
and
\lx@glossaries@gls@link{main}{len}{{{}}\mathtt{len}}(c)\in{\mathbb{N}_{\infty}}
is defined as the unique number such that
\chi^{\mathtt{len}(c)}(c)=*
.
First, we verify
\Xi(\varphi)
is well-defined.
This is the case since

\varphi(\chi^{\mathtt{len}(c)}_{1}(c))(\alpha(m,a))=e_{B}

by definition of a measuring and
\mathtt{len}(c)
and

\varphi_{\chi^{i}_{1}(c)}(\alpha(m,a))=\beta(m\bullet\chi^{i}_{0}(c),\varphi_{\chi^{i+1}_{1}(c)}(a))

by definition of a measuring.
Second, we verify
\Xi(\varphi)
is a morphism of coalgebras.
To this end, we note

\Xi(\varphi)(\chi(c))=\left(\varphi_{\chi(c)},\left(\chi^{i+1}_{0}(c),\varphi_{\chi^{i+1}_{1}(c)}\right)_{2\leq i\leq\mathtt{len}(c)}\right),

which agrees with the coalgebra structure on
\underline{\mathrm{Alg}}(A,B)
.

The inverse of the bijection
\Xi\colon\mu_{C}(A,B)\to\mathrm{coAlg}(C,\underline{\mathrm{Alg}}(A,B))
is given by sending a coalgebra homomorphism
\widetilde{\varphi}\colon C\to\underline{\mathrm{Alg}}(A,B)

to the measuring

\Xi^{-1}(\widetilde{\varphi})\colon(c,a)\mapsto(\operatorname{pr}_{0}\circ\widetilde{\varphi}(c))(a).

This is well-defined by the properties on
\underline{\mathrm{Alg}}(A,B)
.
The bijection
\Xi\colon\mu_{C}(A,B)\to\mathrm{coAlg}(C,\underline{\mathrm{Alg}}(A,B))
is natural.
We conclude
\underline{\mathrm{Alg}}(A,B)
represents
\mu_{-}(A,B)
.
By the above the evaluation map is also the terminal object in the category of measurings, since any measuring
\varphi\colon C\times A\to B
factors uniquely through
\mathrm{ev}\colon\underline{\mathrm{Alg}}(A,B)\times A\to B
.

Next we would like to compute
C\triangleright A
.
The measuring tensor
C\triangleright A
is given by the coequalizer of

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{M^{*}\times(C\times(1+M\times A)+\{e_{C\times F(A)}\})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{M^{*}\times(C\times A+\{e_{C\times A}\})},}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Psi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{id}\times(\mathrm{id}\times\alpha+\mathrm{id})}

where
\Psi
is the transpose of

\widetilde{\Psi}\colon C\times 1+M\times A\xrightarrow{\chi\times\mathrm{id}_{F(A)}}1+M\times C\times 1+M\times A\xrightarrow{\nabla_{A,B}}1+M\times(C\times A)\xrightarrow{F(\eta^{\operatorname{Fr}})}\\
1+M\times(M^{*}\times(C\times A+\{e_{C\times A}\}))\xrightarrow{\alpha_{\operatorname{Fr}}}M^{*}\times(C\times A+\{e_{C\times A}\}).

If we compute the composition we find

\displaystyle\widetilde{\Psi}\colon C\times 1+M\times A

\displaystyle\to M^{*}\times(C\times A+\{e_{C\times A}\})

\displaystyle(c,*)

\displaystyle\mapsto\chi(c)\bullet([\>],e_{C\times A})

\displaystyle(c,(m,a))

\displaystyle\mapsto\begin{cases}([\>],e_{C\times A})&\text{ if }\chi(c)=*\\
([m^{\prime}\bullet m],(c^{\prime},a))&\text{ if }\chi(c)=(m^{\prime},c^{\prime}).\end{cases}

Taking its transpose
\Psi=\varepsilon\circ\operatorname{Fr}(\widetilde{\Psi})
we find the explicit formula for
\Psi
to be

\displaystyle\Psi\colon{M^{*}\times(C\times(1+M\times A)+\{e_{C\times F(A)}\})}

\displaystyle\to{M^{*}\times(C\times A+\{e_{C\times A}\})}

\displaystyle(\ell,e_{C\times F(A)})

\displaystyle\mapsto(\ell,e_{C\times A})

\displaystyle(\ell,(c,*))

\displaystyle\mapsto(\ell,e_{C\times A})

and

\displaystyle(\ell,(c,(m,a)))

\displaystyle\mapsto\begin{cases}(\ell,e_{C\times A})&\text{ if }\chi(c)=*\\
(\ell++[m^{\prime}\bullet m],(c^{\prime},a))&\text{ if }\chi(c)=(m^{\prime},c^{\prime}).\end{cases}

Now that we know explicitly which maps we want to coequalize, we can compute

\operatorname{Fr}(C\times A)/\sim\>\>=M^{*}\times((C\times A)+\{e_{C\times A}\})/\sim.

Here the equivalence relation
\sim
is generated by

\displaystyle(\ell,c,e_{A})

\displaystyle\sim(\ell,e_{C\times A})

\displaystyle(\ell,c,\alpha(m,a))

\displaystyle\sim(\ell,e_{C\times A})\text{ if }\chi(c)=*

\displaystyle(\ell,c,\alpha(m,a))

\displaystyle\sim(\ell++[m\bullet m^{\prime}],c^{\prime},a)\text{ if }\chi(c)=(m^{\prime},c^{\prime}).

Its algebra structure is given by

\displaystyle 1+M\times C\triangleright A

\displaystyle\to C\triangleright A

\displaystyle*

\displaystyle\mapsto[[\>],e_{C\times A}]

\displaystyle(m,[\ell,(c,a)])

\displaystyle\mapsto[m:\ell,(c,a)].

Intuitively, given an element
(\ell,c,a)
the equivalence relation transfers the first elements of the stream-like
c
and the list-like
a
to the beginning of the list
\ell
by combining them using the monoid structure on
M
.

To verify
\mu_{C}(A,B)\cong\mathrm{Alg}(C\triangleright A,B)
, we explicitly construct the bijection.
It is given by

\displaystyle\Xi\colon\mu_{C}(A,B)

\displaystyle\to\mathrm{Alg}(C\triangleright A,B)

\displaystyle\varphi

\displaystyle\mapsto\Xi(\varphi),

where
\Xi(\varphi)
is given by

\displaystyle\Xi(\varphi)\colon C\triangleright A

\displaystyle\to B

\displaystyle[([\>],e_{C\times A})]

\displaystyle\mapsto e_{B}

\displaystyle[(m:\ell,e_{C\times A})]

\displaystyle\mapsto\beta(m,\Xi(\varphi)([(\ell,e_{C\times A})]))

\displaystyle[([\>],(c,a))]

\displaystyle\mapsto\varphi(c,a)

\displaystyle[(m:\ell,(c,a))]

\displaystyle\mapsto\beta(m,\Xi(\varphi)([(\ell,(c,a))])).

To check
\Xi(\varphi)
is well-defined, one needs to perform a straightforward check using the fact that
\varphi
is a measuring.
To see
\Xi(\varphi)
is a morphism of algebras, we note this is the case by definition of
C\triangleright A
and the definition of
\Xi(\varphi)
.

The inverse of the bijection
\Xi\colon\mu_{C}(A,B)\to\mathrm{Alg}(C\triangleright A,B)
is given by sending a morphism
\widetilde{\varphi}\colon C\triangleright A\to B

to the measuring

\Xi^{-1}(\widetilde{\varphi})\colon(c,a)\mapsto\widetilde{\varphi}([([\>],(c,a))]),

which is well-defined by the properties on
C\triangleright A
.
The bijection
\Xi\colon\mu_{C}(A,B)\to\mathrm{Alg}(C\triangleright A,B)
is natural, which can be checked by a straightforward calculation.
We conclude
C\triangleright A
represents
\mu_{C}(A,-)
.

Finally, we define the convolution algebra
[C,B]
to have algebra structure given by

\displaystyle 1+M\times[C,B]

\displaystyle\to[C,B]

\displaystyle*

\displaystyle\mapsto(c\mapsto e_{B})

\displaystyle(m,f)

\displaystyle\mapsto\left(c\mapsto\begin{cases}\beta(m\bullet m^{\prime},f(c^{\prime}))\text{ if }\chi(c)=(m^{\prime},c^{\prime})\\
e_{A}\text{ if }\chi(c)=*\end{cases}\right).

Example 50

We would like to calculate
\underline{\mathrm{Alg}}(M^{\leq n},B)
for arbitrary
B\in\mathrm{Alg}
.
Our aim is to leverage the isomorphism

\mathrm{coAlg}(C,\underline{\mathrm{Alg}}(M^{\leq n},B))\cong\mu_{C}(M^{\leq n},B)\cong\mathrm{Alg}(M^{\leq n},[C,B]).

To do so, we make an observation about
\mathrm{Alg}(M^{\leq n},Z)
for an arbitrary algebra
(Z,\zeta)\in\mathrm{Alg}
.
Since
M^{\leq n}
is preinitial, any morphism out of
M^{\leq n}
is unique.
So,
\mathrm{Alg}(M^{\leq n},Z)
has at most one element.
The question now becomes if there is a condition on
Z
for
\mathrm{Alg}(M^{\leq n},Z)
to be inhabited.
We claim this condition is

\zeta(m,\mathord{\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\mathinvertedexclamationmark$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\mathinvertedexclamationmark$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\mathinvertedexclamationmark$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathinvertedexclamationmark$}}}_{Z}(\ell))=\zeta(m,\mathord{\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\mathinvertedexclamationmark$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\mathinvertedexclamationmark$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\mathinvertedexclamationmark$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathinvertedexclamationmark$}}}_{Z}(\mathtt{take}(n-1)(\ell)))\text{ for all }(m,\ell)\in M\times M^{\leq n}.

(5.5.1)

Notice that
\mathtt{take}(n-1)(\ell)=\ell
for all lists of length less than
n
, so the only non-trivial case is when
\mathtt{len}(\ell)=n
.
If
Eq.

5.5.1
is satisfied, we claim the unique algebra morphism
M^{\leq n}\to Z
is given by

\mathord{\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\mathinvertedexclamationmark$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\mathinvertedexclamationmark$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\mathinvertedexclamationmark$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathinvertedexclamationmark$}}}_{Z}\circ\iota\colon M^{\leq n}\hookrightarrow M^{*}\to Z,

where
\iota\in[M^{\leq n},M^{*}]
is an inclusion of sets, not an algebra morphism.
For brevity, we will write
\mathord{\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\mathinvertedexclamationmark$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\mathinvertedexclamationmark$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\mathinvertedexclamationmark$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathinvertedexclamationmark$}}}_{Z}\circ\iota=\mathord{\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\mathinvertedexclamationmark$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\mathinvertedexclamationmark$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\mathinvertedexclamationmark$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathinvertedexclamationmark$}}}_{Z}
if the domain is understood.
To verify this is an algebra morphism, we must check it commutes with the algebra structures.
If we denote the algebra structure on
M^{\leq n}
by
\alpha_{n}
, we need to check

\mathord{\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\mathinvertedexclamationmark$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\mathinvertedexclamationmark$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\mathinvertedexclamationmark$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathinvertedexclamationmark$}}}_{Z}(\alpha_{n}(m,\ell))=\zeta(m,\mathord{\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\mathinvertedexclamationmark$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\mathinvertedexclamationmark$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\mathinvertedexclamationmark$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathinvertedexclamationmark$}}}_{Z}(\ell)),

since the case of the empty list is trivial.
Using
Eq.

5.5.1
and that
\mathord{\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\mathinvertedexclamationmark$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\mathinvertedexclamationmark$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\mathinvertedexclamationmark$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathinvertedexclamationmark$}}}_{Z}\colon M^{*}\to Z
is an algebra morphism we can make the following deduction:
