\displaystyle(\mathord{\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\mathinvertedexclamationmark$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\mathinvertedexclamationmark$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\mathinvertedexclamationmark$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathinvertedexclamationmark$}}}_{Z}\circ\iota)(\alpha_{n}(m,\ell))

\displaystyle=(\mathord{\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\mathinvertedexclamationmark$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\mathinvertedexclamationmark$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\mathinvertedexclamationmark$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathinvertedexclamationmark$}}}_{Z}\circ\iota)(m:\mathtt{take}(n-1)(\ell))

\displaystyle=\mathord{\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\mathinvertedexclamationmark$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\mathinvertedexclamationmark$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\mathinvertedexclamationmark$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathinvertedexclamationmark$}}}_{Z}(m:\mathtt{take}(n-1)(\ell))

\displaystyle=\zeta(m,\mathord{\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\mathinvertedexclamationmark$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\mathinvertedexclamationmark$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\mathinvertedexclamationmark$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathinvertedexclamationmark$}}}_{Z}(\mathtt{take}(n-1)(\ell)))

\displaystyle=\zeta(m,\mathord{\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\mathinvertedexclamationmark$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\mathinvertedexclamationmark$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\mathinvertedexclamationmark$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathinvertedexclamationmark$}}}_{Z}(\ell))

\displaystyle=\zeta(m,(\mathord{\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\mathinvertedexclamationmark$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\mathinvertedexclamationmark$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\mathinvertedexclamationmark$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathinvertedexclamationmark$}}}_{Z}\circ\iota)(\ell)).

Conversely, if there exists
\ell\in M^{\leq n}
such that
\zeta(m,\mathord{\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\mathinvertedexclamationmark$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\mathinvertedexclamationmark$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\mathinvertedexclamationmark$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathinvertedexclamationmark$}}}_{Z}(\ell))\neq\zeta(m,\mathord{\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\mathinvertedexclamationmark$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\mathinvertedexclamationmark$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\mathinvertedexclamationmark$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathinvertedexclamationmark$}}}_{Z}(\mathtt{take}(n-1)(\ell)))
it is impossible to construct an algebra morphism
f\colon M^{\leq n}\to Z
.
This can be seen by trying to construct such a morphism
f
and observing
f(\alpha_{n}(m,\ell))\neq\zeta(m,f(\ell))
in this case.

Now that we have an indication on whether
\mathrm{Alg}(M^{\leq n},Z)
is inhabited for arbitrary
Z\in\mathrm{Alg}
, we can focus in
Z=[C,B]
.
Observing the algebra structure on
[C,B]
, we see the morphism
\mathord{\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\mathinvertedexclamationmark$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\mathinvertedexclamationmark$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\mathinvertedexclamationmark$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathinvertedexclamationmark$}}}_{[C,B]}
is given by

\displaystyle\mathord{\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\mathinvertedexclamationmark$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\mathinvertedexclamationmark$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\mathinvertedexclamationmark$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathinvertedexclamationmark$}}}_{[C,B]}\colon M^{*}

\displaystyle\to[C,B]

\displaystyle[\>]

\displaystyle\mapsto(c\mapsto e_{B})

\displaystyle m:\ell

\displaystyle\mapsto\left(c\mapsto\begin{cases}\beta(m\bullet m^{\prime},\mathord{\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\mathinvertedexclamationmark$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\mathinvertedexclamationmark$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\mathinvertedexclamationmark$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathinvertedexclamationmark$}}}_{[C,B]}(\ell)(c^{\prime}))\text{ if }\chi(c)=(m^{\prime},c^{\prime})\\
e_{B}\text{ if }\chi(c)=*.\end{cases}\right).

Denoting the algebra structure on
[C,B]
by
\alpha
, we would like to know under which condition on
C
and
B

\alpha(m,\mathord{\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\mathinvertedexclamationmark$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\mathinvertedexclamationmark$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\mathinvertedexclamationmark$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathinvertedexclamationmark$}}}_{[C,B]}(\ell))=\alpha(m,\mathord{\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\mathinvertedexclamationmark$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\mathinvertedexclamationmark$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\mathinvertedexclamationmark$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathinvertedexclamationmark$}}}_{[C,B]}(\mathtt{take}(n-1)(\ell)))
.
Unpacking the above, we find this is the case if

\beta(m\bullet m^{\prime},\mathord{\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\mathinvertedexclamationmark$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\mathinvertedexclamationmark$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\mathinvertedexclamationmark$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathinvertedexclamationmark$}}}_{[C,B]}(\ell)(c^{\prime}))=\beta(m\bullet m^{\prime},\mathord{\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\mathinvertedexclamationmark$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\mathinvertedexclamationmark$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\mathinvertedexclamationmark$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathinvertedexclamationmark$}}}_{[C,B]}(\mathtt{take}(n-1)(\ell))(c^{\prime}))

for all
(m^{\prime},c^{\prime})\in\operatorname{im}(\chi)
.
By definition of
\mathord{\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\mathinvertedexclamationmark$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\mathinvertedexclamationmark$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\mathinvertedexclamationmark$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathinvertedexclamationmark$}}}_{[C,B]}
, the above condition can be satisfied if
\mathtt{len}(c)\leq n
for all
c\in C
or if condition
Eq.

5.5.1
holds for
B
.
So, we have the following:

\mathrm{Alg}(M^{\leq n},[C,B])\cong\begin{cases}\{*\}\text{ if }\beta(m,\mathord{\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\mathinvertedexclamationmark$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\mathinvertedexclamationmark$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\mathinvertedexclamationmark$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathinvertedexclamationmark$}}}_{B}(\ell))=\beta(m,\mathord{\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\mathinvertedexclamationmark$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\mathinvertedexclamationmark$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\mathinvertedexclamationmark$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathinvertedexclamationmark$}}}_{B}(\mathtt{take}(n-1)(\ell)))\,\\
\hskip 90.00014pt\forall(m,\ell)\in M\times M^{\leq n}\\
\{*\}\text{ if }\mathtt{len}(c)\leq n\text{ for all }c\in C\\
\emptyset\text{ otherwise}.\end{cases}

Now we can make use the fact that
\mathrm{coAlg}(C,\underline{\mathrm{Alg}}(M^{\leq n},B))\cong\mathrm{Alg}(M^{\leq n},[C,B])
.
We observe that in the case of
\beta(m,\mathord{\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\mathinvertedexclamationmark$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\mathinvertedexclamationmark$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\mathinvertedexclamationmark$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathinvertedexclamationmark$}}}_{B}(\ell))=\beta(m,\mathord{\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\mathinvertedexclamationmark$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\mathinvertedexclamationmark$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\mathinvertedexclamationmark$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathinvertedexclamationmark$}}}_{B}(\mathtt{take}(n-1)(\ell)))
, the object
\underline{\mathrm{Alg}}(M^{\leq n},B)
has the universal property of the terminal coalgebra
M^{\leq\infty}
.
Whenever this is not the case,
\underline{\mathrm{Alg}}(M^{\leq n},B)
has the universal property of
M^{\leq n}\in\mathrm{coAlg}
.
We conclude

\underline{\mathrm{Alg}}(M^{\leq n},B)=\begin{cases}M^{\leq\infty}\text{ if }\beta(m,\mathord{\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\mathinvertedexclamationmark$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\mathinvertedexclamationmark$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\mathinvertedexclamationmark$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathinvertedexclamationmark$}}}_{B}(\ell))=\beta(m,\mathord{\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\mathinvertedexclamationmark$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\mathinvertedexclamationmark$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\mathinvertedexclamationmark$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathinvertedexclamationmark$}}}_{B}(\mathtt{take}(n-1)(\ell))),\\
\hskip 100.00015pt\forall(m,\ell)\in M\times M^{\leq n}\\
M^{\leq n}\text{ otherwise}.\end{cases}

5.5.6
C
-Initial Algebras

We have already seen that
\mathbb{n}
is the terminal
\mathbb{n}^{\circ}
-initial algebra.
An educated guess is that
M^{\leq n}
is the terminal
({M^{\leq n}})^{\circ}
-initial algebra as well.
and this is indeed the case. Moreover, for any
k\geq n
,
M^{\leq k}
is a
{M^{\leq n}}^{\circ}
-initial algebra, just as in the case of natural numbers.
We have already seen a proof for the natural numbers, which in this context corresponds to consider the trivial monoid
M\cong 1
.
One could adapt that proof to hold for any monoid
M
, but we will not do that here. In
Sect.

5.7.5
, we will prove this for a more complicated type: general trees.

One could also wonder about the preinitial algebra
(M^{\prime})^{*}
.
An educated guess might be that
(M^{\prime})^{*}
is a
{(M^{\prime})^{*}}^{\circ}
-initial algebra.
Alas, we have already seen at the start of this section that there does not exist a measuring

\varphi\colon{(M^{\prime})^{*}}^{\circ}\times(M^{\prime})^{*}\to(M^{\prime})^{*},

hence
(M^{\prime})^{*}
cannot be an
{(M^{\prime})^{*}}^{\circ}
-initial algebra.

But what then can we say about coalgebras stemming from a subset
M^{\prime}\subseteq M
, such as
{(M^{\prime})^{*}}^{\circ}
and
{((M^{\prime})^{\leq n})}^{\circ}
?
As it turns out, they inherit their initial algebras from their counterparts stemming from the entire monoid
M
.
This is due to the fact that in this case, the monomorphisms
\iota_{n}\colon{(M^{\prime})_{n}^{*}}^{\circ}\rightarrowtail{(M^{\leq n})}^{\circ}\in\mathrm{coAlg}
induce monomorphisms

\displaystyle\iota_{n}\triangleright\mathrm{id}_{A}\colon{(M^{\prime})_{n}^{*}}^{\circ}\triangleright A

\displaystyle\to{M_{n}^{*}}^{\circ}\triangleright A

\displaystyle[\ell,\ell^{\prime},a]

\displaystyle\mapsto[\ell,\ell^{\prime},a].

An algebra
A
is
{M_{n}^{*}}^{\circ}
-initial if and only if
{M_{n}^{*}}^{\circ}\triangleright A\cong I
, where
I\cong M^{*}
is the initial algebra.
A monomorphism into an initial object is an isomorphism, so using
\iota_{n}\triangleright\mathrm{id}_{A}
we see
{(M^{\prime})_{n}^{*}}^{\circ}\triangleright A\cong I
.
This implies any
{M_{n}^{*}}^{\circ}
-initial algebra
A
is also
{(M^{\prime})_{n}^{*}}^{\circ}
-initial.

5.6
The Binary Tree Type

Having seen the theory applied to the case of lists labeled in a monoid, we can continue to explore types frequently used in computer science.
One of them is the binary tree type. This type adds to the complexity by allowing a branching structure.
The branching structure is very predictable however, which makes it a nice stepping stone to the last section where we allow all branching structures.
The type of binary trees with nodes labeled in a commutative monoid
(M,\bullet,e)
is the
initial algebra of the functor
F
given by

\displaystyle F\colon\mathrm{Set}

\displaystyle\longrightarrow\mathrm{Set}

\displaystyle X

\displaystyle\longmapsto 1+M\times X\times X.

The functor
F
is lax monoidal by

\displaystyle\eta\colon

\displaystyle 1\to 1+M

\displaystyle*\mapsto e

and

\displaystyle\nabla_{A,B}\colon(1+M\times X\times X)\times(1+M\times Y\times Y)

\displaystyle\to 1+M\times X\times Y\times X\times Y

\displaystyle(*,x)

\displaystyle\mapsto*

\displaystyle(x,*)

\displaystyle\mapsto*

\displaystyle((m,x,x^{\prime}),(m^{\prime},y,y^{\prime}))

\displaystyle\mapsto(m\bullet m^{\prime},x,y,x^{\prime},y^{\prime}).

The category of
F
-algebras has elements

\alpha\colon 1+M\times A\times A\to A

denoted
(A,\alpha)
.
Morphisms
f\colon(A,\alpha)\to(B,\beta)
are given by functions
f\colon A\to B

which make the following diagram commute:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 1+M\times A\times A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 1+M\times B\times B}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B.}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F(f)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\beta}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}

The category of
F
-coalgebras has elements

\chi\colon C\to 1+M\times C\times C,

denoted
(C,\chi)
.
Morphisms
f\colon(C,\chi)\to(D,\delta)
are given by functions
f\colon C\to D

which make the following diagram commute:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 1+M\times C\times C}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 1+M\times D\times D}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces D.}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F(f)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\chi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\delta}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}

5.6.1
Initial and Terminal Objects

The initial algebra is given by the collection of all finite binary trees with nodes labeled in the monoid
M
.
In order to define the underlying set, we define the sets
T

M

, which are the binary trees with values in
M
of depth at most
n
.
Let
T_{M,0}=\{e_{T}\}\cong 1
, then we can write

T_{M,n}=M\times T_{M,n-1}\times T_{M,n-1}+T_{M,0}.

Note that we have a filtration
T_{M,0}\subseteq T_{M,1}\subseteq\dots\subseteq T_{M,n-1}\subset T_{M,n}\subseteq T_{M,n+1}\subseteq\cdots
.
The underlying set of the initial algebra is given by

T_{M}\coloneqq\bigcup_{n\in\mathbb{N}}T_{M,n}.

Its algebra structure is given by

\displaystyle 1+M\times T_{M}\times T_{M}

\displaystyle\longrightarrow T_{M}

\displaystyle*

\displaystyle\longmapsto e_{T}

\displaystyle(m,\ell,r)

\displaystyle\longmapsto(m,\ell,r).

Given an element
(m,\ell,r)\in T_{M}
, we will call
m
the value stored in the node,
\ell
the left child and
r
the right child.
Some examples of preinitial algebras include
T_{M,n}
for all
n\in\mathbb{N}
,
T_{M^{\prime}}
and
T_{M^{\prime},n}
, where
M^{\prime}\subseteq M
.
A slightly less obvious example is
M^{*}
, with algebra structure

\displaystyle\alpha_{\ell}\colon 1+M\times M^{*}\times M^{*}

\displaystyle\longrightarrow M^{*}

\displaystyle*

\displaystyle\longmapsto[\>]

\displaystyle(m,\ell,r)

\displaystyle\mapsto m:\ell.

The unique algebra morphism
T_{M}\to(M^{*},\alpha_{\ell})
returns the left most branch of the tree as a list.
Altering the algebra structure on
M^{*}
does give different results.
Consider the algebra structure

\displaystyle\alpha_{\max}\colon 1+M\times M^{*}\times M^{*}

\displaystyle\to M^{*}

\displaystyle*

\displaystyle\mapsto[\>]

\displaystyle(m,\ell,r)

\displaystyle\mapsto\begin{cases}m:\ell&\text{ if }\mathtt{len}(\ell)\geq\mathtt{len}(r)\\
m:r&\text{ otherwise}.\end{cases}

In this case, the unique algebra morphism
T\to(M^{*},\alpha_{\max})
gives the (leftmost) longest branch.
Lastly, we remark there exists a morphism which can capture the shape of the binary tree while forgetting its contents.
It is defined as the unique algebra morphism

\mathtt{shape}\colon T_{M}\to T_{M,1}.

In order to define the terminal coalgebra we need to define consider the set
\widetilde{T}_{M,\infty}
of trees of infinite depth, labeled in
M
.
The terminal coalgebra has as underlying set
\lx@glossaries@gls@link{main}{TreeInf}{{{}}{T}_{M,\infty}}=T_{M}+\widetilde{T}_{M,\infty}
with coalgebra structure given by

\displaystyle T_{M,\infty}

\displaystyle\to 1+M\times T_{M,\infty}\times T_{M,\infty}

\displaystyle e_{T}

\displaystyle\mapsto*

\displaystyle(m,\ell,r)

\displaystyle\mapsto(m,\ell,r).

Subterminal coalgebras are given by subsets of
T_{M,\infty}
which respect the algebra structure.
Again, we will use
(-)^{\circ}
to distinguish algebras and coalgebras which have the same underlying set.
Some examples are
T_{M,n}^{\circ},T_{M}^{\circ},\widetilde{T}_{M,\infty}
, but we also have the example of

\displaystyle M^{\leq\infty}

\displaystyle\to 1+M\times M^{\leq\infty}\times M^{\leq\infty}

\displaystyle[\>]

\displaystyle\mapsto*

\displaystyle m:\ell

\displaystyle\mapsto(m,\ell,[\>]).

Again, changing the coalgebra structure on
M^{\leq\infty}
will give a plethora of different coalgebras, each with their own interpretation.

5.6.2
Measurings

Given algebras
A,B\in\mathrm{Alg}
and a coalgebra
C\in\mathrm{coAlg}
, a measuring is a function
\varphi\colon C\times A\to B
satisfying

1.

\varphi_{c}(e_{A})=e_{B}
for all
c\in C

2.

\varphi_{c}(\alpha(m,a_{\ell},a_{r}))=e_{B}
if
\chi(c)=*

3.

\varphi_{c}(\alpha(m,a_{\ell},a_{r}))=\beta(m^{\prime}\bullet m,\varphi_{c_{\ell}}(a_{\ell}),\varphi_{c_{r}}(a_{r}))

if
\chi(c)=(m^{\prime},c_{\ell},c_{r})
.

Comparing this with the definition of measuring found in
Sect.

5.5
, we see they are very similar.
The only difference is that in this case, we are accommodating the branching structure of binary trees.

Example 51

For similar reasons as in
Example

47
, we remark there does not exist an algebra morphism
T_{M,n}\to T_{M}
.
There does however exist a measuring from
T_{M,n}
to
T_{M}
by
T_{M,n}^{\circ}
.
It is given by

\displaystyle\varphi\colon T_{M,n}^{\circ}\times T_{M,n}

\displaystyle\to T_{M}

\displaystyle(e_{T},(m,\ell,r))

\displaystyle\mapsto e_{T}

\displaystyle((m^{\prime},\ell^{\prime},r^{\prime}),e_{T})

\displaystyle\mapsto e_{T}

\displaystyle((m^{\prime},\ell^{\prime},r^{\prime}),(m,\ell,r))

\displaystyle\mapsto y(m^{\prime}\bullet m,\varphi(\ell^{\prime},\ell),\varphi(r^{\prime},r)),

which is a measuring by definition.
Again, for reasons similar to those seen in
Example

47
a measuring to
T_{M}
does exist, where an algebra morphism does not.
The coalgebra
T_{M,n}^{\circ}
gives us control over which parts of an element in
T_{M,n}
we consider.

Example 52

If we only care about modifying the shape of an element in
T_{M}
and don’t want to introduce any twist, we can consider the measuring

\displaystyle\varphi\colon T_{\{e\}}^{\circ}\times T_{M}

\displaystyle\to T_{M}

\displaystyle(e_{T},(m,\ell,r))

\displaystyle\mapsto e_{T}

\displaystyle((e,\ell^{\prime},r^{\prime}),e_{T})

\displaystyle\mapsto e_{T}

\displaystyle((e,\ell^{\prime},r^{\prime}),(m,\ell,r))

\displaystyle\mapsto y(m,\varphi(\ell^{\prime},\ell),\varphi(r^{\prime},r)),

which is a measuring by definition.
This measuring takes a tree containing only the unit of the monoid
t^{\prime}\in T_{\{e\}}^{\circ}
and uses its shape to modify a tree
t\in T_{M}
to fit within the shape of
t^{\prime}
.

Example 53

Again, we can generalize the map function using a measuring to obtain more fine grained control over which function gets applied to which elements.
Recall we define the function
r_{m}\colon M\to M
as multiplication by
m
on the right.
Let
[M,M]
be the set of functions
M\to M
, and let
T_{[M,M]}
be the set of all binary trees with labels in
[M,M]
.
We can then define an algebra structure on
T_{[M,M]}
by

\displaystyle 1+M\times T_{[M,M]}\times T_{[M,M]}

\displaystyle\to T_{[M,M]}

\displaystyle*

\displaystyle\mapsto e_{T}

\displaystyle(m,\ell,r)

\displaystyle\mapsto(r_{m},\ell,r).

Now we have a measuring given by

\displaystyle\varphi\colon T_{M}^{\circ}\times T_{[M,M]}

\displaystyle\to T_{M}

\displaystyle(e_{T},(f,\ell,r))

\displaystyle\mapsto e_{T}

\displaystyle((m^{\prime},\ell^{\prime},r^{\prime}),e_{T})

\displaystyle\mapsto e_{T}

\displaystyle((m^{\prime},\ell^{\prime},r^{\prime}),(f,\ell,r))

\displaystyle\mapsto(f(m),\varphi(\ell^{\prime},\ell),\varphi(r^{\prime},r)).

To check this is a measuring, we verify

\varphi((m^{\prime},\ell^{\prime},r^{\prime}),(r_{m},\ell,r))=(r_{m}(m^{\prime}),\varphi(\ell^{\prime},\ell),\varphi(r^{\prime},r))=(m^{\prime}\bullet m,\varphi(\ell^{\prime},\ell),\varphi(r^{\prime},r)).

5.6.3
Free and Cofree Functors

Given
X\in\mathrm{Set}
, we use the following description of the free algebra on
X
. Its underlying set is the set of all finite trees where nodes are labeled by
M
and leaves are labeled by
X+\{e_{\operatorname{Fr}}\}
. It has the algebra structure

\displaystyle 1+M\times\operatorname{Fr}(X)\times\operatorname{Fr}(X)

\displaystyle\to\operatorname{Fr}(X)

\displaystyle*

\displaystyle\mapsto e_{\operatorname{Fr}}

\displaystyle(m,\ell,r)

\displaystyle\mapsto(m,\ell,r).

One can think of
\operatorname{Fr}(X)
as the set of all finite trees with elements in
M
, where we allow the leaves to contain elements of
X
.
The behavior of
\operatorname{Fr}
on functions
f\colon X\to Y
is forced and given by applying
f
to all elements of
X
in any element of
\operatorname{Fr}(X)
.
Checking this is a left adjoint to the forgetful functor can be done by constructing the natural bijection

[X,A]\cong\mathrm{Alg}(\operatorname{Fr}(X),A).

It is given by sending a function
f\colon X\to A
to the morphism

\displaystyle\widetilde{f}\colon\operatorname{Fr}(X)

\displaystyle\to A

\displaystyle(m,\ell,r)

\displaystyle\mapsto\beta(m,\widetilde{f}(\ell),\widetilde{f}(r))

\displaystyle x

\displaystyle\mapsto f(x)

\displaystyle e_{\operatorname{Fr}}

\displaystyle\mapsto\beta(*).

and sending a morphisms
\widetilde{f}\colon\operatorname{Fr}(X)\to A
to its restriction to
X\subseteq\operatorname{Fr}(X)
.
The morphisms
\widetilde{f}
then sends the nodes to their embedding into
A
, and then appends the leaves with the trees
f(x)\in A
.

The cofree functor is defined by mapping
X\in\mathrm{Set}
to

\displaystyle\delta\colon X\times T_{M\times X\times X,\infty}

\displaystyle\to 1+M\times X\times T_{M\times X\times X,\infty}\times X\times T_{M\times X\times X,\infty}

\displaystyle(x_{0},e_{T})

\displaystyle\mapsto*

\displaystyle(x_{0},(m,x_{\ell},x_{r},t_{\ell},t_{r}))

\displaystyle\mapsto(m,x_{\ell},t_{\ell},x_{r},t_{r}).

The behavior of
\operatorname{Cof}
on functions
f\colon X\to Y
is forced and given by applying
f
to all elements of
X
in any element of
Y\times T_{M\times Y\times Y,\infty}
.
Checking this is a right adjoint to the forgetful functor can be done by constructing the natural bijection

[C,X]\cong\mathrm{coAlg}(C,\operatorname{Cof}(X)).

It is given by sending a function
f\colon C\to X
to the morphism

\displaystyle\widetilde{f}\colon C

\displaystyle\to X\times T_{M\times X\times X,\infty}

\displaystyle c

\displaystyle\mapsto\begin{cases}(f(c),e_{T})\text{ if }\chi(c)=*\\
(f(c),(m,f(c_{\ell}),f(c_{r})),\widetilde{f}(c_{\ell}),\widetilde{f}(c_{r}))\text{ if }\chi(c)=(m,c_{\ell},c_{r}).\end{cases}

Conversely, a morphism
\widetilde{f}\colon C\to\operatorname{Cof}(X)
is sent to its composition with the projection on the first coordinate.

5.6.4
Representing Objects

Here, we we will be very brief about the representing objects of
\mu
.
Constructing them and verifying they do indeed represent
\mu
is completely analogous to the constructions and verification done in
Sect.

5.5

The representing object
\underline{\mathrm{Alg}}(A,B)
is a subset

\underline{\mathrm{Alg}}(A,B)\subseteq T_{M\times[A,B],\infty}.

We can view elements of
T_{M\times[A,B],\infty}
as two trees with the same shape, one containing elements in
M
and the other functions
A\to B
.
We will write
(t_{m},t_{f})\in T_{M\times[A,B],\infty}
for these two trees.
An element
(t_{m},t_{f})\in T_{M\times[A,B]}^{\infty}
is included in
\underline{\mathrm{Alg}}(A,B)
if and only if

f_{i}(e_{A})=e_{B}
for all
f_{i}\in t_{f}
and

f_{i}(\alpha(m,a_{\ell},a_{r}))=\beta(m\bullet m_{i},f_{i_{\ell}}(a_{\ell}),f_{i_{r}}(a_{r}))
,
where
f_{i_{\ell}},f_{i_{r}}
are the children of
f_{i}
if they exist and are otherwise are taken to be the constant function
\operatorname{const}_{e_{B}}
.

The measuring tensor
C\triangleright A
is given by

C\triangleright A=\operatorname{Fr}(C\times A)/\sim

where

\displaystyle(c,e_{A})

\displaystyle\sim e_{\operatorname{Fr}}

\displaystyle(c,\alpha(m,a_{\ell},a_{r}))

\displaystyle\sim e_{\operatorname{Fr}}\text{ if }\chi(c)=*

\displaystyle(c,\alpha(m,a_{\ell},a_{r}))

\displaystyle\sim(m^{\prime}\bullet m,(c_{\ell},a_{\ell}),(c_{r},a_{r}))\text{ if }\chi(c)=(m^{\prime},c_{\ell},c_{r}).

and we also apply the above relation recursively throughout the tree in
\operatorname{Fr}(C\times A)
.
An element of
C\triangleright A
can be viewed as a tree containing elements of
M
in its nodes and having elements of
C\times A
at its leaves.
Given a tuple
(c,a)\in C\times A
at the leaf, we attempt to expand out its tree structure using
\alpha
and
\chi
, and replace it with an empty leaf if one of these two turns out to be empty.

Given an algebra
B
and a coalgebra
C
the convolution algebra
[C,B]
has the algebra structure

\displaystyle 1+M\times[C,B]\times

\displaystyle[C,B]\to[C,B]

\displaystyle*

\displaystyle\mapsto(c\mapsto e_{B})

\displaystyle(m,f_{\ell},f_{r})

\displaystyle\mapsto\left(c\mapsto\begin{cases}e_{B}\text{ if }\chi(c)=*\\
\beta(m^{\prime}\bullet m,f_{\ell}(c_{\ell}),f_{r}(c_{r}))\text{ if }\chi(c)=(m^{\prime},c_{\ell},c_{r})\end{cases}\right).

5.6.5
C
-Initial Algebras

Many of our results from
Sect.

5.5
carry over to this case.
In line with our expectations,
T_{M,n}
is indeed the terminal
T_{M,n}^{\circ}
-initial algebra.
We also have that the algebra
T_{M,k}
is
T_{M,n}^{\circ}
-initial for all
k\geq n
.
Moreover, the algebras
T_{M,k}
are
T_{M^{\prime},n}^{\circ}
-initial for all
k\geq n
and subsets
M^{\prime}\subseteq M
, again similar to
Sect.

5.5
.

5.7
The Type of Trees

For our final example, we explore what happens if we lose control over the amount of branching.
We want to study the type of trees, which contains trees where each node can have any (possibly infinite) number of children.
Again, we will see measurings introduce control over the shape of tree, as well as some element-wise twisting.

Let
F
be the functor given by

\displaystyle F\colon\mathrm{Set}

\displaystyle\longrightarrow\mathrm{Set}

\displaystyle X

\displaystyle\longmapsto 1+M\times X^{\leq\infty}

\displaystyle f

\displaystyle\longmapsto\mathrm{id}_{1}+\mathrm{id}_{M}\times\mathtt{map}(f)

where
(M,\bullet,e)
is a commutative monoid and
map
is a function frequently used in functional programming.
It is defined as

\displaystyle\mathtt{map}\colon[X,Y]\times X^{\leq\infty}

\displaystyle\to Y^{\leq\infty}

\displaystyle(f,[\>])

\displaystyle\mapsto[\>]

\displaystyle(f,x:\ell)

\displaystyle\mapsto f(x):\mathtt{map}(f)(\ell),

hence takes a list
\ell\in X^{\leq\infty}
and a function
f\colon X\to Y
and applies
f
to every element of the list
\ell
, returning a list containing elements of
Y
.
We claim
F
is lax monoidal by

\eta\colon 1\to 1+M\times 1^{\leq\infty}\cong 1+M\times{\mathbb{N}_{\infty}},*\mapsto(e,\infty)

and

\displaystyle\nabla_{A,B}\colon(1+M\times X^{\leq\infty})\times(1+M\times Y^{\leq\infty})

\displaystyle\to 1+M\times(X\times Y)^{\leq\infty}

\displaystyle(*,x)

\displaystyle\mapsto*

\displaystyle(x,*)

\displaystyle\mapsto*

\displaystyle((m,\ell),(m^{\prime},k))

\displaystyle\mapsto(m\bullet m^{\prime},\mathtt{zip}(\ell,k)),

where
zip
is a function borrowed from functional programming and defined as

\displaystyle\mathtt{zip}\colon X^{\leq\infty}\times Y^{\leq\infty}

\displaystyle\to(X\times Y)^{\leq\infty}

\displaystyle([\>],\ell)

\displaystyle\mapsto[\>]

\displaystyle(\ell,[\>])

\displaystyle\mapsto[\>]

\displaystyle(x:\ell,y:k)

\displaystyle\mapsto(x,y):\mathtt{zip}(\ell,k).

The category of
F
-algebras has elements

\alpha\colon 1+M\times A^{\leq\infty}\to A

denoted
(A,\alpha)
.
Morphisms
f\colon(A,\alpha)\to(B,\beta)
are given by functions
f\colon A\to B

which make the following diagram commute:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 1+M\times A^{\leq\infty}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 1+M\times B^{\leq\infty}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B.}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F(f)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\beta}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}

The category of
F
-coalgebras has elements

\chi\colon C\to 1+M\times C^{\leq\infty}
,
denoted
(C,\chi)
.
Morphisms
f\colon(C,\chi)\to(D,\delta)
are given by functions
f\colon C\to D

which make the following diagram commute:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 1+M\times C^{\leq\infty}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 1+M\times D^{\leq\infty}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces D.}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F(f)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\chi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\delta}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}

5.7.1
Initial and Terminal Objects

The initial algebra is given by the collection of all trees labeled in
M
with finite depth.
In order to define the underlying set, we define the sets
S

M
,
n

, which are the trees labeled in
M
of depth at most
n
.
Let
S_{M,0}=\{e_{S}\}\cong 1
, then we can write

S_{M,n}=M\times(S_{M,n-1})^{\leq\infty}\times S_{M,0}.

Note that we have a filtration
S_{M,0}\subseteq S_{M,1}\subseteq\dots\subseteq S_{M,n-1}\subseteq S_{M,n}\subseteq S_{M,n+1}\subseteq\dots
.
The underlying set of the initial algebra is given by

\lx@glossaries@gls@link{main}{STree}{{{}}S_{M}}\coloneqq\bigcup_{n\in\mathbb{N}}S_{M,n}.

Its algebra structure is given by

\displaystyle 1+M\times(S_{M})^{\leq\infty}

\displaystyle\to S_{M}

\displaystyle*

\displaystyle\mapsto e_{S}

\displaystyle(m,\ell)

\displaystyle\mapsto(m,\ell).

For
(m,\ell)\in S_{M}
we will call
m
the value stored at the node and
\ell
the list of children.

Some examples of preinitial algebras include
S_{M,n}
for all
n\in\mathbb{N}
,
S_{M^{\prime}}
and
S_{M^{\prime},n}
, where
M^{\prime}\subseteq M
.
A slightly more subtle example is
M^{*}
, with algebra structure

\displaystyle\alpha\colon 1+M\times(M^{*})^{\leq\infty}

\displaystyle\to M^{*}

\displaystyle*

\displaystyle\mapsto[\>]

\displaystyle(m,[\>])

\displaystyle\mapsto[m]

\displaystyle(m,\ell:k)

\displaystyle\mapsto m:\ell.

The unique algebra morphism
S_{M}\to M^{*}
returns the left most branch of the tree as a list.
Altering the algebra structure on
M^{*}
does give different results.
Consider the algebra structure

\displaystyle\alpha_{\max}\colon 1+M\times(M^{*})^{\leq\infty}

\displaystyle\to M^{*}

\displaystyle*

\displaystyle\mapsto[\>]

\displaystyle(m,[\>])

\displaystyle\mapsto[m]

\displaystyle(m,k)

\displaystyle\mapsto m:\ell\text{ where }\ell=\mathtt{argmax}_{\ell^{\prime}\in k}(\mathtt{len}(\ell^{\prime}))

where
\mathtt{argmax}_{\ell^{\prime}\in k}(\mathtt{len}(\ell^{\prime}))
is the maximum
\mathtt{len}(\ell^{\prime})
over all
\ell^{\prime}\in k
.
In this case, the unique algebra morphism
T\to(M^{*},\alpha_{\max})
gives the longest branch.
Lastly, we remark there exists a morphism which can capture the shape of the tree while forgetting its contents.
It is defined as the unique algebra morphism

\mathtt{shape}\colon S_{M}\to S_{1}.

In order to define the terminal coalgebra, let
\widetilde{S}_{M,\infty}
denote the set which contains trees of infinite depth, labeled in
M
.
The terminal coalgebra has as underlying set
\lx@glossaries@gls@link{main}{STreeInf}{{{}}{S}_{M,\infty}}=S_{M}+\widetilde{S}_{M,\infty}
with coalgebra structure given by

\displaystyle S_{M,\infty}

\displaystyle\to 1+M\times(S_{M,\infty})^{\leq\infty}

\displaystyle e_{S}

\displaystyle\mapsto*

\displaystyle(m,\ell)

\displaystyle\mapsto(m,\ell).

Subterminal coalgebras are given by subsets of
S_{M,\infty}
which respect the algebra structure.
Again, we will use
(-)^{\circ}
to distinguish algebras and coalgebras which have the same underlying set.
Some examples are
{S_{M,n}}^{\circ},S_{M}^{\circ},\widetilde{S}_{M,\infty}
, but we also have the example of

\displaystyle M^{\leq\infty}

\displaystyle\to 1+M\times(M^{\leq\infty})^{\leq\infty}

\displaystyle[\>]

\displaystyle\mapsto*

\displaystyle m:\ell

\displaystyle\mapsto(m,[\ell]).

Again, changing the coalgebra structure on
M^{\leq\infty}
will give a plethora of different coalgebras, each with their own interpretation.

5.7.2
Measurings

Given algebras
(A,\alpha),(B,\beta)\in\mathrm{Alg}
and a coalgebra
(C,\chi)\in\mathrm{coAlg}
, a measuring is a function
\varphi\colon C\times A\to B
satisfying

1.

\varphi_{c}(e_{A})=e_{B}
for all
c\in C

2.

\varphi_{c}(\alpha(m,\ell))=e_{B}
if
\chi(c)=*

3.

\varphi_{c}(\alpha(m,\ell))=\beta(m^{\prime}\bullet m,\mathtt{map}(\varphi)(\mathtt{zip}(cs,\ell)))

if
\chi(c)=(m^{\prime},\ell)
.

It is again very similar to previous definitions of measurings.
The most notable difference is that we are zipping the lists of children
c
and
\alpha(m,\ell)
have.
This means a measuring does not only give us control over the depth of a tree, which would be analogous to lists, but gives us control over the branching structure of the tree as well.

Example 54

For similar reasons as in previous examples, we remark there does not exist an algebra morphism
S_{M,n}\to S_{M}
.
There does however exist a measuring from
S_{M,n}
to
S_{M}
by
S_{M,n}^{\circ}
.
It is given by

\displaystyle\varphi\colon S_{M,n}^{\circ}\times S_{M,n}

\displaystyle\to S_{M}

\displaystyle(e_{S},(m,\ell))

\displaystyle\mapsto e_{S}

\displaystyle((m^{\prime},\ell^{\prime}),e_{T})

\displaystyle\mapsto e_{T}

\displaystyle((m^{\prime},\ell^{\prime}),(m,\ell))

\displaystyle\mapsto(m^{\prime}\bullet m,\,\mathtt{map}(\varphi)(\mathtt{zip}(\ell^{\prime},\ell))),

which is a measuring by definition.
Again, reasoning similar to that seen in Example
47
applies as to why a measuring to
S_{M}
does exist, where an algebra morphism does not.
The coalgebra
S_{M,n}^{\circ}
gives us control over which parts of an element in
S_{M,n}
we consider.

Example 55

Consider the measuring

\displaystyle\varphi\colon S_{\{e\}}^{\circ}\times S_{M}

\displaystyle\to S_{M}

\displaystyle(e_{S},(m,\ell))

\displaystyle\mapsto e_{S}

\displaystyle((e,\ell^{\prime}),e_{T})

\displaystyle\mapsto e_{T}

\displaystyle((e,\ell^{\prime}),(m,\ell))

\displaystyle\mapsto(m,\mathtt{map}(\varphi)(\mathtt{zip}(\ell^{\prime},\ell))),

which is a measuring by definition.
This measuring takes a tree containing only the unit of the monoid
t^{\prime}\in S_{1}^{\circ}
and uses its shape to modify a tree
t\in S_{M}
to fit within the shape of
t^{\prime}
.

Example 56

Again, we can generalize the map function using a measuring to obtain more fine grained control over which function gets applied to which elements.
Recall we define the function
r_{m}\colon M\to M
as multiplication by
m
on the right.
We define an algebra structure on
S_{[M,M]}
by

\displaystyle 1+M\times S_{[M,M]}\times S_{[M,M]}

\displaystyle\to S_{[M,M]}

\displaystyle*

\displaystyle\mapsto e_{T}

\displaystyle(m,\ell,r)

\displaystyle\mapsto(r_{m},\ell,r).

Now we have a measuring given by

\displaystyle\varphi\colon S_{M}^{\circ}\times S_{[M,M]}

\displaystyle\to S_{M}

\displaystyle(e_{S},(f,\ell))

\displaystyle\mapsto e_{S}

\displaystyle((m^{\prime},\ell^{\prime}),e_{S})

\displaystyle\mapsto e_{S}

\displaystyle((m^{\prime},\ell^{\prime}),(f,\ell))

\displaystyle\mapsto(f(m^{\prime}),\mathtt{map}(\varphi)(\mathtt{zip}(\ell^{\prime},\ell))).

To check this is a measuring, we verify

\varphi((m^{\prime},\ell^{\prime}),(r_{m},\ell))=(r_{m}(m^{\prime}),\mathtt{map}(\varphi)(\mathtt{zip}(\ell^{\prime},\ell)))=(m^{\prime}\bullet m,\mathtt{map}(\varphi)(\mathtt{zip}(\ell^{\prime},\ell))).

5.7.3
Free and Cofree Functors

We use the following description of the free algebras.
Given
X\in\mathrm{Set}
, the free algebra
\operatorname{Fr}(X)
has as underlying set the set of all trees of finite depth whose nodes are labeled by
M
and whose leaves are labeled by
X+\{e_{\operatorname{Fr}}\}
. Its algebra structure is given by

\displaystyle 1+M\times\operatorname{Fr}(X)^{\leq\infty}

\displaystyle\to\operatorname{Fr}(X)

\displaystyle*

\displaystyle\mapsto e_{\operatorname{Fr}}

\displaystyle(m,\ell)

\displaystyle\mapsto(m,\ell).

One can think of
\operatorname{Fr}(X)
as the set of all finite-depth trees with elements in
M
, where we allow the leaves to contain elements of
X
.
The behavior of
\operatorname{Fr}
on functions
f\colon X\to Y
is forced and given by applying
f
to all elements of
X
in any element of
\operatorname{Fr}(X)
.
Checking this is a left adjoint to the forgetful functor can be done by constructing the natural bijection

[X,A]\cong\mathrm{Alg}(\operatorname{Fr}(X),A).

It is given by sending a function
f\colon X\to A
to the morphism

\displaystyle\widetilde{f}\colon\operatorname{Fr}(X)

\displaystyle\to B

\displaystyle(m,\ell)

\displaystyle\mapsto\beta(m,\mathtt{map}(\widetilde{f})(\ell))

\displaystyle x

\displaystyle\mapsto f(x)

\displaystyle e_{\operatorname{Fr}}

\displaystyle\mapsto\beta(*).

and sending a morphisms
\widetilde{f}\colon\operatorname{Fr}(X)\to A
to its restriction to
X\subseteq\operatorname{Fr}(X)
.
The morphisms
\widetilde{f}
sends the nodes to their embedding into
A
, and then appends the leaves with the trees
f(x)\in A
.

The cofree functor is defined by mapping
X\in\mathrm{Set}
to

\displaystyle\delta\colon X\times S_{\operatorname{Cof}(X)}

\displaystyle\to 1+M\times(X\times S_{\operatorname{Cof}(X)})^{\leq\infty}

\displaystyle(x_{0},e_{S})

\displaystyle\mapsto*

\displaystyle(x_{0},(m,\ell))

\displaystyle\mapsto(m,\ell)

The behavior of
\operatorname{Cof}
on functions
f\colon X\to Y
is forced and given by applying
f
to all elements of
X
in any part of
\operatorname{Cof}(Y)
.
Checking this is a right adjoint to the forgetful functor can be done by constructing the natural bijection

[C,X]\cong\mathrm{coAlg}(C,\operatorname{Cof}(X)).

It is given by sending a function
f\colon C\to X
to the morphism

\displaystyle\widetilde{f}\colon C

\displaystyle\to X\times S_{\operatorname{Cof}(X)}

\displaystyle c

\displaystyle\mapsto\begin{cases}(f(c),e_{S})\text{ if }\chi(c)=*\\
(f(c),(m,\mathtt{map}(\widetilde{f})(\ell))\text{ if }\chi(c)=(m,\ell).\end{cases}

Conversely, a morphism
\widetilde{f}\colon C\to\operatorname{Cof}(X)
is sent to its composition with the projection on the first coordinate.

5.7.4
Representing Objects

Here, we we will be very brief about the representing objects of
\mu
.
Constructing them and verifying they do indeed represent
\mu
is analogous to the constructions and arguments done in
Sect.

5.5

The universal measuring coalgebra is a subset

\underline{\mathrm{Alg}}(A,B)\subseteq S_{M\times[A,B]}^{\infty}.

We can view elements of
S_{M\times[A,B]}^{\infty}
as two trees with the same shape, one containing elements in
M
and the other functions
A\to B
.
We will write
(t_{m},t_{f})\in S_{M\times[A,B]}^{\infty}
for these two trees.
An element
(t_{m},t_{f})\in\underline{\mathrm{Alg}}(A,B)
if and only if

1.

f_{i}(e_{A})=e_{B}
for all
f_{i}\in t_{f}

2.

f_{i}(\alpha(m,\ell))=\beta(m\bullet m_{i},\mathtt{zipapply}(f,\ell))
,

where here by
\lx@glossaries@gls@link{main}{zipapply}{{{}}\mathtt{zipapply}}(f,\ell)
we mean applying each function
f_{i}
to each element
\ell_{i}
to obtain a new list.

The measuring tensor
C\triangleright A
is given by

C\triangleright A=\operatorname{Fr}(C\times A)/\sim

where

\displaystyle(c,e_{A})

\displaystyle\sim e_{\operatorname{Fr}}

\displaystyle(c,\alpha(m,\ell))

\displaystyle\sim e_{\operatorname{Fr}}\text{ if }\chi(c)=*

\displaystyle(c,\alpha(m,\ell))

\displaystyle\sim(m^{\prime}\bullet m,\mathtt{zip}(k,\ell))\text{ if }\chi(c)=(m^{\prime},k)

and we also apply the above relation recursively throughout the tree in
\operatorname{Fr}(C\times A)
.
The object
C\triangleright A
can be interpreted as being a tree containing elements of
M
in its nodes and having elements of
C\times A
at its leaves.
Given a tuple
(c,a)\in C\times A
at the leaf, we attempt to expand out its tree structure using
\alpha
and
\chi
, and replace it with an empty leaf if one of the two turns out to be empty.

Given an algebra
B
and a coalgebra
C
, the convolution algebra
[C,B]
has algebra structure

\displaystyle 1+M\times[C,B]^{*}

\displaystyle\to[C,B]

\displaystyle*

\displaystyle\mapsto(c\mapsto e_{B})

\displaystyle(m,\ell)

\displaystyle\mapsto\left(c\mapsto\begin{cases}e_{B}\text{ if }\chi(c)=*\\
\beta(m^{\prime}\bullet m,\,\mathtt{zipapply}(\ell,k))\text{ if }\chi(c)=(m^{\prime},k)\end{cases}\right).

5.7.5
C
-Initial Algebras

In this section we would like to give a proof of the fact that
S_{M,n}
is the terminal
S_{M,n}^{\circ}
-initial algebra.
We have already seen a proof of a similar result for the natural numbers, and this proof has the same outline.

We first show there exists a morphism
A\to S_{M,n}
for any
S_{M,n}^{\circ}
-initial algebra
A
using induction.
After that, we will show the morphism is unique.
We start with a lemma which allows us to use induction later on.

Lemma 4

Let
A
be a
S_{M,n}^{\circ}
-initial algebra. Then
A
is also
S_{M,n-1}^{\circ}
initial.

Proof

Consider the coalgebra morphism
\iota\colon S_{M,n-1}^{\circ}\to S_{M,n}^{\circ},s\mapsto s
.
This induces a morphism

\displaystyle\iota\triangleright A\colon S_{M,n-1}^{\circ}\triangleright A

\displaystyle\to S_{M,n}^{\circ}\triangleright A

\displaystyle[e_{\operatorname{Fr}}]

\displaystyle\mapsto[e_{\operatorname{Fr}}]

\displaystyle[s,a]

\displaystyle\mapsto[s,a]

\displaystyle[m,\ell]

\displaystyle\mapsto[m,\mathtt{map}(\iota\triangleright A)(\ell)].

This morphism is monomorphic by definition.
Since
A
is
S_{M,n}^{\circ}
-initial, we know
S_{M,n}\triangleright A
is an initial object.
This means
\iota\triangleright A
is a monomorphism into the initial object, hence an isomorphism.

Corollary 4

Let
A
be a
S_{M,n}^{\circ}
-initial algebra. Then
A
is also
S_{M,k}^{\circ}
initial for all
k\leq n
.

The next lemma is a technical lemma which we will be able to leverage during the induction step.

Lemma 5

Let
A
be a
S_{M,n}^{\circ}
-initial algebra and let
\varphi\colon S_{M,n}^{\circ}\times A\to S_{M}
be the unique measuring from
A
to
S_{M}
by
S_{M,n}^{\circ}
.
For all
0\leq i\leq n
, let
s_{i}\in S_{M,n}
be the largest tree of depth
i
in
S_{M,n}
which contains only the unit
e\in M
.
Then for all
0\leq i\in n
and
0\leq j\leq n-i
we have

\varphi_{s_{i}}(a)=\varphi_{s_{i}}(\mathord{\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\mathinvertedexclamationmark$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\mathinvertedexclamationmark$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\mathinvertedexclamationmark$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathinvertedexclamationmark$}}}_{A}\circ\varphi_{s_{i+j}}(a)).

Proof

Let
k\leq n
.
First, we define a function which takes two trees and appends the second tree to all the roots of the first tree.
We define it as

\displaystyle(++)\colon S_{M,\infty}\times S_{M,\infty}

\displaystyle\to S_{M,\infty}

\displaystyle(e_{S},s)

\displaystyle\mapsto s

\displaystyle((m,\ell),s)

\displaystyle\mapsto(m,\mathtt{map}((++)s)(\ell)).

For all
0\leq j\leq n-k
, define the coalgebra morphism

\displaystyle p_{j}\colon S_{M,k}^{\circ}

\displaystyle\to S_{M,n}^{\circ}\times S_{M,n}^{\circ}

\displaystyle s

\displaystyle\mapsto(s,\widetilde{s}++s_{j}),

where
\widetilde{s}
is the unique tree
\widetilde{s}\in S_{\{e\},k}
such that
\mathtt{shape}(\widetilde{s})=\mathtt{shape}(s)
.
Consider the composition of measurings
\varphi\circ(\mathrm{id}\times\mathord{\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\mathinvertedexclamationmark$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\mathinvertedexclamationmark$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\mathinvertedexclamationmark$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathinvertedexclamationmark$}}}_{A}\circ\varphi)\in\mu_{S_{M,n}^{\circ}\times S_{M,n}^{\circ}}(A,S_{M})
.
We can precompose this measuring with the coalgebra morphism
p_{j}
to obtain a measuring

S_{M,k}^{\circ}\times A\xrightarrow{p_{j}\times\mathrm{id}_{A}}S_{M,n}^{\circ}\times S_{M,n}^{\circ}\times A\xrightarrow{\varphi\circ(\mathrm{id}\times\mathord{\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\mathinvertedexclamationmark$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\mathinvertedexclamationmark$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\mathinvertedexclamationmark$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathinvertedexclamationmark$}}}_{A}\circ\varphi)}S_{M}.

We also have the coalgebra morphism
\iota\colon S_{M,k}^{\circ}\to S_{M,n}^{\circ},s\mapsto s
which we can precompose with
\varphi
.
This gives us a measuring

S_{M,k}^{\circ}\times A\xrightarrow{\iota\times\mathrm{id}_{A}}S_{M,n}^{\circ}\times A\xrightarrow{\varphi}S_{M}.

Since
A
is also
S_{M,k}^{\circ}
-initial, we know these measurings must coincide.
Hence we can state

\varphi(s,a)=\varphi(s,\mathord{\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\mathinvertedexclamationmark$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\mathinvertedexclamationmark$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\mathinvertedexclamationmark$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathinvertedexclamationmark$}}}_{A}\circ\varphi(\widetilde{s}++s_{j},a))

for all
s\in S_{M,k}^{\circ}
and
0\leq j\leq n-k
.
The only restriction placed on
k
was that
k\leq n
.
Iterating over all
0\leq k\leq n
, we arrive at the desired result

\varphi(s,a)=\varphi(s,\mathord{\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\mathinvertedexclamationmark$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\mathinvertedexclamationmark$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\mathinvertedexclamationmark$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathinvertedexclamationmark$}}}_{A}\circ\varphi(\widetilde{s}++s_{j},a))

for all
0\leq i\leq n
and
0\leq j\leq n-i
.
In particular this holds for
s=s_{i}
, and noting
s_{i}++s_{j}=s_{i+j}
we obtain the desired result.

Corollary 5

Let
A
be
S_{M,n}^{\circ}
-initial and let
\varphi
be the unique measuring to
S_{M}
.
For all
0\leq i\leq n
and
a\in A
, we have
\varphi(s_{i},a)\in S_{M,i}
.

Proof

We will proceed by induction over
i
.
For the base case
i=0
, we have
s_{0}=e_{S}
, so
\varphi(s_{0},a)=e_{S}\in S_{M,0}
.
For the induction step, assume
\varphi(s_{i},a)\in S_{M,i}
.
Using
s_{i+1}=(e,(s_{i})^{(\infty)})
and the previous lemma we can write

\varphi(s_{i+1},a)=\varphi((e,(s_{i})^{(\infty)}),\mathord{\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\mathinvertedexclamationmark$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\mathinvertedexclamationmark$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\mathinvertedexclamationmark$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathinvertedexclamationmark$}}}_{A}(\varphi(s_{i+1},a))).

Let
\alpha\colon 1+M\times(S_{M})^{\leq\infty}\to S_{M}
, then we can write
(m,s)=\alpha^{-1}(\varphi(s_{i+1},a))
where
s
is the list with components
s_{i}
.
We then know by definition of a measuring that

\varphi(s_{i+1},a)=\varphi((e,(s_{i})^{(\infty)}),\mathord{\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\mathinvertedexclamationmark$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\mathinvertedexclamationmark$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\mathinvertedexclamationmark$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathinvertedexclamationmark$}}}_{A}(\varphi(s_{i+1},a)))=(e\bullet m,\mathtt{map}(\varphi)(\mathtt{zip}((s_{i})^{(\infty)},k))),

where
k=\mathtt{map}(\mathord{\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\mathinvertedexclamationmark$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\mathinvertedexclamationmark$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\mathinvertedexclamationmark$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathinvertedexclamationmark$}}}_{A})(s)
.
By the induction hypothesis, we know

\mathtt{map}(\varphi)(\mathtt{zip}((s_{i})^{(\infty)},k))\in(S_{M,i})^{\leq\infty},

hence
\varphi(s_{i+1},a)\in S_{M,i+1}
by definition.
This concludes the induction step and the proof.

Now we are ready to define the a family of functions which will culminate in an algebra morphism
A\to S_{M,n}
for any
S_{M,n}^{\circ}
initial algebra
A
.

Definition 23

Let
A
be
S_{M,n}^{\circ}
-initial, let
\varphi
be the unique measuring to
S_{M}
and let
0\leq k\leq n
. Define the functions

\displaystyle\varphi_{k}\colon A

\displaystyle\to S_{M,k}

\displaystyle a

\displaystyle\mapsto\varphi(s_{k},a),

which are well-defined by the previous corollary.

Remark 13

With this new definition, we can restate
Lemma

5
as

\varphi_{i}=\varphi_{i}\circ\mathord{\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\mathinvertedexclamationmark$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\mathinvertedexclamationmark$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\mathinvertedexclamationmark$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathinvertedexclamationmark$}}}_{A}\circ\varphi_{i+j}

for all
0\leq i\leq n
and
0\leq j\leq n-i
.

Proposition 8

The functions
\varphi_{k}\colon A\to S_{M,k}
from
Definition

23
are algebra morphisms for all
0\leq k\leq n
.

Proof

We proceed by induction over
k
.
For
k=0
, we have that
S_{M,k}\cong 1
, the terminal object in
\mathrm{Set}
.
Hence
\varphi_{0}\colon A\to S_{M,0}
is an algebra morphism.
For the inductive step, assume
\varphi_{k-1}\colon A\to S_{M,k-1}
is an algebra morphism.
We wish to show
\varphi_{k}
is an algebra morphism.
In more detail, we aim to show

\alpha_{k}(m,\mathtt{map}(\varphi_{k})(\ell))=\varphi_{k}(\alpha(m,\ell))

for all
(m,\ell)\in M\times A^{\leq\infty}
.
First, we will write
\ell=(a_{i})_{i=0}^{j}
, where
j\in{\mathbb{N}_{\infty}}
.
We can now write

\displaystyle\alpha_{k}(m,\mathtt{map}(\varphi_{k})(\ell))

\displaystyle=\alpha_{k}(m,\mathtt{map}(\varphi_{k})((a_{i})_{i}))

\displaystyle=\alpha_{k}(m,(\varphi_{k}(a_{i}))_{i})

\displaystyle=\alpha_{k}(m,(\varphi_{k}\circ\mathord{\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\mathinvertedexclamationmark$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\mathinvertedexclamationmark$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\mathinvertedexclamationmark$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathinvertedexclamationmark$}}}_{A}\circ\varphi_{k}(a_{i}))_{i}).

Writing
\varphi_{k}\circ\mathord{\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\mathinvertedexclamationmark$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\mathinvertedexclamationmark$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\mathinvertedexclamationmark$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathinvertedexclamationmark$}}}_{A}\circ\varphi_{k}(a_{i})=t_{i}
, we can make the case distinction

t_{i}=\begin{cases}e_{S}&\text{ if }\varphi_{k}(a_{i})=e_{S}\in S_{M}\\
(m_{i},\mathtt{map}(\varphi_{k-1}\circ\mathord{\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\mathinvertedexclamationmark$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\mathinvertedexclamationmark$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\mathinvertedexclamationmark$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathinvertedexclamationmark$}}}_{A})(a_{i}))&\text{ if }\varphi_{k}(a_{i})=(m_{i},a_{i})\in S_{M}\end{cases}

by
\varphi\colon S_{M,n}^{\circ}\times A\to S_{M}
being a measuring.
This means

\alpha_{k}(m,\mathtt{map}(\varphi_{k})(\ell))=\alpha_{k}(m,(t_{i})_{i}).

By the algebra structure of
S_{M,k}
, we can write this as

\alpha_{k}(m,(t_{i})_{i})=(m,(r_{i})_{i}),

where

r_{i}=\begin{cases}e_{S}&\text{ if }\varphi_{k}(a_{i})=e_{S}\in S_{M}\\
\alpha_{k-1}(m_{i},\mathtt{map}(\varphi_{k-1}\circ\mathord{\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\mathinvertedexclamationmark$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\mathinvertedexclamationmark$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\mathinvertedexclamationmark$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathinvertedexclamationmark$}}}_{A})(a_{i}))&\text{ if }\varphi_{k}(a_{i})=(m_{i},a_{i})\in S_{M}.\end{cases}

By the induction hypothesis

\alpha_{k-1}(m_{i},\mathtt{map}(\varphi_{k-1}\circ\mathord{\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\mathinvertedexclamationmark$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\mathinvertedexclamationmark$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\mathinvertedexclamationmark$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathinvertedexclamationmark$}}}_{A})(a_{i}))=(\varphi_{k-1}\circ\mathord{\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\mathinvertedexclamationmark$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\mathinvertedexclamationmark$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\mathinvertedexclamationmark$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathinvertedexclamationmark$}}}_{A})(m_{i},a_{i})

and we can write

r_{i}=(\varphi_{k-1}\circ\mathord{\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\mathinvertedexclamationmark$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\mathinvertedexclamationmark$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\mathinvertedexclamationmark$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathinvertedexclamationmark$}}}_{A})(\varphi_{k}(a_{i}))=(\varphi_{k-1}\circ\mathord{\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\mathinvertedexclamationmark$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\mathinvertedexclamationmark$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\mathinvertedexclamationmark$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathinvertedexclamationmark$}}}_{A}\circ\varphi_{k-1})(a_{i})

using
Lemma

5
.
Retracing our steps, we now compute

\displaystyle\alpha_{k}(m,\mathtt{map}(\varphi_{k})(\ell))

\displaystyle=(m,(r_{i})_{i})

\displaystyle=(m,(\varphi_{k-1}\circ\mathord{\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\mathinvertedexclamationmark$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\mathinvertedexclamationmark$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\mathinvertedexclamationmark$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathinvertedexclamationmark$}}}_{A}\circ\varphi_{k-1})(a_{i})_{i})

\displaystyle=(m,\mathtt{map}(\varphi_{k-1})((\mathord{\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\mathinvertedexclamationmark$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\mathinvertedexclamationmark$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\mathinvertedexclamationmark$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathinvertedexclamationmark$}}}_{A}\circ\varphi_{k-1})(a_{i}))_{i})

\displaystyle=\varphi_{k}(\alpha(m,(\mathord{\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\mathinvertedexclamationmark$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\mathinvertedexclamationmark$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\mathinvertedexclamationmark$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathinvertedexclamationmark$}}}_{A}\circ\varphi_{k-1})(a_{i}))_{i}))

\displaystyle=(\varphi_{k}\circ\mathord{\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\mathinvertedexclamationmark$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\mathinvertedexclamationmark$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\mathinvertedexclamationmark$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathinvertedexclamationmark$}}}_{A}\circ\varphi_{k})(\alpha(m,(a_{i})_{i}))

\displaystyle=\varphi_{k}(\alpha(m,\ell))

using that
\varphi
is a measuring twice.
We conclude
\varphi_{k}\colon A\to S_{M,k}
is an algebra morphism for all
0\leq k\leq n
.

In particular, this lemma shows
\varphi_{n}\colon A\to S_{M,n}
is an algebra morphism.
It still remains to show this algebra morphism is unique.

Lemma 6

For any
S_{M,n}^{\circ}
-initial algebra
A
, there exist at most one algebra morphism
A\to S_{M,n}
.

Proof

By
Proposition

6
, there exists a unique morphism
A\to[S_{M,n}^{\circ},S_{M}]
for any
S_{M,n}^{\circ}
-initial algebra
A
.
Since
S_{M,n}
is
S_{M,n}^{\circ}
-initial we know there exists a unique morphism
\iota\colon S_{M,n}\rightarrowtail[S_{M,n}^{\circ},S_{M}]
.
This map is given by

\displaystyle\iota\colon S_{M,n}

\displaystyle\to[S_{M,n}^{\circ},S_{M}]

\displaystyle e_{S}

\displaystyle\mapsto\operatorname{const}_{e_{S}}

\displaystyle(m,\ell)

\displaystyle\mapsto\left(s^{\prime}\mapsto\begin{cases}e_{S}&\text{ if }s^{\prime}=e_{S}\\
(m^{\prime}\bullet m,\mathtt{map}(\iota)(\mathtt{zip}(\ell,\ell^{\prime})))&\text{ if }s^{\prime}=(m^{\prime},\ell^{\prime}).\end{cases}\right).

We can easily conclude
\iota
is a monomorphism by observing that
\iota(s)(s_{n})=s
for all
s\in S_{M,n}
.
Given any two morphisms
f,g\colon A\to S_{M,n}
, we can draw the following diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{[S_{M,n}^{\circ},S_{M}]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces S_{M,n}.}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces!}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota}

Since the morphism
A\to[S_{M,n}^{\circ},S_{M}]
is unique, we know the composites
\iota\circ f=\iota\circ g
, and by
\iota
being mono we conclude
f=g
.
Hence, there can be at most one algebra morphism from a
S_{M,n}^{\circ}
-initial algebra
A
to
S_{M,n}
.

Putting all the above together, we arrive at the following result.

Theorem 5.2

The algebra
S_{M,n}
is the terminal
S_{M,n}^{\circ}
-initial algebra.

Proof

Given an
S_{M,n}^{\circ}
-initial algebra
A
, by
Proposition

8
, we obtain an algebra morphism
\varphi_{n}\colon A\to S_{M,n}
.
By
Lemma

6
, it is unique.
We conclude
\mathbb{n}
is the terminal
S_{M,n}^{\circ}
-initial algebra.
